# West of Ireland Planting Guide

**Your available materials:** Clay soil · Multipurpose compost · Ericaceous compost · Horticultural bark · Horticultural grit · Small pebbles · Vermiculite · Perlite

---

## DRAINAGE: YOUR MOST IMPORTANT RULE

West of Ireland clay soil is typically heavy, poorly draining, and mildly acidic to neutral (pH ~6.0–7.0). The Atlantic climate brings very high rainfall year-round. **The single most important thing you can do for the majority of these plants is improve drainage before planting.**

**General clay soil recipe for most non-ericaceous plants:**
- Dig each planting hole at least twice the width and depth of the rootball
- Mix the excavated clay 50/50 with multipurpose compost
- Add 20–30% horticultural grit by volume to that mix
- After planting, mulch with horticultural bark (5–7cm), keeping mulch away from stems/crowns
- Test your soil pH before planting — west of Ireland soils vary considerably

---

## HOW TO USE YOUR MATERIALS

| Material | Best Use |
|---|---|
| **Clay soil** | Base for all in-ground plantings |
| **Multipurpose compost** | Soil amendment for most plants (50/50 with clay) |
| **Ericaceous compost** | Blueberry · Heather · Lingonberry · Cranberry · Japanese Maple (partial) · Star Magnolia (partial) · Deer Fern · Hydrangea macrophylla (for blue flowers) · Japanese Skimmia · Dicksonia antarctica (partial) · Japanese Camellia · Prickly Heath |
| **Horticultural bark** | Surface mulch for all in-ground plants (5–7cm), keep away from stems |
| **Horticultural grit** | Drainage improver mixed into clay (20–30%); vital for rosemary, lavender, roses, peonies, pasqueflower, fritillaria, creeping juniper, dianthus, phlox subulata, bearded iris, dwarf iris, alliums, freesia, ixia, sempervivum, saxifrage, campanula saxifraga, edelweiss, auricula, siberian statice |
| **Small pebbles** | Base drainage layer in pots; decorative surface mulch for Mediterranean herbs and rock plants |
| **Vermiculite** | Seed-starting; mix into potting compost for moisture retention |
| **Perlite** | Drainage improver in pots (10–20%); especially for rosemary, lavender, blueberry, acidanthera, herbs |

---

## THE PLANTS

*Groups: A = Annual Flowers | B = Bulbs | C = Climbers & Wall Plants | F = Fruit Trees & Shrubs | G = Groundcover & Creeping Plants | H = Kitchen Herbs & Vegetables | P = Ornamental Perennials | S = Shade & Woodland Plants | T = Ornamental Trees & Shrubs | W = Pond & Water Plants*

---

## GROUP A: ANNUAL FLOWERS

### A1. Borage *(Borago officinalis)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Borráiste gorm · Бурачник лекарственный*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Borago officinalis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borago_officinalis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Very tolerant, including poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — an easy, undemanding annual herb.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 1cm deep, spring.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants; self-seeds very freely.

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Strawberries, tomatoes — traditional companion, said to attract pollinators and deter some pests; excellent for bees.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, fast-growing annual herb with bristly leaves and clusters of vivid blue, star-shaped, edible flowers beloved by bees, traditionally used in salads and drinks (Pimm's). Self-seeds so readily in the mild Irish climate that it can become a permanent, if welcome, self-sustaining patch once introduced.

---

### A2. Bush Slipperwort 'Sunshine' *(Calceolaria integrifolia 'Sunshine')*


***Russian:*** *Кальцеолярия*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Calceolaria integrifolia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calceolaria_integrifolia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade, sheltered from cold.

**Height/Spread:** 20cm–1m tall depending on form (bedding cultivars like 'Sunshine' stay compact; the species can reach 1m as a perennial), 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Light, moderately fertile, free-draining, slightly acidic soil; pH 5.5–6.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost and grit; ericaceous compost suits it well given the acid preference.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Other tender summer bedding and container plants with similar sun/acid-soil needs.

**West of Ireland:** RHS AGM (species). A bushy evergreen sub-shrub from Chile/Argentina, grown for its clusters of bright yellow, slipper-shaped flowers through spring and summer. Borderline hardy — needs a sheltered, frost-free spot, or treat as summer bedding/container plant lifted or replaced each year in most of Ireland.

---

### A3. California Poppy *(Eschscholzia californica)*


***Russian:*** *Эшшольция калифорнийская*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Eschscholzia californica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschscholzia_californica)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential; flowers close in cloudy weather.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Poor to moderately fertile, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.0. Dislikes rich, wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — thrives on poor soil; improve heavy clay with grit if needed.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 3–5mm deep, spring; dislikes root disturbance from transplanting.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** None required — over-feeding produces lush leaves and fewer flowers.

**Companions:** Cornflower, love-in-a-mist and other easy hardy annuals sharing its sunny, well-drained preference.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, cheerful annual with feathery blue-green foliage and silky orange, yellow or red flowers from February to September, mainly in spring/summer. Self-seeds readily once established, so it returns reliably year after year in a sunny spot with decent drainage.

---

### A4. Cockscomb *(Celosia argentea (Cristata & Plumosa Groups))*


***Russian:*** *Целозия серебристая*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Celosia argentea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celosia_argentea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun, though appreciates afternoon shade in hot spells.

**Height/Spread:** 20–60cm tall depending on cultivar/group (Cristata types shorter, Plumosa types taller, up to 1.2m in some), 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; this is a tender tropical annual best raised in pots/under cover in Ireland.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out after frost risk has passed, at pot level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Other tender summer bedding annuals; good as a cut flower.

**West of Ireland:** A striking tender annual with velvety, brightly coloured crested (cockscomb) or feathery plumed flowerheads that last for weeks and dry well. Needs real summer warmth to perform at its best — start under glass and plant out only once nights are reliably warm; treat as a container or greenhouse-assisted annual in a typical Irish summer.

---

### A5. Cornflower *(Centaurea cyanus)*


***Russian:*** *Василёк синий*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Cornflower — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaurea_cyanus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 15–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit; avoid over-rich ground, which produces weak, floppy stems.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed 1–2cm deep, spring (or autumn for earlier flowers).

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; annual, self-seeds

**Fertiliser:** Little required.

**Companions:** Poppies, love-in-a-mist, briza maxima — classic hardy-annual cutting garden mix, and good for pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** Easy, reliable, and long-flowering through summer if deadheaded regularly. Self-seeds freely; the classic bright blue form is most traditional, though pink and white forms are also available.

---

### A6. Cosmos *(Cosmos bipinnatus)*


***Russian:*** *Космея дваждыперистая*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Cosmos bipinnatus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmos_bipinnatus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 60cm–1.2m tall (occasionally to 2m), 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Poor to moderately fertile, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Rich soil produces lush leaves at the expense of flowers.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — thrives on ordinary or even poor soil; avoid heavy feeding.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct or under cover, 1cm deep, spring once frost risk has passed.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little to none required.

**Companions:** Zinnia, cornflower and other sunny cutting-garden annuals; attractive to pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, floriferous annual with finely cut, feathery foliage and daisy-like pink, white or crimson flowers from summer into autumn. Deadhead regularly to prolong flowering; taller forms may need light staking in the region's wind.

---

### A7. Drumstick Flower *(Craspedia globosa)*


***Russian:*** *Краспедия шаровидная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Craspedia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craspedia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level, or sow under cover in spring.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed.

**Companions:** Strawflower, statice — classic dried/everlasting flower bed companions.

**West of Ireland:** An Australian annual/short-lived perennial grown for its perfectly spherical, golden-yellow "drumstick" flowerheads on tall wiry stems, popular fresh or dried. Needs a warm, sunny, well-drained spot to perform reliably.

---

### A8. Globe Amaranth *(Gomphrena globosa)*


***Russian:*** *Гомфрена шаровидная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Gomphrena globosa — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gomphrena_globosa)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moderately fertile, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost and grit; a genuinely heat-loving tropical annual, best in a warm, sheltered spot or container in Ireland.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out after frost risk has passed, at pot level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Other tender summer bedding annuals sharing its need for warmth and sun.

**West of Ireland:** A tender annual with round, papery, clover-like flowerheads in magenta, purple, pink, orange or white that hold their colour when dried — popular for everlasting arrangements. Needs a warm, sheltered, sunny spot to perform well through an Irish summer; start under glass for the most reliable results.

---

### A9. Greater Quaking Grass *(Briza maxima)*


***Russian:*** *Трясунка большая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Briza Maxima — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Briza_maxima)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 30–50cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed 5mm deep in spring, direct or in trays.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; annual, self-seeds

**Fertiliser:** Little required; over-feeding produces floppy growth.

**Companions:** Cornflower, poppies, love-in-a-mist — classic annual cutting-garden/cottage border mix; the papery seed heads are excellent for dried arrangements.

**West of Ireland:** An easy hardy annual ornamental grass with distinctive nodding, oat-like seed heads that rustle in the breeze — self-seeds readily, so allow some heads to drop if you want it to return next year, or deadhead if not.

---

### A10. Lacy Phacelia *(Phacelia tanacetifolia)*


***Russian:*** *Фацелия пижмолистная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Phacelia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phacelia_tanacetifolia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Most well-drained soils including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Very unfussy.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — often used as a green manure/cover crop precisely because it grows so easily on poor or heavy ground.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed 1cm deep, spring through summer for successional flowering.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; annual, sow successionally

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Excellent sown alongside or near vegetable beds and fruit — one of the very best plants for attracting bees and other pollinators, and can be dug in as a green manure to improve soil structure.

**West of Ireland:** Fast, easy, and highly valuable for pollinators, with fuzzy purple-blue flower heads. Sow a patch near the vegetable garden or fruit trees to boost pollination, or use as a soil-improving green manure between crops.

---

### A100. Lambs Tails *(Ptilotus exaltatus)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Ptilotus exaltatus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptilotus_exaltatus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30cm–1.2m tall depending on conditions, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining soil, tolerant of poor and sandy ground; pH 6.0–7.5. Genuinely drought-tolerant once established, reflecting its arid Australian origin.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 30% horticultural grit; a raised bed, gravel garden or container gives the most reliable drainage.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out after frost risk has passed, at pot level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed — thrives on lean soil; over-feeding produces soft, floppy growth.

**Companions:** Other drought-tolerant annuals and gravel-garden plants sharing its need for sun and sharp drainage.

**West of Ireland:** Also called Tall Mulla Mulla or Pink Mulla Mulla, an Australian annual/short-lived perennial grown for its dense, woolly, bottlebrush-like flower spikes in vivid pink to magenta, a striking and unusual addition to a sunny border or gravel garden. Native to arid and semi-arid Australia, so it is not remotely frost-hardy and must be treated as a half-hardy annual here — started under glass and planted out only once all frost risk has passed, since the mild but consistently damp west of Ireland climate is a world away from its natural home.

---

### A11. Love-in-a-Mist *(Nigella damascena)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Nigéal · Чернушка дамасская (девица в зелени, нигелла)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Love-in-a-Mist — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigella_damascena)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 15–23cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed 1cm deep, spring or autumn, direct where it is to flower (dislikes root disturbance from transplanting).

**Spacing:** 20cm between plants; annual, self-seeds freely

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed if soil is poor; otherwise little required.

**Companions:** Cornflower, poppies, briza maxima — classic hardy annual cutting garden mix; the inflated seed pods are attractive dried.

**West of Ireland:** Airy, feathery foliage surrounds delicate blue, white or pink flowers in summer, followed by ornamental seed pods. Self-seeds very readily — thin seedlings if too dense, or deadhead before seed pods form if self-seeding isn't wanted.

---

### A12. Mealycup Sage 'Blue Bedder' *(Salvia farinacea 'Blue Bedder')*


***Russian:*** *Шалфей мучнистый*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Rosemary — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvia_rosmarinus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential — at least 6 hours daily. Poor, leggy growth and reduced flavour in shade.

**Height/Spread:** 1–1.5m tall, 1–1.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Excellent drainage is essential — rosemary is Mediterranean in origin and will not tolerate sitting in wet clay, especially over winter.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay generously with 30%+ horticultural grit, or plant on a raised mound/bed. Add a layer of grit or pebbles as surface mulch to keep the crown dry.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; do not bury the woody base.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Very little required — rich soil produces soft, less aromatic growth. A light balanced feed in spring is sufficient. Container plants: occasional liquid feed through summer.

**Companions:** Classic companion for brassicas (deters cabbage moth), carrots and beans in the vegetable garden; also thyme, lavender and other Mediterranean herbs with the same sun/drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** One of the herbs most likely to struggle on wet west of Ireland clay in winter — a raised bed, gritty mound, or a large terracotta container that can be given a sheltered spot is strongly recommended over open ground. Trim lightly after flowering to keep compact; avoid cutting into old bare wood.

---

### A13. Poached Egg Plant *(Limnanthes douglasii)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cúr léana · Лимнантес Дугласа*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Limnanthes douglasii — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limnanthes_douglasii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant and easy.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 5mm deep, spring or autumn.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Excellent for attracting bees and hoverflies; good edging for a vegetable bed.

**West of Ireland:** A low, spreading annual with cheerful yellow-and-white flowers resembling a fried/poached egg, from late spring into summer. Easy and self-seeding in the mild Irish climate; a real pollinator magnet.

---

### A14. Poppies *(Papaver spp., e.g. P. rhoeas, Shirley poppies)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cailleach dhearg · Мак самосейка*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Poppies — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaver_rhoeas)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 45–90cm tall, 30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit; avoid over-rich ground.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed on the surface or just barely covered (needs light to germinate), spring or autumn, direct where it is to flower.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; annual, self-seeds freely

**Fertiliser:** Little required.

**Companions:** Cornflower, love-in-a-mist, briza maxima — classic hardy annual meadow/cutting garden mix.

**West of Ireland:** The classic annual field/Shirley poppy self-seeds very freely and is one of the easiest hardy annuals to naturalise in a sunny border or wildflower patch. (Note: perennial Oriental poppies, *Papaver orientale*, are a different, longer-lived border plant with similar sun/drainage needs if a non-annual poppy is wanted instead.)

---

### A15. Pot Marigold *(Calendula officinalis)*


***Russian:*** *Календула лекарственная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Calendula officinalis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendula_officinalis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–50cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Very tolerant, including poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — an easy, undemanding annual.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 1cm deep, spring or autumn.

**Spacing:** 30cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Vegetable beds — traditional companion said to deter aphids and attract pollinators/beneficial insects.

**West of Ireland:** One of the easiest annuals there is, with cheerful orange or yellow daisy flowers from late spring right through to autumn if deadheaded. Self-seeds readily in the mild Irish climate; petals are edible and traditionally used medicinally.

---

### A16. Snapdragons *(Antirrhinum majus)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Srubh lao · Львиный зев*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Antirrhinum majus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antirrhinum_majus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–90cm tall depending on variety, 25–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with grit.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out at pot level.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Cosmos, zinnia — classic cutting-garden annual bed companions.

**West of Ireland:** A classic cottage-garden annual/short-lived perennial with tall spikes of colourful, snap-jawed flowers from summer into autumn. Often grown as a half-hardy annual in Ireland; taller varieties benefit from staking in wind.

---

### A17. Strawflower *(Xerochrysum bracteatum)*


***Russian:*** *Бессмертник прицветниковый*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Xerochrysum bracteatum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerochrysum_bracteatum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 20–80cm tall depending on cultivar, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Poor to moderately fertile, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — tolerant of poor, dry soil; improve heavy clay with grit.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out after frost risk, at pot level.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little required.

**Companions:** Other everlasting/dried-flower annuals; cornflower, statice.

**West of Ireland:** An Australian annual with brilliant, papery-petalled daisy flowers in gold, orange, red, pink or white from spring to autumn, prized for retaining colour and shape when dried. Straightforward in a warm, sunny, well-drained spot; deadhead to prolong flowering.

---

### A18. Sweet Alyssum *(Lobularia maritima)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Alasam cumhra · Лобулярия приморская (алиссум морской)*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Lobularia maritima — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobularia_maritima)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — very tolerant, even of poor soil.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 3mm deep, spring.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Edging for beds and containers; excellent for attracting pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** A low, fragrant, mat-forming annual smothered in tiny white, pink or purple flowers from early summer into autumn. Easy, self-seeding, and a genuine pollinator favourite in the mild Irish climate.

---

### A19. Sweet Peas *(Lathyrus odoratus)*


***Russian:*** *Душистый горошек*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Sweet Peas — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lathyrus_odoratus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 1.8–2.5m tall (climbing), 30–45cm spread at base.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig in plenty of compost — sweet peas are hungrier and thirstier than most annuals. Improve drainage with grit if the site is very wet.

**Planting depth:** Sow seed 2cm deep, spring (or autumn under cover for earlier, stronger plants).

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants against support

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed weekly once flowering begins; a high-potash feed (tomato feed) encourages more blooms.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, clematis — grow up the same wall/trellis for a long season of colour; also a classic vegetable-garden companion, attracting pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** Fragrant, prolific climbing annual for a sunny wall, trellis or wigwam of canes — regular picking (rather than letting pods form) is the key to a long flowering season. Needs support to climb by tendrils and consistent moisture at the roots.

---


---

### A20. Winged Everlasting *(Ammobium alatum)*


***Russian:*** *Аммобиум крылатый*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Ammobium alatum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammobium_alatum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 60–100cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Poor to moderately fertile, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — an easy, undemanding Australian native; improve heavy clay with grit.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct or under cover, spring, at pot level once transplanted.

**Spacing:** 30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little required.

**Companions:** Strawflower, statice and other papery-flowered everlastings for a dried-flower bed.

**West of Ireland:** An easy annual/short-lived perennial with distinctive winged stems and small, bright yellow-centred flowers surrounded by papery white bracts, useful fresh or dried, summer into autumn. Straightforward in a sunny, well-drained spot.

---

### A21. Zinnia *(Zinnia elegans)*


***Russian:*** *Цинния изящная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Zinnia elegans — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinnia_elegans)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–90cm tall depending on variety, 25–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moderately fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes cold, wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost and grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** Sow under cover in spring, plant out after frost risk, at pot level; dislikes root disturbance.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants; good air circulation reduces powdery mildew risk.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Cosmos, cornflower and other sunny cutting-garden annuals; excellent for pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** A classic warmth-loving cutting-garden annual with bold, long-lasting daisy flowers in a wide range of colours, summer into autumn. Needs a warm, sheltered, sunny spot and good airflow in the region's damp climate to avoid mildew; deadhead regularly for continued flowering.

---

## GROUP B: BULBS

### B1. Acidanthera (Abyssinian Gladiolus) *(Gladiolus murielae)*


***Russian:*** *Шпажник Мюриэл*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Acidanthera — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gladiolus_murielae)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — essential. Plant in the warmest, sunniest spot available.

**Height/Spread:** 70–100cm tall, 10–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, well-drained, pH 6.0–7.0. Plant on sharp sand base.

**Planting depth:** 10–16cm deep on a bed of sharp sand. Plant April–May. Lift after first frosts; dry and store frost-free.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between corms

**Fertiliser:** Balanced bulb fertiliser or tomato feed fortnightly from when shoots appear until flowering in August–September. Feed until foliage begins to die down.

**Companions:** Salvias, dahlias, gladioli, ornamental grasses, white agapanthus. Elegant near deep purple salvias or against dark evergreen hedging.

---

### B2. African Lily *(Agapanthus africanus)*


***Russian:*** *Агапантус африканский*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Agapanthus africanus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agapanthus_africanus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes winter waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit; a raised bed or large container gives the most reliable results on wet clay.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Crocosmia, ornamental grasses — classic late-summer border companions.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Grows very well in a large container, which also makes winter protection straightforward on wet clay.

**West of Ireland:** A striking South African bulb with tall stems topped by rounded clusters of blue or white trumpet flowers in mid-to-late summer. Borderline hardy — reliable outdoors in mild, sheltered coastal gardens with sharp drainage, or grown in a container that can be given winter protection elsewhere.

---

### B3. Anemone *(Anemone spp.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus na gaoithe · Ветреница нежная*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Wood Anemone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone_nemorosa) · [Balkan Anemone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone_blanda) · [Poppy Anemone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone_coronaria)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Balkan Anemone | *A. blanda* | 10–15cm | Early–mid spring | 5–8cm deep | Daisy-like blue, pink or white flowers; naturalises well in light woodland; soak corms 24 hours before planting |
| Poppy Anemone | *A. coronaria* | 20–40cm | Spring (April–June in Ireland's cooler climate) | 5–8cm deep | Large, poppy-like single flowers in vivid red, blue, purple or white with a black centre; needs consistent moisture while in growth; less reliably perennial than the other two, often grown as a replaced-yearly display |
| Wood Anemone | *A. nemorosa* | 10–15cm | Early–mid spring | 5–8cm deep | Native Irish woodland-floor species; white, sometimes pink-flushed, star flowers; the easiest and most reliable of the three to naturalise |

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade (woodland conditions) for Balkan and Wood Anemone. Poppy Anemone wants full sun and consistent moisture, unlike its shade-tolerant relatives.

**Height/Spread:** 10–40cm tall depending on species (see table), 10–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Balkan and Wood Anemone: humus-rich, moist but well-drained soil, pH 6.0–7.5; both are genuine woodland-floor plants. Poppy Anemone: well-drained but reliably moist soil during the growing season, pH 6.0–7.5 — dislikes drying out while in leaf and flower.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost/leaf mould for all three.

**Planting depth:** All three: 5–8cm deep, in autumn (September–October). Soak corms of all three for 24 hours before planting to improve establishment.

**Spacing:** Balkan and Wood Anemone: 10cm between corms, planted in generous drifts. Poppy Anemone: 10–15cm between corms.

**Fertiliser:** None of the three are heavy feeders. Light balanced fertiliser in spring; allow foliage to die back naturally.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, bluebells, foxgloves in a woodland-edge bed for Balkan and Wood Anemone. Poppy Anemone suits a sunnier cutting-garden bed alongside ranunculus and other spring corms.

**West of Ireland:** Wood Anemone is native and the easiest to naturalise here, spreading gently by rhizome into drifts under trees and shrubs. Balkan Anemone behaves similarly and combines beautifully with it for an extended display. Poppy Anemone is the odd one out — it wants full sun rather than shade, and while fully hardy, it is less reliably perennial in Ireland's wet winters than the other two; many gardeners treat it as a colourful, replaceable spring bedding corm rather than a permanent naturaliser.

---

### B4. Camassia *(Camassia spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Камассия*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Camassia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camassia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–100cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. More tolerant of damp ground than most bulbs.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; less reliant on heavy grit than most bulbs in this guide.

**Planting depth:** 10–15cm deep, autumn.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between bulbs.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting; otherwise little needed.

**Companions:** Alliums, late tulips, ornamental grasses — a good bridge between spring bulbs and early-summer perennials.

**West of Ireland:** A tall, elegant North American bulb with spikes of star-shaped blue, purple or white flowers in late spring/early summer. Tolerates the region's moist soil better than many bulbs, making it a reliable, easy naturaliser in a sunny border.

---

### B5. Corn Lily *(Ixia spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Иксия*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Ixia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixia_(plant))

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 30–50cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Light, very free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.0. Corms rot in wet, heavy clay.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay heavily with grit, or grow in containers of free-draining compost for best results.

**Planting depth:** 8–10cm deep, pointed end up, in spring (March–April) once frost risk has passed.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between corms

**Fertiliser:** Low-nitrogen bulb/liquid feed while in leaf; avoid feeding once foliage dies back.

**Companions:** Freesia and other South African corms with similar sun and drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** Borderline hardy (RHS H3) — in most of the west of Ireland best grown in pots that can be given a sheltered, dry position over winter, or lifted after the foliage dies down in autumn and stored dry and frost-free like gladioli, replanting the following spring. Wiry stems of star-shaped flowers in early summer.

---

### B6. Crocus *(Crocus spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Крокус*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Crocus tommasinianus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocus_tommasinianus) · [Crocus chrysanthus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocus_chrysanthus) · [Crocus speciosus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocus_speciosus) · [Crocus sativus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocus_sativus)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Early Crocus ('Tommie') | *C. tommasinianus* | 7–10cm | Early spring (February) | 7–10cm deep | Pale lilac; self-seeds prolifically and forms spreading carpets within a few years — the easiest crocus to naturalise |
| Snow Crocus | *C. chrysanthus* | 7–10cm | Late winter–early spring | 7–10cm deep | Smaller flowers in a wide colour range, often bicoloured; one of the earliest to flower alongside 'Tommie' |
| Dutch/Large-flowered Crocus | *Crocus* spp. (Dutch hybrids) | 10–15cm | Early–mid spring | 10–12cm deep | The familiar bold, large-cupped garden and lawn crocus |
| Autumn Crocus (Bieberstein's) | *C. speciosus* | 10–15cm | Autumn (October–November) | 8–10cm deep; plant in August | Lilac-blue with orange stigmas, flowers before the leaves appear; 'Albus' tolerates light shade under deciduous shrubs |
| Saffron Crocus | *C. sativus* | 10–15cm | Autumn (October) | 8–10cm deep; plant in August | Purple flowers with the vivid red stigmas harvested as saffron; needs a warm, sheltered, sharply-drained spot |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun preferred for most types. *C. speciosus* 'Albus' tolerates light shade under deciduous shrubs. Spring crocuses: sunny border, lawn or pot. Autumn crocuses: as for spring types.

**Height/Spread:** 7–15cm tall depending on species (see table), 5–8cm spread per corm, spreading into clumps.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained to gritty, poor to moderately fertile, pH 6.0–7.5. On heavy clay, add a handful of horticultural grit beneath each corm to prevent waterlogging. Spring types tolerate summer moisture if drainage is right; *C. sativus* prefers dry summers and is best grown in a pot.

**Soil prep:** Add 20–25% grit to clay planting areas. In lawns, use a bulb auger or trowel to make individual holes; place a little grit beneath each corm.

**Planting depth:** *C. tommasinianus* and *C. chrysanthus*: 7–10cm deep in small groups. Dutch hybrids: bigger corms, can go deeper at 12cm in garden lawns. *C. speciosus* and *C. sativus*: 8–10cm deep, planted a little earlier, in August. All: space individually about 9cm apart, pointed tip facing upward, September–November for spring-flowering types. A 20cm pot holds 15–20 corms comfortably — layer them 5–8cm deep, almost touching, for a dense display. Use a mix of multipurpose compost and horticultural grit (roughly 3:1) for sharp drainage. Fully hardy — corms are left in the ground permanently and never need lifting.

**Spacing:** 8–9cm between corms.

**Fertiliser:** Feed after flowering when leaves are still in active growth. Very little feeding required otherwise. A light balanced bulb fertiliser while foliage is green is sufficient. Allow foliage to yellow before removing — this feeds the corm for the following year.

**Companions:** For a layered spring display in grass, combine *C. tommasinianus* and *C. chrysanthus* with snowdrops (January–February) and daffodils (March–April). The three together give nearly four months of colour before the grass needs cutting. Muscari, squill, and winter aconites are also superb companions. Excellent beneath deciduous shrubs. Do not mow for at least 6 weeks after flowering in lawns. ⚠️ Squirrels and mice dig up freshly planted corms — cover with chicken wire for the first season.

**Pot size:** Use pots at least 15cm deep with drainage holes. A 20cm pot holds 15–20 corms comfortably.

**West of Ireland:** Crocuses naturalise magnificently in Irish gardens. *C. tommasinianus* — early February, pale lilac — is the standout performer, spreading into carpets with almost no effort once established, and is the crocus most worth planting generously if naturalising is the goal. Autumn-flowering types (*C. speciosus*, *C. sativus*) extend interest into October–November.

---

### B7. Daffodils *(Narcissus spp.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus an chromchinn · Нарцисс*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Daffodils — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcissus_(plant))

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Naturalise under deciduous trees.

**Height/Spread:** 15–45cm tall depending on variety, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, pH 6.0–7.0. Add grit at base of each planting hole.

**Planting depth:** 15cm (3× bulb height). Plant September–October. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Balanced bulb fertiliser after flowering while foliage still green. Allow foliage to yellow for at least 6 weeks before removing. Autumn compost mulch on naturalised drifts.

**Companions:** Snowdrops (succession), tulips (follow on), Pulmonaria and muscari for ground cover. Naturalise magnificently under apple trees.

---

### B8. Dwarf Iris *(Iris reticulata)*


***Russian:*** *Ирис сетчатый*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Dwarf Iris — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_reticulata)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining soil essential; pH 6.0–7.5. Bulbs rot in wet, heavy clay over winter.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay generously with 30–40% horticultural grit; excellent drainage is the key requirement. Raised beds, troughs or pots are often more reliable than open clay ground.

**Planting depth:** 8–10cm deep, pointed end up, in autumn. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Bonemeal or bulb fertiliser at planting (autumn). Low-nitrogen liquid feed while in leaf after flowering, to build next year's bulb.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, crocus and dwarf daffodils for a very early, low-growing spring display; also excellent at the front of a border or in rockeries.

**West of Ireland:** One of the earliest bulbs to flower, often in February. Best in a very free-draining spot such as a raised bed, trough or pot given typical clay drainage; can struggle to persist in heavy wet ground.

---

### B27. Dwarf Sword Lily *(Gladiolus nanus)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Gladiolus nanus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gladiolus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 40–70cm tall, 10–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Plant on a bed of sharp sand to aid drainage; improve clay with 20–25% grit.

**Planting depth:** 10–16cm deep, spring.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between corms.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly while in active growth.

**Companions:** Freesia, ixia and other South African corms with similar sun and drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** A dwarf, early-flowering gladiolus (Nanus Group) — smaller and dainter than the tall, later-flowering "large-flowered" gladiolus, with star-shaped flowers in soft pastel colours in early summer. More weather-resistant than its taller relatives and generally doesn't need staking. In milder west of Ireland gardens a thick, dry winter mulch is often enough to leave corms in the ground; in colder, more exposed spots lift when foliage dies back and store dry and frost-free over winter, replanting in spring.

---

### B9. Erythronium (Dog's Tooth Violet) *(Erythronium dens-canis)*


***Russian:*** *Кандык*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Erythronium dens-canis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erythronium_dens-canis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade; woodland conditions.

**Height/Spread:** 15–25cm tall, 10–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, humus-rich, well-drained soil that does not dry out; pH 5.5–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost or leaf mould; keep bulbs slightly damp before planting, since they dry out and fail to establish if left dry for long.

**Planting depth:** 10cm deep, planted in autumn while the soil is still warm; avoid spring planting, as bulbs establish poorly outside their natural cycle.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between bulbs; spreads gently into clumps over the years.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting; otherwise very little needed.

**Companions:** Ferns, hellebores and other woodland-floor perennials; excellent under deciduous trees, in rock gardens, or near ponds and streams.

**West of Ireland:** A charming, nodding, lily-shaped spring bulb (mid-spring, April–June) with recurved mauve-pink petals marked with cinnamon and yellow at the throat, above attractive mottled purple-brown foliage. Naturalises gently in the cool, consistently moist woodland-floor conditions the west of Ireland climate provides, without becoming invasive — one of the more refined, less thuggish additions to a shaded spring border.

---

### B10. Freesia *(Freesia spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Фрезия*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Freesia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freesia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 8–10cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Light, free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.0. Corms are tender/half-hardy and dislike cold, wet clay.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with grit and compost, or grow entirely in containers of multipurpose/free-draining compost for reliability.

**Planting depth:** 5–8cm deep, pointed end up, in spring (March–April) once frost risk has passed.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between corms

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed fortnightly once in growth, until foliage yellows after flowering.

**Companions:** Ixia, other South African corms with similar needs; good in mixed pots with other tender bulbs.

**West of Ireland:** Not reliably frost-hardy (RHS H2–H3) — in most west of Ireland gardens best grown in containers that can be moved under cover, or lifted and stored dry over winter like gladioli. Fragrant, long-lasting cut flowers.

---

### B11. Fritillaria *(Fritillaria spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Checkered Lily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_meleagris) · [Crown Imperial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_imperialis) · [Fritillaria acmopetala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_acmopetala) · [Fox's Grape Fritillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_uva-vulpis) · [F. michailovskyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_michailovskyi) · [Persian Fritillary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritillaria_persica)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Checkered Lily (Snake's Head Fritillary) | *F. meleagris* | 20–30cm | April–May | 10cm deep | Checkered purple-and-white nodding bells; tolerates damp clay far better than the rest — the easiest to naturalise |
| *F. acmopetala* | *F. acmopetala* | 30–70cm | April | 10cm deep | Bell-shaped flowers flaring at the mouth; yellowish-green outer tepals with red veins, purplish-brown and yellow inside; easy and reliable |
| Crown Imperial | *F. imperialis* | 80cm–1.2m | April–May | 20–30cm deep, on its side | Tall crown-topped spike of orange, red or yellow bells; needs alkaline, sharply drained soil |
| Fox's Grape Fritillary | *F. uva-vulpis* | 15–30cm | April | 10cm deep | Small plum-brown bells with a grey bloom; needs a dry winter position |
| *F. michailovskyi* | *F. michailovskyi* | 10–20cm | April–May | 8–10cm deep | Small maroon bells tipped bright yellow; the most demanding of the group for drainage |
| Persian Fritillary | *F. persica* | 60cm+ | May | 15–20cm deep, on its side | Tall spike of small plum-purple bells; needs alkaline, sharply drained soil like Crown Imperial |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for all — *F. meleagris* also tolerates partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20–90cm tall depending on species (see table), 8–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 6.0–8.0 depending on species. *F. meleagris*: moist but reasonably drained, tolerates damp clay. All others (*acmopetala*, *imperialis*, *uva-vulpis*, *michailovskyi*, *persica*): free-draining soil essential, and *imperialis*/*persica* need it neutral to alkaline; none tolerate waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** *F. meleagris*: improve clay with compost; heavy grit is less critical given its preference for damper ground. The rest: improve clay generously with 25–40% horticultural grit (30% for *imperialis* and *persica*, worked in over a 35–40cm depth with grit at the base of the hole); a raised bed, trough or pot gives the most reliable results for *acmopetala*, *uva-vulpis* and *michailovskyi* on heavy west of Ireland clay.

**Planting depth:** Plant all in autumn. *F. meleagris*: 10cm deep. *F. acmopetala*: 10cm deep. *F. uva-vulpis*: 10cm deep. *F. michailovskyi*: 8–10cm deep. *F. imperialis*: 20–30cm deep, on its side. *F. persica*: 15–20cm deep, on its side. All pointed end up except where planted on their side to stop water pooling in the hollow crown top. Fully hardy — none need lifting in the west of Ireland climate, though small bulbs of *meleagris*, *acmopetala*, *uva-vulpis* and *michailovskyi* dry out quickly out of the ground, so plant promptly after purchase, and *imperialis*/*persica* resent disturbance once settled.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs for the smaller species (including *acmopetala*); 20–25cm for Persian Fritillary; 25cm for Crown Imperial.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting for all. Crown Imperial and Persian Fritillary are hungrier: balanced bulb fertiliser as shoots emerge, plus a high-potash liquid feed once or twice before flowering. The rest need very little beyond the initial bonemeal.

**Companions:** *F. meleagris*: snowdrops, daffodils, damp border or pond-margin planting. *F. acmopetala*, *F. uva-vulpis*, *F. michailovskyi*: other species fritillarias, dwarf iris, crocus in a sharply drained rockery. *F. imperialis*, *F. persica*: tulips, alliums, euphorbia, wallflowers.

**West of Ireland:** *F. meleagris* is one of the easiest bulbs to naturalise here, thriving in the moist climate. The rest are trickier: *F. imperialis* and *F. persica* are ⚠️ challenging on wet clay and are most reliable in a raised bed or large gritty container, while *F. uva-vulpis* and *F. michailovskyi* need a raised bed, trough or pot rather than open clay ground to survive winter wet.

---

### B12. Grape Hyacinth *(Muscari spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Мышиный гиацинт (мускари)*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Muscari — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscari)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 20–25% grit.

**Planting depth:** 8–10cm deep, autumn.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between bulbs.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting.

**Companions:** Daffodils, tulips, crocus — classic layered spring bulb display.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, reliably hardy small bulb with dense spikes of tiny, bead-like blue (occasionally white) flowers in spring, smelling faintly of grapes. Naturalises readily in a sunny border or under deciduous shrubs; can self-seed generously once established.

---

### B100. Hyacinth *(Hyacinthus orientalis)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hyacinthus orientalis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyacinthus_orientalis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ light shade; flowers less freely in shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20–30cm tall, 10–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moderately fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with 20–25% horticultural grit to improve drainage.

**Planting depth:** 10cm deep, autumn.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs.

**Fertiliser:** Bonemeal or bulb fertiliser at planting; balanced liquid feed as foliage emerges in spring.

**Companions:** Daffodils, tulips, forget-me-nots — classic spring bedding companions.

**West of Ireland:** One of the most intensely fragrant spring bulbs, with dense, waxy racemes of bell-shaped flowers in blue, white, purple, pink or yellow, opening from March to April. Fully hardy (down to around -20°C) and easy in the mild Irish climate; excellent for pots near a door or window where the scent can be appreciated. Container-grown bulbs should be protected from excessive winter wet.

---

### B13. Iron Cross *(Oxalis tetraphylla 'Iron Cross')*


***Russian:*** *Кислица*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Iron Cross — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxalis_tetraphylla)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** 5cm deep, growing point up, in spring (April–May) once frost risk has passed.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between tubers; lift over winter

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Front-of-border and container planting with other low, colourful summer bulbs such as ixia or dwarf dahlias.

**West of Ireland:** Grown for its distinctive four-lobed leaves marked with a dark maroon "iron cross" pattern and pink summer flowers. Tuber is not reliably frost-hardy — lift and store dry over winter like a dahlia, or grow in containers that can be moved under cover.

---

### B14. Lilies *(Lilium spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Лилия*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lilies — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilium)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Most prefer heads in sun, roots shaded by low plants.

**Height/Spread:** 60cm–1.8m tall depending on type, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, pH 6.0–7.0. Drainage essential.

**Soil prep:** Compost and 20–25% grit. Place 5cm grit at base of each hole.

**Planting depth:** 15cm (3× bulb height) on a bed of grit, in autumn (September–October) or early spring. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 15–30cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Balanced bulb fertiliser as shoots emerge. Tomato feed fortnightly from bud formation until flowering. Continue feeding until foliage yellows. Do not remove foliage until it dies back naturally.

**Companions:** Hostas, geraniums, low salvias (shade the soil around bulbs). Roses (lilies fill the midsummer gap). Alliums in the same border.

---

### B15. Martagon Lily *(Lilium martagon)*


***Russian:*** *Лилия кудреватая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Martagon Lily — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilium_martagon)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to sun; happier in dappled woodland-edge light than full baking sun.

**Height/Spread:** 90–150cm tall, 30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, humus-rich soil, tolerant of neutral to slightly alkaline conditions; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Compost and 20% grit at planting; unlike many lilies it tolerates heavier soil reasonably well once drainage is adequate.

**Planting depth:** 15cm deep (3× bulb height), on a bed of grit, in autumn (September–October) or early spring. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Balanced bulb fertiliser as shoots emerge; otherwise undemanding once established. Do not remove foliage until it dies back naturally.

**Companions:** Naturalises well among shrubs, in light woodland or a shady border with hostas, ferns and hellebores — unlike many lilies, martagon lily is happy in part shade.

**West of Ireland:** More shade-tolerant and better suited to naturalising under trees/shrubs than most garden lilies, with distinctive nodding, "turk's cap" recurved flowers in early-mid summer. Slow to establish — settle for two to three years before flowering well, but long-lived once happy.

---

### B16. Native Bluebells *(Hyacinthoides non-scripta)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Coinnle corra · Гиацинтоидес неописанный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Native Bluebells — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyacinthoides_non-scripta)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred; ideal under deciduous trees where it flowers before the canopy closes. Tolerates full sun to full shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20–40cm tall, 5–10cm spread per bulb, spreading into drifts.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil; slightly acid, pH 6.0–6.8. Tolerates clay reasonably well if not waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with leaf mould or compost; heavy grit amendment is not essential.

**Planting depth:** 8cm deep, pointed end up, in autumn.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Little required. Bonemeal at planting is sufficient; leave foliage to die back naturally each year to feed the bulb.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, primroses, wood anemones — classic native woodland-floor planting under deciduous trees and shrubs such as hawthorn, rowan or Japanese maple.

**West of Ireland:** Ireland's native bluebell, distinguished from the non-native Spanish bluebell by its narrower, more strongly recurved deep violet-blue flowers borne on one side of a nodding stem, its sweet scent, and creamy-white (not blue) pollen. Naturalises readily in a woodland-style planting and is excellent for pollinators. Spreads by both offsets and self-seeding, forming drifts over time, but is less aggressive and better behaved in a garden setting than Spanish bluebell.

⚠️ Harmful if eaten; wear gloves when handling bulbs, as the sap can irritate skin.

---

### B17. Ornamental Onion *(Allium spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Allium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium) · [Allium giganteum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_giganteum) · [Allium moly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_moly) · [Allium oreophilum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_oreophilum) · [Allium neapolitanum (cowanii)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_neapolitanum) · [Allium nigrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_nigrum) · [Allium atropurpureum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_atropurpureum) · [Allium 'Millennium'](https://www.rhs.org.uk/plants/315303/allium-millennium/details)

| Species | Latin name | Flower colour | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Giant Onion | *A. giganteum* | Purple | 1.2–1.5m | Late spring–early summer | 20cm deep | Huge globes to 15cm across; RHS AGM; the classic tall border allium |
| Golden Garlic | *A. moly* | Bright yellow | 25–30cm | Late spring | 8–10cm deep | Star-shaped flowers in loose clusters; spreads readily, almost naturalising |
| Ostrowsky's Onion | *A. oreophilum* | Rose-pink | 15–20cm | Late spring | 8–10cm deep | Loose clusters on short stems; good rockery/front-of-border allium |
| Naples Garlic | *A. cowanii* (syn. *A. neapolitanum*) | White | 20–30cm | Early–mid spring | 8–10cm deep | Fragrant star flowers; flowers earlier than the rest; slightly less reliably hardy — see note below |
| *Allium* 'Nigrum' | *A. multibulbosum* 'Nigrum' (= *A. nigrum*) | White, dark centre | 60–90cm | Late spring–early summer | 15cm deep | Unusual open, spike-like flower head rather than a tight ball |
| Purple-flowered Onion | *A. atropurpureum* | Deep maroon-purple | 80cm–1m | Late spring–early summer | 20cm deep | Smaller, darker blooms than Giant Onion; a strong eye-catcher |
| Honey Garlic (Sicilian Honey Lily) | *A. siculum* (syn. *Nectaroscordum siculum*) | Cream with maroon streaks | Up to 1.2m | May–early June | 15–20cm deep | Graceful drooping bell-shaped flowers on tall stems, followed by decorative upright seed pods; blue-grey twisted foliage; self-seeds readily |
| *Allium* 'Millennium' | *A. nutans* × *A. lusitanicum* hybrid | Rose-purple | 40cm | Mid–late summer (July–August) | 10–15cm deep | RHS Plant of the Year-winning hybrid bred by Mark McDonough; neat, tidy clump-forming habit with glossy deep green foliage that stays attractive all season; long-lasting 5cm globe flowerheads, excellent for pollinators; less prone to self-seeding than many alliums |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for all.

**Height/Spread:** 20cm–1.2m tall depending on species (see table), 10–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining soil; pH any, but bulbs rot in waterlogged clay, so drainage is essential. All tolerate loam, sand, clay and stony ground provided it drains.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% horticultural grit before planting; raised beds or mounded planting help on heavy soil.

**Planting depth:** Plant all in autumn (mid to late autumn is ideal). Roughly 2–3× the bulb's own depth, pointed end up (about 20cm for Giant Onion's large bulbs, 8–10cm for the smaller species). Space bulbs about twice the bulb's diameter apart. All are fully hardy and, once planted, are left in the ground permanently — none need lifting in the west of Ireland climate. *A. cowanii* is the one partial exception: as a Mediterranean species it is slightly less robustly hardy than the others, so in colder inland gardens (rather than the milder coastal west) it benefits from a sheltered spot or a winter mulch, though it is not usually necessary to lift it.

**Spacing:** 10–20cm between bulbs for the smaller species; 25–30cm for Giant Onion and *A.* 'Nigrum'/atropurpureum.

**Fertiliser:** Bonemeal or bulb fertiliser at planting. Balanced liquid feed as foliage emerges in spring, stopped once flowering finishes.

**Companions:** Roses (alliums are classic rose-border companions and can help deter some pests), peonies, irises, ornamental grasses. The smaller species (moly, oreophilum, cowanii) suit rockeries and gravel gardens; the taller ones (giganteum, 'Nigrum', atropurpureum) suit borders and cut-flower beds.

**West of Ireland:** All flower in late spring—for *A. cowanii* it's notably earlier, into March–April. Excellent in pots for good drainage on wet clay sites; leave foliage to die back naturally to feed next year's bulb. Honey Garlic self-seeds readily in the mild, moist climate here — deadhead promptly if you want to keep it contained, or let the ornamental seed pods stand for a naturalised look.

---

### B18. Pineapple Lily *(Eucomis spp.)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Eucomis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucomis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 25–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging, especially in winter while dormant.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit for drainage; a raised bed or large container gives the most reliable results.

**Planting depth:** Plant the bulb with its neck/tip at or just below the soil surface, roughly 10–15cm deep, in spring once frost risk has passed.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between bulbs.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed fortnightly through the growing season; high-potash feed as flower spikes develop.

**Companions:** Alliums, crocosmia, dahlias — bold, late-summer border companions with similar sun and drainage needs.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Grows very well in a large container, which makes lifting for winter storage in colder areas straightforward.

**West of Ireland:** A dramatic, exotic-looking bulb from South Africa with rosettes of strap-shaped leaves and tall, densely packed flower spikes topped with a tuft of leafy bracts (resembling a pineapple, hence the name), in white, green, pink or maroon depending on species/cultivar, from mid-to-late summer. Borderline hardy (RHS H3–H4 depending on species) — in most of the west of Ireland, mulch the dormant bulb thickly over winter if left in the ground, or lift and store dry and frost-free, similar to a dahlia tuber; a large container that can be moved to a sheltered spot for winter is the most reliable approach on wet clay.

---

### B19. Ranunculus *(Ranunculus asiaticus)*


***Russian:*** *Лютик азиатский*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Ranunculus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranunculus_asiaticus)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile, pH 6.0–7.0. Tubers rot in wet clay.

**Soil prep:** Compost and 20–30% grit. Plant on a bed of grit.

**Planting depth:** 5–8cm deep, claws pointing downward. Soak tubers 2–4 hours before planting. Plant in spring (February–April). Not reliably hardy — lift after flowering, dry, and store frost-free over winter, or treat as a seasonal pot plant.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between tubers

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid fertiliser every 2 weeks once shoots appear. Liquid tomato feed from bud formation until flowering. Container: monthly balanced liquid feed.

**Companions:** Pansies and violas in cool-season containers. Muscari, fritillaria, spring anemones flower simultaneously.

**West of Ireland:** ⚠️ Treat as seasonal pot plant — lift after flowering, dry, and store frost-free.

---

### B28. River Lily *(Hesperantha coccinea*, syn. *Schizostylis coccinea)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hesperantha coccinea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hesperantha_coccinea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ light shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.0. Tolerates damp ground well and even poorly-drained soil, given its native streamside habitat.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost; unlike most bulbs in this guide, this plant does not need extra grit and actually prefers a moisture-retentive spot.

**Planting depth:** Rhizome at soil level, spring.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; spreads gradually by rhizome to form good-sized clumps.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Astilbe, Japanese anemone, ornamental grasses — good companions for a moist, sunny border or pond-margin edge.

**West of Ireland:** Also called Crimson Flag Lily, a South African semi-evergreen perennial with sword-shaped leaves and spikes of vivid scarlet-red, star-shaped flowers from late summer into autumn — a valuable source of late-season colour when much else has finished. Genuinely happy in the moist soil typical of the west of Ireland climate; hardy to around -5 to -10°C, so a sheltered spot or a dry winter mulch is worthwhile in colder, more exposed gardens.

---

### B20. Siberian Squill *(Scilla siberica)*


***Russian:*** *Пролеска сибирская*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Scilla siberica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scilla_siberica)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 20–25% grit.

**Planting depth:** 8–10cm deep, autumn.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between bulbs.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, crocus, daffodils — classic early spring bulb layering.

**West of Ireland:** A small, reliably hardy bulb with vivid blue, nodding, bell-shaped flowers in early spring, naturalising readily under deciduous trees or in a lawn. Easy and low-maintenance once established.

---

### B21. Snowdrops *(Galanthus nivalis)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Plúirín sneachta · Подснежник белоснежный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Snowdrops — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galanthus_nivalis)

**Light:** 🌥️ to 🌑 Partial to full shade. Under deciduous trees is ideal — winter/spring light before canopy forms.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs

**Soil needs:** Humus-rich, moisture-retentive but well-drained, pH 5.5–7.0. Best planted "in the green."

**Planting depth (dry bulbs):** 10cm deep, 8–10cm apart, in autumn (September–October).
**Planting depth (in the green):** At same depth as growing; plant in spring, just after flowering, while leaves are still on. Fully hardy either way — never needs lifting.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced fertiliser in early spring while foliage is still green. Annual leaf mould or compost mulch. Allow foliage to die back completely before removing.

**Companions:** Hellebores (classic January–March winter combination). Aconites, early narcissus, cyclamen coum in February–March. Underplant shrubs (forsythia, lilac) with snowdrops for colour before the shrubs flower.

---

### B22. Snowflake (Summer) *(Leucojum aestivum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Plúirín samhraidh · Белоцветник летний*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Snowflake — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leucojum_aestivum)

**Note on naming:** Despite its common name, the Summer Snowflake, *Leucojum aestivum*, flowers in mid-spring and goes dormant during summer, not in summer itself. It features nodding, bell-shaped white flowers with green-tipped tepals, and is softly chocolate-scented.

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. They prefer soil that stays cool and damp, making them ideal for borders, woodland edges, or near water.

**Height/Spread:** 15–45cm tall depending on species, 8–10cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Organically rich, medium moisture, well-drained soils in full sun to part shade. Adapts to clay soils. Plants need consistent moisture during the spring period of growth and bloom. pH 5.5–7.0. They are notably tolerant of wet and clay soils.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost. Plants will naturalize well by bulb offsets. Bulbs are best left undisturbed once set in the ground. Mulch to retain summer moisture.

**Planting depth:** Plant dry bulbs 8–10cm deep in autumn in moist, humus-rich soil. Plant bulbs in autumn about 10cm deep and 8–10cm apart. Water well after planting and keep the soil evenly moist, especially during dry spells in spring. Allow the foliage to die back completely after flowering to feed the bulbs for next year's growth.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced bulb fertiliser in spring while foliage is green, or annual compost top-dressing around the planting area. Low-maintenance once established.

**Companions:** Mixes beautifully with other spring flowering bulbs. Tolerates boggy spots and will do well on the edges of natural ponds. Spectacular in large sweeping drifts. Excellent with daffodils, snowdrops, hellebores, and moisture-loving plants. Plant at pond edges where it will naturalise freely.

**Pot size:** 30–40cm diameter, 25cm+ deep; multipurpose compost.

**West of Ireland:** This attractive bulb is ideal for naturalising in areas of grass or planting in generous clumps throughout the front of borders and rockeries. The moist Irish conditions and winter-spring wet suit it perfectly. An excellent naturalistic bulb for damp areas that can be challenging for other bulbs.

---

### B23. Spring Sowbread *(Cyclamen coum)*


***Russian:*** *Цикламен косский (дряква косская)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Spring Sowbread — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclamen_coum)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade; ideal under deciduous trees and shrubs.

**Height/Spread:** 5–8cm tall, 10–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging around the tuber.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with leaf mould/compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** Tuber just below the soil surface (2–3cm), flattest side down, planted in late summer to early autumn (August–September) while dormant. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between tubers

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed as leaves emerge in autumn/winter. Top-dress with leaf mould annually.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, winter aconite, hellebores — classic winter/early-spring woodland-floor planting.

**West of Ireland:** Flowers from midwinter into early spring, often alongside snowdrops, with attractively marbled leaves persisting after flowering. Once settled it self-seeds gently to form a carpet under trees and shrubs — avoid disturbing the tubers once established.

---

### B24. Tulip *(Tulipa spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulip) · [Turkestan Tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_turkestanica) · [Whittall's Tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_orphanidea) · [Wild Tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_sylvestris) · ['Little Beauty'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_humilis) · [T. saxatilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_saxatilis) · [T. tarda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulipa_tarda)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Tulip (garden hybrids) | *Tulipa* spp. | 30–60cm | April–May | 20cm deep (3× bulb height) | The familiar large-flowered garden tulip; rots readily in wet clay |
| 'Little Beauty' | *T. humilis* 'Little Beauty' (syn. *T. hageri* 'Little Beauty') | 10–15cm | Mid-spring (April–early May) | 10–12cm deep | RHS AGM; fragrant cherry-red flowers with a cornflower-blue heart; a true species tulip that reliably perennialises |
| *T. saxatilis* (syn. *T. bakeri*) | *T. saxatilis* | 15–20cm | Mid-spring | 10–12cm deep | Lilac-pink flowers with a yellow base; the 'Lilac Wonder' form has an RHS AGM; spreads by stolons in a warm, sharply-drained spot |
| *T. tarda* | *T. tarda* | 10–15cm | April | 8–10cm deep | RHS AGM; up to five star-shaped yellow flowers with white tips per bulb; one of the most reliable species tulips for naturalising |
| Turkestan Tulip | *T. turkestanica* | 10–15cm | February–March | 10cm deep | Up to a dozen small white, yellow-centred star flowers per bulb; one of the earliest tulips |
| Whittall's Tulip | *T. orphanidea* | 15–20cm | Mid-spring | 10–15cm deep | Copper-orange star flowers, one to four per stem; reliably perennial |
| Wild Tulip | *T. sylvestris* | 20–30cm | Mid-spring | 10–15cm deep | Fragrant, nodding, buttercup-yellow; more shade- and damp-tolerant than the rest, spreads by stolons |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for all — except Wild Tulip, which tolerates partial shade as a woodland-edge species. 'Little Beauty' tolerates a little afternoon shade too.

**Height/Spread:** 10–60cm tall depending on species (see table), 8–15cm spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 6.0–7.5. Well-drained soil essential for garden hybrids and all the smaller species tulips ('Little Beauty', *saxatilis*, *tarda*, Turkestan, Whittall's) — all rot in wet winter clay and prefer to stay dry over summer dormancy. Wild Tulip is the one exception, tolerating damper, more clay-rich conditions than the others.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and 20–30% horticultural grit for garden hybrids and the smaller species tulips; raised beds or pots give the most reliable results on heavy west of Ireland ground. For Wild Tulip, heavy grit amendment is less critical.

**Planting depth:** Plant all in autumn (November is ideal for garden hybrids, to reduce tulip fire disease risk; the species tulips can go in a little earlier). Garden hybrids: 20cm deep (3× bulb height). 'Little Beauty': 10–12cm deep. *T. saxatilis*: 10–12cm deep. *T. tarda*: 8–10cm deep. Turkestan Tulip: 10cm deep. Whittall's Tulip: 10–15cm deep. Wild Tulip: 10–15cm deep. All pointed end up. The species tulips are fully hardy and never need lifting. Garden hybrid tulips are also hardy, but in Ireland's cool, wet climate many gardeners lift the bulbs after the foliage dies back in early summer, dry them off and replant in autumn, as this improves the reliability of repeat flowering more than leaving them in permanently damp ground; treating hybrids as annuals and replanting fresh bulbs each year is also common practice here.

**Spacing:** 8–10cm between bulbs for the species tulips; 10–15cm for garden hybrids.

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting for the species tulips; otherwise little required. Garden hybrids benefit from a high-potash bulb fertiliser in early spring as shoots emerge, and a balanced feed after flowering while the foliage is still green if being kept for a second year.

**Companions:** Garden hybrids: forget-me-nots, wallflowers, daffodils, alliums, stocks. 'Little Beauty', *T. saxatilis*, *T. tarda*: rockeries, gravel gardens and troughs alongside crocus, dwarf iris and sempervivum. Turkestan Tulip: crocus, dwarf iris and other very early bulbs. Whittall's Tulip: crocus, grape hyacinth and other species tulips. Wild Tulip: naturalises well in grass or light woodland with daffodils.

**West of Ireland:** The species tulips (including the three newer additions here) naturalise far more reliably here than showier garden hybrids, which are best treated as a shorter-lived display unless lifted and dried each summer. 'Little Beauty' and *T. tarda* in particular are among the easiest and most reliable species tulips to leave permanently in the ground, given the sharp drainage they need; *T. saxatilis* spreads readily once established in a warm, well-drained spot.

---

### B25. Winter Aconite *(Eranthis hyemalis)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Eranthis hyemalis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eranthis_hyemalis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade, ideally the dappled shade of deciduous trees.

**Height/Spread:** 5–15cm tall, 5–8cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Humus-rich, moderately fertile soil that doesn't dry out; pH 6.5–7.5. RHS notes it tolerates most soil types but does best in alkaline conditions — unusual among the woodland bulbs in this guide, most of which prefer neutral to acid ground.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; keep tubers slightly damp before planting, since dry tubers establish poorly.

**Planting depth:** 5cm deep, planted in early autumn while the soil is still warm; "in the green" (as growing plants just after flowering) is a more reliable alternative to dry tubers if available.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between tubers; self-seeds and naturalises into spreading carpets over the years.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed; a light bonemeal at planting is sufficient.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, crocus and other very early bulbs — classic companions for a layered winter-into-spring display.

**Warnings ⚠️:** All parts are toxic if ingested — a documented risk to dogs in particular.

**West of Ireland:** RHS AGM. One of the very earliest flowers of the year, pushing bright yellow, buttercup-like cup flowers through frozen ground in January–February, often before snowdrops. Fully hardy and naturalises readily in the mild, moist Irish climate once established, though it can be slow to settle in the first year or two — patience is rewarded with increasing carpets of gold each winter.

---

### B26. Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem *(Gagea lutea)*


***Russian:*** *Гусиный лук жёлтый*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Gagea — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gagea_lutea)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to sun; naturally a woodland-floor plant, flowering before the canopy closes.

**Height/Spread:** 5–15cm tall, 5cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, base-rich (near-neutral to slightly alkaline) soil; pH 6.5–7.5. Never allow it to dry out during the growing season.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost/leaf mould; avoid heavy grit amendment, since this species prefers more moisture-retentive ground than most bulbs.

**Planting depth:** 5–8cm deep, pointed end up, in autumn. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 5–8cm between bulbs

**Fertiliser:** Light bonemeal at planting. Otherwise little required; allow foliage to die back naturally.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, wood anemones and other early woodland-floor bulbs.

**West of Ireland:** A delicate, early-flowering native British woodland bulb; note that it is genuinely rare across Britain and Ireland and its status as a wild Irish native is uncertain — most sources describe it as a rare/scattered Great Britain species rather than confirmed native to Ireland, so plants sold for gardens are nursery-propagated stock rather than "native Irish" in origin. Grow in a shaded, undisturbed woodland-style corner.

---


---

## GROUP C: CLIMBERS & WALL PLANTS

### C1. Blue Passion Flower *(Passiflora caerulea)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus na páise · Страстоцвет голубой*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Passiflora (Blue Passion Flower) — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passiflora_caerulea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun, sheltered from cold wind — a south- or west-facing wall is ideal.

**Height/Spread:** 3–4m tall as a climber, 1–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most well-drained soils including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Good drainage is essential — this is the hardiest passion flower but still dislikes waterlogged roots in winter.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width against the wall base. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark, 30–45cm from the wall, angled towards support.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants against wall/fence

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general or high-potash liquid feed monthly through the growing season. Avoid excess nitrogen, which favours leaf growth over flowers.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, clematis and honeysuckle on the same wall/structure for a long season of colour.

**West of Ireland:** RHS hardiness H4 (hardy to about −5 to −10°C) — reliably hardy in most west of Ireland gardens with wall shelter, though may be cut back by hard frost and regrow from the base. Needs a sturdy support (trellis or wires) to climb by tendrils.

---

### C2. Chocolate Vine *(Akebia quinata)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Akebia quinata — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akebia_quinata)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 3–5m tall as a climber, 1–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Very adaptable.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost; not fussy about soil type once drainage is reasonable.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 3–5m between plants against a sturdy support — this is a genuinely vigorous vine.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; otherwise little needed.

**Companions:** Best given a large, robust structure of its own — a strong pergola, arch or substantial fence — rather than sharing a modest trellis with more restrained climbers.
