**Pruning:** Prune hard after flowering as needed to keep within bounds; tolerates hard cutting back well.

**Warnings ⚠️:** RHS AGM winner, but extremely vigorous — sources cite growth of 6–12m. Needs a sturdy structure and at least one hard prune a year to stay controlled; can self-seed and naturalise in mild, sheltered gardens.

**West of Ireland:** A semi-evergreen to deciduous twining climber with unusual spicy, chocolate-scented, deep purple-brown flowers in spring, followed occasionally by sausage-shaped purple fruit in a good summer. Tolerates the mild, moist west of Ireland climate well in sun or part shade, but its vigour means it needs a genuinely strong structure and regular management — not a plant for a delicate garden trellis expected to also support other climbers.

---

### C3. Clematis *(Clematis spp.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Gabhrán · Клематис*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Clematis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clematis)

**Light:** 🌤️ Roots prefer shade (cover with stone or tile), stems flower best in sun or dappled light. Evergreen types prefer sheltered, sunny, south-facing wall.

**Height/Spread:** 2.5–10m tall depending on species (see table), 1–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile, neutral to slightly alkaline, pH 6.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost plus 20% grit. Shade the root zone.

**Planting depth:** Groups 2 & 3 large-flowered: 10–15cm DEEPER than pot; C. montana (Group 1): 8–10cm; Evergreen types: at pot level only.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between; cool roots essential

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release fertiliser in early spring. From April: high-potash liquid feed (tomato feed) fortnightly until August. Feed regularly — clematis are hungry. Do not feed after August.

**Companions:** Climbing roses on the same wall (classic combination). Clematis through other shrubs. Late-flowering Group 3 through roses for second flush of colour.

**Species for the west of Ireland:** All of the following are hardy and well suited to the Atlantic climate; pruning group is noted for each.

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *C. armandii* | *C. armandii* | 5–9m | Early–mid spring (March–April) | At pot level (evergreen type) | Evergreen, glossy leathery leaves; fragrant white (or pink in 'Apple Blossom') flowers; vigorous, needs a warm, sheltered wall for best flowering |
| *C. montana* | *C. montana* | 8–10m | Late spring | 8–10cm deep (Group 1) | The most vigorous of the nine, covering itself in single, four-petalled white or pink flowers; best given a large wall, tree or shed to scramble over |
| *C. alpina* | *C. alpina* | 2–3m | April | 8–10cm deep (Group 1) | Compact, with nodding, lantern-shaped single flowers (four narrow sepals) in blue, purple, pink or white; good for a windy or north-facing spot |
| *C. macropetala* | *C. macropetala* | 2–3m | Mid–late spring | 8–10cm deep (Group 1) | Very similar to *C. alpina* in size and season, but the flowers look "double": extra narrow petal-like staminodes fill the centre |
| *C. viticella* | *C. viticella* | 3–4m | Midsummer–autumn | 10–15cm deeper than pot (Group 3) | Small, nodding, open bell-shaped flowers in purple, red or pink; naturally resistant to clematis wilt, a reliable choice on damp Irish sites |
| *C. tangutica* | *C. tangutica* | 3.5–4.5m | Late summer–early autumn | 10–15cm deeper than pot (Group 3) | Bright yellow, nodding lantern-shaped flowers followed by large, silvery, fluffy seedheads that persist into winter |
| *C. terniflora* | *C. terniflora* | 3–6m | Late summer–autumn | 10–15cm deeper than pot (Group 3) | The latest to flower, with small, star-shaped, fragrant white flowers in large sprays; extremely vigorous, so give it plenty of room |
| *C. cirrhosa* ('Freckles' / 'Wisley Cream') | *C. cirrhosa* | 2.5–4m | Winter (November–February) | At pot level (evergreen type) | Evergreen, fern-like foliage; nodding, bell-shaped flowers — 'Freckles' speckled maroon on cream, 'Wisley Cream' plain cream; wants a sunny, sheltered site for best flowering |
| *C.* 'Winter Beauty' | *C. urophylla* 'Winter Beauty' | About 3m | Early–late winter | At pot level (evergreen type) | Evergreen; nodding, urn-shaped, creamy-white flowers to 4cm across from pale green buds, produced right through winter; ⚠️ sap is a skin irritant |

---

### C4. Climbing Honeysuckle *(Lonicera periclymenum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Féithleann · Жимолость вьющаяся*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Climbing Honeysuckle — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lonicera_periclymenum)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Woodland-edge habit: roots shaded, stems growing into light.

**Height/Spread:** 4–7m tall as a climber, 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils; prefers moist, humus-rich.

**Soil prep:** Hole the same size as rootball. Mix in compost. Firm in and mulch.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser in spring (Growmore) or manure mulch. Liquid tomato feed fortnightly in summer encourages flowering. Avoid excessive nitrogen.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, clematis (classic combination). Plant near windows or doorways for evening fragrance. Native woodbine is a superb wildlife plant.

---

### C5. Climbing Hydrangea *(Hydrangea anomala subsp. petiolaris)*


***Russian:*** *Гортензия черешковая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Climbing Hydrangea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrangea_petiolaris)

**Light:** 🌑 to 🌤️ North or east-facing walls are ideal — will grow and flower in shade that defeats most climbers. Full sun tolerated if soil stays consistently moist. Self-clinging via aerial roots; slow to start but ultimately vigorous (can reach 10–15m). RHS hardiness rating: H5 (to -15°C).

**Height/Spread:** 8–15m tall as a climber, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil, pH 6.0–7.0. Tolerates clay, loamy and sandy soils. Dislikes waterlogged or excessively dry conditions.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice as wide and the same depth as the rootball. Mix in compost. If against a wall or fence, allow a 45cm gap between plant and wall (rain shadow). Mulch well. Space 60cm+ between plants.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Can tolerate slightly deeper planting as it forms shoot-like roots on buried stem.

**Spacing:** 45cm from wall; 60cm+ between plants

**Fertiliser:** Needs little feeding for the first three years — do not feed during establishment. After year three: balanced slow-release fertiliser in early spring if leaves appear yellow; otherwise a single spring application of balanced granular feed is sufficient. Annual mulch with compost in late summer. Low-maintenance overall.

**Companions:** Grows beautifully with ivy on north-facing walls for year-round coverage. Ferns at its base (same moisture preference). Shade-tolerant hostas and hellebores beneath. Invaluable for dark walls where most climbers fail.

**West of Ireland:** Exceptional plant for the Irish climate. One of the very few climbers that genuinely thrives on cold, north-facing or shaded walls. The moist Atlantic climate suits it perfectly. Slow in the first 2–3 years but can ultimately cover an entire house wall.

---

### C6. Climbing Roses *(Rosa, climbing cultivars)*


***Russian:*** *Плетистая роза*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Climbing Roses — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climbing_rose)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun preferred — at least 6 hours.

**Height/Spread:** 2–5m tall depending on variety, 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, well-drained, pH 6.0–7.0. Never plant where a rose previously grew.

**Soil prep:** Double-dig (50–60cm deep). Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit. Position at least 45cm from wall.

**Planting depth:** Graft union just below soil level.

**Spacing:** 45cm from wall; 2–3m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Specialist rose fertiliser in late March and again in early June. Liquid high-potash feed fortnightly May–August. Stop feeding by late August. Annual bark or manure mulch.

**Companions:** Clematis (trained together on the same wall). Lavender at base. Alliums, salvia, hardy geraniums as foreground.

---

### C7. Common Jasmine *(Jasminum officinale)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Jasminum officinale — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasminum_officinale)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade, ideally against a warm, sheltered wall.

**Height/Spread:** 5–9m tall as a climber, 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit; provide a sturdy trellis or wires for its twining stems.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark, 30–45cm out from the wall or fence base.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants against a wall or pergola.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring; high-potash liquid feed through summer to encourage flowering.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, clematis and honeysuckle on the same wall or pergola for a long season of scent and colour.

**Pruning:** Prune immediately after flowering, thinning out crowded and dead growth; flowers on the current year's growth, so avoid hard pruning in late winter/spring, which removes the coming season's flowers.

**West of Ireland:** A vigorous, twining deciduous-to-semi-evergreen climber grown above all for its powerfully fragrant white summer flowers, beloved for training over an archway, pergola or seating area where its scent can be appreciated. Fully hardy in the mild, moist west of Ireland climate, though a sheltered spot gives the healthiest growth and the best flowering.

---

### C8. Hardy Grapes *(Vitis vinifera)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Fíonchaora · Виноград культурный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hardy Grapes — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitis_vinifera)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — south-facing wall essential. Without maximum sun, fruit will not ripen.

**Height/Spread:** 6–9m tall as a climber, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, pH 6.0–6.5. Neutral to slightly alkaline preferred; mulch to keep soil moist.

**Soil prep:** Clay/compost/grit plus coarse gravel at base. At least 20cm from wall.

**Planting depth:** At pot depth; graft above soil.

**Spacing:** 1.2–1.5m between vines; 20cm from wall

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser with potassium in early spring. High-potash liquid feed fortnightly through summer. Do not overfeed with nitrogen. Grapevines on somewhat lean soil often fruit better.

**Companions:** Lavender, rosemary, garlic, hyssop at base. Good air circulation is critical in Irish conditions to reduce fungal disease risk.

**Your varieties:**

**'Phoenix'** — A mid-season white dessert and wine grape with large bunches of pale green fruits. Bred in Germany; its most important attribute for west of Ireland growing is its excellent built-in resistance to both powdery mildew and downy mildew, making it one of the most reliable outdoor grapes in the UK and Ireland. Hardy and very productive. Harvest: mid-September. One of the very best choices for Irish conditions (source: RHS outdoor cultivation guide; Pomona Fruits; Roots Plants).

**'Regent'** — A vigorous, disease-resistant red/blue-black dual-purpose grape for wine-making, juice or dessert eating. Originally bred in Germany (Chambourcin × Müller-Thurgau parentage). Bunches of large, dark red grapes mature to blue-black in early autumn; foliage turns red and purple in autumn. Excellent resistance to fungal diseases including downy mildew; slightly less resistant to mildew in unusually humid seasons. Hardy to northern European conditions. Harvest: September–October depending on season. RHS recommends growing in full sun in well-drained, humus-rich, neutral to slightly alkaline soil (RHS plant profile, Vitis 'Regent').

**'Boskoop Glory'** (also sold as 'Gloire of Boskoop') — A very popular and reliable outdoor dessert and wine grape developed in the Netherlands in the 1950s; a hybrid between *V. vinifera* and *V. labrusca*. Vigorous, disease-resistant and very cold-tolerant — one of the hardiest outdoor grape varieties available, RHS Award of Garden Merit holder. Large bunches of sweet, blue-black, seedless grapes ripen in early autumn (typically late August–September). Suitable for outdoor cultivation almost anywhere in the UK and Ireland. Leaves turn shades of red and purple in autumn. Can grow to an impressive 10m high and 4m wide. ⚠️ Harmful to dogs if eaten. (Source: RHS plant profile Vitis 'Boskoop Glory'; Wikipedia; Roots Plants; Marshalls Garden.)

---

### C9. Hops *(Humulus lupulus)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus an leanna · Хмель обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hops — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humulus_lupulus)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Full sun produces more vigorous growth and larger cones.

**Height/Spread:** 6m tall as a climber, 1–1.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Rich, moist, well-drained, pH 6.0–8.0. Tolerates clay well.

**Soil prep:** Dig and enrich generously with compost. Heavy feeders.

**Planting depth:** Rhizome 5–8cm deep; pot-grown plants at pot level.

**Spacing:** 1–1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** High-nitrogen fertiliser in spring, then balanced fertiliser with potassium once flowering begins. Thick compost or manure mulch each spring. Container: balanced liquid feed monthly throughout the growing season.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, clematis, ornamental grasses. Golden hop ('Aureus') looks spectacular against dark-foliaged plants (yew, holly).

---

### C10. Ivy *(Hedera helix / H. hibernica — native Irish ivy)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Eidhneán · Плющ обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Ivy — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedera_helix)

**Light:** 🌑 Full shade to ☀️ full sun. One of the most light-tolerant plants. In deep shade variegated forms lose markings; green forms thrive.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20m tall as a climber (self-clinging), 3–5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Almost any soil including heavy clay, pH 5.0–8.0.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — basic compost addition to planting hole.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 30–50cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Little required. Balanced slow-release in spring if plant appears weak. Annual bark mulch. Container: balanced liquid fertiliser monthly during the growing season.

**Companions:** Native *H. hibernica* is one of Ireland's most important wildlife plants — nesting habitat, late nectar, winter berries. Grows beautifully under trees alongside ferns, snowdrops, and bluebells. With Virginia creeper on walls for year-round interest.

---

### C11. Kiwi 'Jenny' *(Actinidia deliciosa 'Jenny')*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Kiwi — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Actinidia_deliciosa)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to partial shade. Best fruiting in full sun; a south- or west-facing wall gives the most reliable cropping in Ireland.

**Height/Spread:** 4–9m tall as a climber, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil, pH 6.0–7.0. Sandy loam is ideal; dislikes waterlogging, so on clay, drainage improvement is essential.

**Soil prep:** Dig a generous hole at least 60cm wide and 45cm deep, 45cm out from the wall or fence. Mix the excavated clay 50/50 with multipurpose compost and 20–25% horticultural grit to guarantee free drainage at the root zone. Install a strong permanent support (trellis, wires or pergola) at planting time — the vine is vigorous and support is far harder to add later. Mulch generously with bark after planting to retain moisture and suppress weeds.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; do not bury the crown. Plant in spring once frost risk has passed (April–May).

**Spacing:** 3–5m between plants, trained against a wall or over a sturdy pergola.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced granular fertiliser (e.g. Growmore) in early spring as buds swell, then a high-potash liquid feed (tomato feed) fortnightly from May to August. Do not feed after August. Mulch annually with well-rotted compost each spring.

**Companions:** Climbing roses, wisteria or clematis on adjacent structures. Lavender and rosemary at the base suit its preference for a warm, sheltered spot; garlic or chives nearby help deter aphids.

**Pruning:** Prune in late winter to establish a framework, then again in summer to control vigorous growth. Fruit forms on the current season's lateral shoots — shorten these to 4–6 leaves beyond the last fruit cluster each summer.

**Hardiness & pot growing:** Hardy to about -10°C to -15°C once established; young plants need frost protection over their first winter. ⚠️ Can be grown in a large container (60–80L minimum) but fruits far less freely than in the ground.

**West of Ireland:** 'Jenny' is self-fertile, removing the need for a second pollinator plant, which makes it the practical choice for most gardens here. Kiwi is at the northern edge of its reliable fruiting range in Ireland, so a sheltered, sun-warmed wall is essential and a modest crop is realistic rather than a heavy one.

---

### C12. Necklace Vine (Maidenhair Vine) *(Muehlenbeckia complexa)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Muehlenbeckia complexa — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muehlenbeckia_complexa)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 3–4m tall (or as groundcover mounds), 1–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most well-drained soils, including poor or dry ground; pH 5.5–7.5. Very adaptable.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — tolerant of poor soil; improve heavy waterlogged clay with grit.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 1–1.5m between plants against a support, or as ground-covering mounds if left unsupported.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed once established.

**Companions:** Works well trained over a wire frame or topiary form, or left to scramble as groundcover among rocks; combines with ornamental grasses and other textural foliage plants.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Can self-seed and spread vigorously in mild, sheltered gardens — monitor and control if this is unwanted, particularly near hedgerows or wild areas.

**West of Ireland:** A wiry-stemmed, small-leaved evergreen (semi-evergreen in colder spots) climber or scrambling groundcover from New Zealand, useful for softening fences, trained over topiary frames, or as dense low cover on a bank. Tolerates coastal exposure and salt-laden wind well, making it a useful, tough choice for an exposed west of Ireland garden, though its spreading habit needs occasional control.

---

### C13. Schisandra *(Schisandra spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Schisandra chinensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schisandra_chinensis) · [Schisandra rubriflora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schisandra_rubriflora)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Five-Flavour Berry (Magnolia Vine) | *S. chinensis* | 6–9m | Late spring (April–May) | At nursery soil mark | Fragrant cream to pale-pink flowers; hanging clusters of red "five-flavour" berries on female plants; RHS-confirmed hardy |
| Red-Flowered Magnolia Vine | *S. rubriflora* | 6–9m | Late spring–early summer | At nursery soil mark | Deep crimson, waxy, cup-shaped flowers — showier than *S. chinensis*; pea-sized red berries on dangling spikes; hardy to about -15°C |

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial or dappled shade is preferred by both, though *S. chinensis* also tolerates full sun provided the soil stays consistently moist. *S. rubriflora* is the more shade-loving of the two and does well against a shady wall or trained up through a tree.

**Height/Spread:** 6–9m tall as a climber, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, humus-rich, moist but well-drained soil for both; pH 6.0–7.5, tolerant of light, medium and heavy soils including clay.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with well-rotted compost; mulch well after planting to help retain the consistent moisture both species need — they resent drying out.

**Planting depth:** At the same depth as in the nursery pot for both. Plant climbers 30–60cm out from a wall or fence base to avoid the driest soil directly beneath, angling the young plant towards its support.

**Spacing:** Allow at least 2–3m for a mature plant of either species against a wall, trellis or through a large shrub or tree.

**Fertiliser:** Feed lightly in spring with a balanced fertiliser to support healthy growth and flowering; an annual mulch of garden compost is otherwise sufficient.

**Companions:** Both are well suited to growing through a large, sturdy shrub or small tree, or trained on a shady wall alongside climbing hydrangea or ivy.

**Pruning:** Tie in young shoots to their support until established. Both flower and fruit on the current season's growth, so prune lightly immediately after flowering to control shape; avoid hard pruning, and remove only damaged, weak or overcrowded stems.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Both species are dioecious (separate male and female plants) — for berries, plant at least one of each sex reasonably close together, since a single plant grown alone will flower but will not fruit.

**West of Ireland:** Both are genuinely hardy woody twining climbers native to the forests of China and neighbouring parts of Asia, well suited to a sheltered spot in an Irish garden. *S. chinensis* ("wu wei zi", the five-flavour berry, so called because its fruit combines sweet, sour, salty, bitter and pungent tastes) is the more commonly available and more sun-tolerant of the two, while *S. rubriflora* has showier, richer crimson flowers and prefers more shade — ideal for a north- or east-facing wall or growing up through an established tree where little else will flower well. Both give attractive yellow autumn colour before leaf fall.

---

### C14. Tufted Vetch *(Vicia cracca)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Peasair na luch · Горошек мышиный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Vicia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicia_cracca)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 1–2m tall (scrambling), 1m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant and unfussy, as a nitrogen-fixing legume.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — very adaptable. Improve heaviest clay with compost if desired.

**Planting depth:** At pot level, or sow seed directly 1–2cm deep in spring.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; scrambles via tendrils

**Fertiliser:** None required — like other vetches, it fixes its own nitrogen. Avoid rich feeding, which encourages excess soft growth.

**Companions:** Grasses, other wildflower meadow and hedgerow species; scrambles happily through shrubs or a wildlife-friendly fence/trellis via tendrils.

**West of Ireland:** A native climbing/scrambling wildflower with clusters of blue-purple pea flowers, valuable for bees and other pollinators. Best suited to a wildflower area, informal hedge or meadow planting rather than a formal border, as it can spread and scramble vigorously.

---

### C15. Virginia Creeper *(Parthenocissus quinquefolia)*


***Russian:*** *Виноград девичий пятилисточковый*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Virginia Creeper — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenocissus_quinquefolia)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Autumn colour more vivid in sunnier positions.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20m tall as a climber, 3–5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Wide range including clay, pH 5.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig twice the rootball, backfill with clay/compost mix.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 1.5–3m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Low-maintenance. Balanced general fertiliser in spring. Annual bark mulch. In poor soils, slow-release granular fertiliser provides sufficient nutrition for the season.

**Companions:** Ivy on same wall for year-round coverage. Late-flowering clematis. Good companion for climbing roses (extends season of interest).

---

### C16. Wisteria *(Wisteria sinensis / W. floribunda)*


***Russian:*** *Глициния китайская*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Wisteria — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisteria)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — south- or west-facing wall essential. Flowers very poorly in shade.

**Height/Spread:** 9–18m tall as a climber, 2–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile, pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** 60cm wide, 40cm deep, at least 45cm from wall base. Clay/compost/grit blend over a wide area.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; graft union above soil. Buy grafted plants — seed-grown can take 15–20 years to flower.

**Spacing:** 45cm from wall; 3m between plants

**Fertiliser:** High-potash fertiliser (tomato feed) from spring through early summer — avoid nitrogen (produces leaves, not flowers). Light dressing of bonemeal in spring. Do not feed after midsummer.

**Companions:** Climbing roses on adjacent wall, alliums and irises at its feet, spring bulbs at the base. Do not plant near drains or gutters.

---


---

## GROUP F: FRUIT TREES & SHRUBS

### F1. Almond *(Prunus dulcis)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Prunus dulcis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_dulcis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, in the warmest, most sheltered spot available.

**Height/Spread:** 4–6m tall (free-standing), 4–5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole twice the rootball width. Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark; graft union above soil level.

**Spacing:** 4–6m between plants if free-standing; fan-trained against a wall needs 4–5m of wall space.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser each February; potash-rich feed as nuts develop.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic nearby to deter aphids; spring bulbs beneath, timed with the very early blossom.

**Pruning:** Prune in summer only, to reduce the risk of silver leaf and bacterial canker common to Prunus species.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Flowers extremely early (often February), so blossom is highly vulnerable to frost damage — this is the single biggest obstacle to a reliable nut crop in a cool climate.

**West of Ireland:** Grown more for its beautiful very early pink-white blossom than for a reliable nut harvest in this climate — most self-fertile ornamental almonds flower before pollinating insects are reliably active, and the region's damp, cool spring rarely gives the sustained summer warmth almonds need to ripen a worthwhile crop of nuts. Best treated as a beautiful, hardy flowering tree in a warm, sheltered, south-facing spot, with any nuts a welcome bonus rather than the main reason to grow it. Self-fertile cultivars remove the need for a pollination partner, but a sheltered site is essential regardless.

---

### F2. Apple *(Malus domestica)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Crann úll · Яблоня домашняя*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Malus domestica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malus_domestica)

**Popular garden varieties — UK/Ireland:**

| Variety | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 'Baya Marisa' | September–October | Compact, disease-resistant modern variety bred specifically for pots and small gardens; usually sold on P22 rootstock |
| Cox's Orange Pippin | October | The classic English dessert apple; complex, aromatic flavour; needs a warm, sheltered site and a pollination partner |
| 'Granny Smith' | Late season | Crisp, tart, green-skinned; needs a long, warm growing season to ripen fully, so cropping is less reliable in a cool Irish summer than the varieties above |

**Popular garden varieties — Russia:**

| Variety | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| White Transparent (Белый налив, Bely/Beliy Naliv) | Mid–late summer | One of the most famous and widely grown Russian apples; very early ripening, extremely hardy, sharp/tart flavour best suited to cooking; also grown across Poland and the Baltic states |
| Antonovka (Антоновка) | Autumn/keeper | "The people's apple" — Russia's classic dacha-garden variety; strongly acidic, aromatic; exceptionally cold-hardy and long-storing; also widely used as a cold-hardy seedling rootstock |
| Grushovka Moskovskaya (Грушовка московская) | Mid-summer | An old, very early-ripening Russian variety, sweet-sour flavour, extremely frost-tolerant (to about -30°C); needs a pollination partner |

**Apple rootstocks:**

| Rootstock | Vigour | Approx. height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| M27 | Extremely dwarfing | 1.2–1.8m | For a container or the very smallest garden; needs permanent staking and good soil |
| M9 | Dwarfing | 1.8–2.4m | The most popular rootstock for a garden bush tree; needs permanent staking |
| P22 | Very dwarfing | 1.2–1.8m | Polish rootstock (M.9 × Common Antonovka cross); good winter hardiness and good resistance to root rots, a real advantage on wet Irish clay; needs permanent staking for life |
| M26 | Dwarfing | 2.4–3m | A popular choice for a slightly larger bush; usually needs staking for its first years |
| MM106 | Semi-vigorous | 3–4m | A good general-purpose choice for a free-standing garden tree; does not usually need permanent staking once established |
| M25 | Vigorous | Over 4.5m | For a full-sized standard tree; not suitable for a small garden |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential — at least 6 hours daily.

**Height/Spread:** 1.2m–4.5m+ tall depending on rootstock (see table), similar spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Dig wider and deeper than the root system. Break up clay beneath. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with multipurpose compost over a broad area. Keep a 60cm weed-free circle around the base permanently.

**Planting depth:** Graft union (bulge near stem base) at or just above soil level. Plant to the nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** Depends entirely on rootstock — see table above.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser (Growmore or specialist fruit feed) each February. Liquid high-potash feed (tomato feed) fortnightly from late spring to midsummer. Do not feed after July. Bonemeal in the planting hole at planting.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic deter aphids (dwarfing rootstocks like P22 are particularly susceptible to woolly apple aphid). Nasturtiums attract beneficial insects. Most apples need a compatible pollination partner flowering at the same time — plant two or more varieties, or rely on neighbouring gardens/wild crab apple, for a reliable crop.

**West of Ireland:** Permanent staking is essential on any of the dwarfing rootstocks (M27, M9, P22, M26) given Atlantic winds. 'Baya Marisa' on P22 is a good, compact, disease-resistant choice for a small garden or large pot; Cox's Orange Pippin rewards a warm, sheltered spot with real flavour but is fussier about site than the others; the Russian varieties (White Transparent, Antonovka, Grushovka Moskovskaya) are all exceptionally cold-hardy and were bred for climates considerably harsher than Ireland's, so they generally thrive here with no special protection needed, though most need a pollination partner. 'Granny Smith' is the least reliable cropper of the group in a cool Irish summer, since it evolved for a warmer climate with a longer ripening season.

---

### F3. Blackberries *(Rubus fruticosus agg.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sméara dubha · Ежевика кустистая*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Black Satin](https://www.gardens4you.ie/blackberry-black-satin-a10966.html) · [Navaho Thornless](https://www.gardens4you.ie/blackberry-navaho-thornless-a10968.html) · [Chester Thornless](https://www.gardens4you.ie/blackberry-chester-thornless-a10967.html)

| Cultivar | Habit | Height/Spread | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Black Satin | Thornless, vigorous, semi-erect | 1.25–1.5m / 80cm–1m | Self-fertile; deep blue-black berries, mid–late season; resistant to cane spot; white spring flowers |
| Navaho Thornless | Thornless, upright, moderately vigorous | 1.5–2m / 1–1.2m | Compact and self-supporting, needing little to no staking; very sweet berries when fully ripened; good disease resistance |
| Chester Thornless | Thornless, semi-erect, vigorous | 1.8–2.5m / 1.2–1.8m | Late-season; very high yields; good resistance to cane blight and sunscald; one of the hardiest thornless varieties |
| Loch Ness | Thornless, compact, upright | 1.5–1.8m / 1–1.2m | RHS AGM; bred in Scotland, well suited to Irish and UK conditions; very heavy cropping; large, firm fruit; mid–late season |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade for best fruiting.

**Height/Spread:** 1.8–3m tall (trained on wires), 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most moist, well-drained soils including clay; pH 5.5–7.0. One of the most clay-tolerant fruiting plants.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants against wires

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser (Growmore) in spring. Annual mulch with compost.

**Companions:** Raspberries and other cane fruit with similar needs; avoid planting near wild bramble, which can cross-pollinate and revert vigour.

**West of Ireland:** Extremely easy and productive on Irish clay — thornless cultivars (e.g. 'Loch Ness', 'Oregon Thornless') are more manageable than wild bramble for a garden setting. Provide a sturdy wire support and tie in new canes each summer; cut back fruited canes after harvest.

---

### F4. Blackcurrant *(Ribes nigrum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cuiríní dubha · Смородина чёрная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Blackcurrant — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackcurrant)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade — tolerates shade better than other currants.

**Height/Spread:** 1.2–1.5m tall, 1.2–1.5m spread.

**Soil prep:** Double dig; compost generously; 15–20% grit on heavy clay.

**Planting depth:** 5–7cm deeper than nursery mark. Cut all stems to 10cm after planting.

**Spacing:** 1.2–1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** High-nitrogen fertiliser in early spring. Again after harvest. Generous compost or manure mulch each winter. Potassium deficiency (brown leaf edges) common — use balanced feed with potassium.

**Companions:** Alliums, garlic, nasturtiums nearby; redcurrant, gooseberry in the same bed.

---

### F5. Blueberry *(Vaccinium corymbosum)*


***Russian:*** *Голубика высокорослая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Blueberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccinium_corymbosum)

**Popular garden varieties:**

| Variety | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 'Bluecrop' | Mid-season | The world's most widely planted blueberry; large, sweet, powder-blue berries; very hardy; reliable all-rounder |
| 'Duke' | Early | RHS-listed; flowers late (avoiding frost) but ripens early; firm, tangy-sweet berries |
| 'Patriot' | Early | RHS AGM; large, sweet, firm berries; vigorous and hardy, with good tolerance of less-than-ideal soil |
| 'Sunshine Blue' | Mid-season | Compact (to about 1m), semi-evergreen; the most reliably self-fertile variety if only growing one bush; ideal for a container |

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. More berries in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 1–1.8m tall depending on variety, 1–1.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Ericaceous, pH 4.0–5.5. Use ericaceous compost only. Rainwater preferred for watering.

**Pot size:** Start in 40cm diameter pot; scale up every 2–3 years. Final container: minimum 50 litres (~60cm diameter, 45–60cm deep). One blueberry per pot. Heather and lingonberry compatible at edges of a wide trough (same pH).

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 1 plant per pot; 60cm between pots

**Fertiliser:** Use only ericaceous liquid fertiliser fortnightly April–August. Top-dress with fresh ericaceous compost in spring. Sulphur chips to maintain acidity over time.

**Companions:** Heather, lingonberry, cranberry, other Vaccinium. Dedicated ericaceous bed with rhododendrons and azaleas if naturally acidic area. Most varieties are only partially self-fertile — planting two different varieties nearby significantly improves berry size and yield, even though a single bush of 'Sunshine Blue' will crop well alone.

---

### F6. Cranberry *(Vaccinium macrocarpon)*


***Russian:*** *Клюква крупноплодная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Cranberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccinium_macrocarpon)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Best fruiting in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20cm tall (trailing groundcover), 60–90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Highly acidic, pH 4.0–5.0. Bog plant — prefers consistently moist, peaty, acidic conditions. Must be grown in ericaceous compost container or purpose-made acidic bog bed.

**Pot size:** 30–45cm wide, 20–30cm deep container; 100% ericaceous compost. Keep consistently moist. Do not add grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; shallow-rooting.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm in acidic bed

**Fertiliser:** Ericaceous liquid fertiliser monthly April–August. Do not use general-purpose fertilisers. Adapted to nutrient-poor conditions — do not overfeed.

**Companions:** Heather, lingonberry, blueberry (all share ericaceous requirements). Low carpet beneath taller blueberry bushes.

**West of Ireland:** The moist, mild Atlantic climate is naturally ideal for cranberry — but only if you provide or replicate acidic bog soil. Container with ericaceous compost is most practical.

---

### F7. Fig *(Ficus carica)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Ficus carica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficus_carica)

**Popular hardy varieties:**

| Variety | Notes |
|---|---|
| 'Brown Turkey' | RHS AGM; the most popular and reliable variety for outdoor UK/Ireland growing; large, sweet, red-fleshed fruit; hardy to about -10°C |
| 'Brunswick' | Very hardy, suited to cooler areas; large fruit, pink-yellow flesh, ripens a little earlier than 'Brown Turkey' |
| 'White Marseilles' | Compact, self-fertile; pale, very sweet fruit |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, ideally against a warm, south- or west-facing wall.

**Height/Spread:** 3–4m tall (roots restricted), 3–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Figs actually crop best when their roots are restricted — overly rich, deep soil produces lush leaves at the expense of fruit.

**Soil prep:** If planting in the ground, restrict the roots using paving slabs or a buried bottomless container, packed with rubble at the base — this is essential for reliable fruiting, not optional. In a pot, use a loam-based compost such as John Innes No. 3 with added grit.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 3–4m if free-standing in the ground with roots restricted; large containers (35–45cm diameter) suit patio growing well.

**Fertiliser:** Weekly liquid tomato feed once the fruit has formed; annual mulch.

**Companions:** Best given its own space, since it casts fairly dense shade once mature.

**Pruning:** Prune in late winter/early spring to shape, and pinch out growing tips in early summer to encourage the embryonic fruit that will overwinter and ripen the following year.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Figs grow very well in a large pot, which naturally restricts the roots and encourages fruiting — often the easiest way to grow a productive fig in a cooler climate.

**West of Ireland:** In a temperate climate like Ireland's, figs typically produce two flushes of embryonic fruit a year: the young "embryo" figs that form in late summer survive winter on the bare branches and ripen the following year, while a second flush that forms in spring rarely has time to ripen outdoors and is best removed at the end of the season if larger than a pea. A warm, sheltered wall out of the worst of the Atlantic wind and rain gives the best results; all three varieties above are reliably hardy and self-fertile, needing no pollination partner (indeed no pollinating wasp, unlike wild figs).

---

### F8. Garden Plum *(Prunus domestica)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Crann plumaí · Слива домашняя*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Garden Plum — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for best fruiting — at least 6 hours daily, sheltered from wind.

**Height/Spread:** 3–5m tall depending on rootstock, 2.5–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, moderately fertile soil, pH 6.0–7.0. Dislikes waterlogged clay — good drainage is essential.

**Soil prep:** Dig wider and deeper than the root system. Break up clay beneath. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit over a broad area. Keep a weed-free circle around the base.

**Planting depth:** Graft union (bulge near stem base) at or just above soil level. Plant to the nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 3–5m between trees depending on rootstock; permanent stake

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser (Growmore or specialist fruit feed) each February. Do not feed after midsummer, to allow wood to ripen before winter. Potash feed helps fruiting.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic to deter aphids. Comfrey or nasturtiums nearby to attract beneficial insects. Many plums (though not all) need a compatible pollination partner flowering at the same time — check the variety before buying.

**Popular hardy cultivars:** 'Victoria' — RHS AGM, self-fertile, the best-known English plum, dual-purpose (eating and cooking), late August–early September, though can crop unreliably some years. 'Czar' — RHS AGM, self-fertile, compact and very reliable, a traditional culinary plum that will even fruit on a north-facing wall, early August. 'Opal' — RHS AGM, self-fertile, early dessert plum with good flavour, very easy to grow, early August.

**West of Ireland:** Permanent staking recommended against Atlantic winds, especially on dwarfing rootstocks. Choose a sheltered, sunny spot away from frost pockets — plum blossoms early and late frosts can damage the crop. 'Czar' is a particularly good choice for a cooler, more exposed west of Ireland garden given its reliability and tolerance of less-than-ideal conditions.

---

### F9. Gooseberry *(Ribes uva-crispa)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Spíonán · Крыжовник обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Gooseberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gooseberry)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Tolerates semi-shade better than most fruit.

**Height/Spread:** 1.2–1.5m tall, 1.2–1.5m spread.

**Soil prep:** Compost and grit amendment. Bonemeal at planting.

**Planting depth:** At nursery mark; roots covered ~5cm. Cut all stems to 30cm after planting.

**Spacing:** 1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Sulphate of potash in late winter/early spring. Balanced fertiliser after flowering. Annual compost mulch. Avoid excessive nitrogen.

**Companions:** Redcurrants, whitecurrants. Tansy nearby reportedly repels gooseberry sawfly.

---

### F10. Green Strawberry *(Fragaria viridis)*


***Russian:*** *Клубника луговая (клубника степная, клубника лесная, земляника зелёная)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Strawberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for maximum fruiting.

**Height/Spread:** 20–30cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 5.5–6.8, well-drained. Raised bed on clay strongly recommended.

**Soil prep:** Compost and grit. Raised bed 20–25cm high.

**Planting depth:** Crown exactly at soil level — too deep = crown rot; too shallow = roots dry out.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants (or 45cm × 75cm rows if in open ground)

**Fertiliser:** High-potash fertiliser (tomato feed) fortnightly from first flowers until fruiting ceases. Balanced granular fertiliser in early spring. Do not use high-nitrogen fertiliser.

**Companions:** Borage, garlic, chives, marigolds. Avoid planting near brassicas.

**Popular garden varieties:**

| Variety | Type | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Korona' | Summer-fruiting | Early–mid-season (from mid-June) | Large, glossy, dark red, very juicy fruit; excellent resistance to verticillium wilt, botrytis and grey mould — a real advantage in the wet west of Ireland climate |
| 'Ostara' | Perpetual (everbearing) | Early summer, then mid-July until first frosts | Conical, medium-sized, firm, juicy fruit with a flavour recalling wild strawberry; vigorous, spreading habit suits raised beds and hanging baskets |
| 'Summer Breeze' | Perpetual (everbearing) | Throughout the growing season | Compact, ornamental patio strawberry, grown as much for its decorative flowers and habit as for its small, sweet berries; good for containers and borders |

---

### F11. Hazelnut *(Corylus avellana)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Corylus avellana — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corylus_avellana)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 4–5m tall (or 2m coppiced), 4–5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any well-drained soil, including clay and chalk; pH 6.0–7.5. Very adaptable.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — one of the easiest, most tolerant fruiting plants of clay in this guide.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 4–5m between plants for a full-sized bush, or 2m if grown as a managed, regularly coppiced hedge.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed once established; a balanced feed in spring for young plants.

**Companions:** Native Irish hedgerow species (blackthorn, hawthorn) if grown as informal hedging; primroses and bluebells beneath.

**Pruning:** Traditionally pruned in late winter ("brutting" — snapping over long sideshoots in late summer improves nut set); can be coppiced or kept as an open-centred bush.

**West of Ireland:** A native Irish tree/large shrub, found wild in hedgerows throughout the country, producing edible nuts in autumn. Fully hardy and thoroughly at home in the mild, moist Atlantic climate. Wind-pollinated by catkins in late winter, so plant at least two bushes (or rely on wild hazel nearby) for a reliable nut crop, since a single isolated bush often crops poorly. Squirrels are usually quicker than gardeners at harvesting the nuts.

---

### F12. Honeyberry (Kamchatka) *(Lonicera caerulea var. kamtschatica)*


***Russian:*** *Жимолость камчатская*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Honeyberry — Kamchatka — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lonicera_caerulea)

**Popular garden varieties:**

| Variety | Taste | Height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Altaj' | Sweet-sour, aromatic, honey-like | 1.5–2m | From southern Siberia; extremely cold-hardy (to -40°C); thornless, compact habit; produces more aromatic fruit than many varieties |
| 'Larisa' | Fresh, sweet | 1.5–1.8m | Elongated berries rich in vitamins B and C; self-pollinating but crops better with a second variety nearby |

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Fruits better in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–2m tall, 1–1.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Very flexible, pH 4.5–7.5, tolerates clay. Plant two different varieties for cross-pollination.

**Soil prep:** Standard clay improvement with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level or slightly deeper.

**Spacing:** 1–1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in early spring. Annual compost mulch. Liquid balanced feed monthly during flowering and fruiting can improve yields.

**Companions:** Blueberry, lingonberry in adjacent beds. Pollinator-attracting flowers nearby (important for early February–April flowers). Needs 2+ varieties: e.g. 'Borealis' and 'Tundra', or 'Altaj' and 'Larisa'.

---

### F13. Lingonberry *(Vaccinium vitis-idaea)*


***Russian:*** *Брусника обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lingonberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingonberry)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred — woodland edge as recommended by the RHS. Tolerates full sun if soil stays consistently moist.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall (trailing groundcover), 30–50cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Acidic, moist but well-drained, pH 4.2–5.5. Same requirements as blueberry. Will not tolerate neutral or alkaline soil.

**Pot size:** 30cm diameter, 20cm+ deep container; 100% ericaceous compost. Or a dedicated acidic bed.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Space 30cm apart.

**Spacing:** 30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Ericaceous liquid fertiliser monthly April–August only. Mulch with pine bark to maintain acidity.

**Companions:** Blueberry, heather, cranberry. Low ground cover under taller blueberry bushes. Also combines well with deer fern (same acidic conditions) in a naturalistic ericaceous bed.

**West of Ireland:** Produces berries twice per year (late July and late September). Self-fertile. Excellent for west of Ireland mild, moist climate.

---

### F14. Olive *(Olea europaea)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Olea europaea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, in the warmest, most sheltered spot available.

**Height/Spread:** 2–3m tall in open ground (mild areas), 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Very free-draining soil, tolerant of poor, dry, alkaline ground; pH 6.0–8.5. Hates waterlogging above all else.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay heavily with 40–50% horticultural grit, or grow in a large container of free-draining, loam-based compost — strongly preferable to open ground on Irish clay.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants if grown in open ground in a mild area; more commonly grown singly as a container specimen.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring; avoid feeding late in the season.

**Companions:** Lavender, rosemary and other Mediterranean plants sharing its need for sun and sharp drainage.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Strongly recommended in this climate — growing in a large container allows the plant to be moved to a sheltered spot, porch or unheated glasshouse during the coldest, wettest spells of winter, which it tolerates far better than being left out in waterlogged ground.

**West of Ireland:** Borderline hardy (RHS H4, roughly to -5°C, though damage typically comes from wet cold rather than dry cold) — reliably surviving outdoors only in the very mildest, most sheltered coastal west of Ireland gardens, and even then rarely ripening a useful crop of fruit given the lack of sustained summer heat. Best treated as a handsome, silvery-leaved evergreen container specimen for its foliage and gnarled form, with any olives a curiosity rather than a realistic harvest. Winter wet, far more than winter cold, is what usually kills an olive in this climate — sharp drainage is essential.

---

### F15. Peach *(Prunus persica)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Prunus persica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_persica)

**Popular varieties for Ireland/UK:**

| Variety | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| 'Peregrine' | Mid-August | RHS AGM; one of the most reliable croppers for a cooler climate thanks to later flowering; white flesh, excellent flavour |
| 'Rochester' | Mid-August | RHS AGM; yellow flesh, reasonable flavour; large, juicy fruit; later flowering gives some frost protection |
| 'Melred' | Early August | Self-fertile; compact (~2m), well suited to a large container; double deep pink-red ornamental blossom as well as fruit |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, in a warm, sheltered spot such as against a south-facing wall.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall depending on training/variety, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole twice the rootball width. Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark; keep the graft union above soil level.

**Spacing:** 2–3m if free-standing; fan-trained against a wall needs 3–4m of wall space.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser each February; potash-rich feed as fruit develops. Stop feeding by midsummer.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic nearby to deter aphids; underplant with spring bulbs.

**Pruning:** Prune only in summer (never winter) to reduce the risk of silver leaf disease.

**Pot/container:** ✅ 'Melred' in particular stays compact, reaching only around 2m — well suited to a large container on a patio, in addition to open ground.

**West of Ireland:** All three varieties above are self-fertile, so a single tree crops without a pollination partner. Blossom appears March–April (protect from late frosts with horticultural fleece) followed by fruit ripening from early to mid-August depending on variety. Best grown fan-trained against a warm, sheltered wall in the cooler west of Ireland climate, which helps ripen both blossom and fruit and offers some protection from wind and rain. 'Peregrine' and 'Rochester', both later-flowering, are the more reliable choices for a genuinely cool, exposed site; 'Melred' suits a smaller garden or patio.

---

### F16. Pear *(Pyrus communis)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Pyrus communis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pear)

**Popular varieties for Ireland/UK:**

| Variety | Pollination | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Conference' | Self-fertile | Mid-late season | By far the most widely grown UK/Ireland pear; reliable, heavy-cropping, familiar elongated shape |
| 'Concorde' | Partially self-fertile | Late season | RHS AGM; a 'Conference' × 'Doyenné du Comice' cross bred at East Malling; combines Conference's shape with Comice's sweetness |
| 'Doyenné du Comice' | Needs a pollination partner | Late season | Widely regarded as the best-flavoured dessert pear, though a less reliable cropper and needs a sheltered spot |

**Pear rootstocks:**

| Rootstock | Vigour | Approx. height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Quince A | Semi-vigorous | Similar to apple M26 | The standard general-purpose pear rootstock; suits bush, half-standard and most trained forms |
| Quince C | More dwarfing | 1.8–2.4m (6–8ft) | Produces a smaller tree, roughly comparable to apple M9/Bud 9; needs staking and good soil |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for best flowering and fruiting.

**Height/Spread:** 1.8m–4m+ tall depending on rootstock (see table), similar spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole twice the rootball width. Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** Graft union at or just above soil level.

**Spacing:** Depends on rootstock and form — see rootstock table above; fan or espalier against a wall needs 3.5–5m of wall space.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser each February; potash-rich feed as fruit develops.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic nearby to deter aphids; most pears need a compatible pollination partner flowering at the same time — check before buying.

**Pruning:** Prune in winter while dormant (unlike stone fruit); trained forms (espalier, cordon) are pruned in summer.

**West of Ireland:** Pears tolerate the mild, moist west of Ireland climate well, though blossom appears early and can be caught by late frosts — a sheltered, sunny spot away from frost pockets gives the best results. 'Conference' is the most forgiving choice for a typical Irish garden, being both self-fertile and a reliable cropper even in a cooler summer; 'Doyenné du Comice' rewards a warm, sheltered wall with genuinely superior flavour but is the least reliable of the three here.

---

### F17. Plum Tree 'Aprimira' *(Prunus domestica 'Aprimira')*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Plum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential — a warm, sheltered spot gives the best fruit set and ripening.

**Height/Spread:** 3–4m tall (patio form more compact), 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, well-drained soil, pH 6.0–7.0. Dislikes waterlogged clay; good drainage matters more than fertility.

**Soil prep — in the ground:** Dig a hole at least twice the width of the rootball. Break up the base of the hole to help roots establish. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with multipurpose compost and 20–30% horticultural grit over a broad area, since good drainage is the single biggest factor in preventing root problems on wet Irish clay. Firm in well and stake permanently — plums often need support for their whole life on windy sites.

**Soil prep — in a pot (recommended for smaller gardens):** Use a container at least 25cm in diameter with drainage holes, and cover the base with a piece of broken pot or crock so water can always drain freely. Fill with a mix of one-third sharp sand, one-third multipurpose compost and one-third good topsoil or potting soil. Loosen the rootball before planting so roots spread outward rather than circling.

**Planting depth:** Plant so the top of the original rootball sits just below the surrounding soil or compost surface; keep the graft union above soil level.

**Spacing:** Sold as a standard (bush-headed) tree, ideal for a large pot or a small garden bed; allow at least 1–1.5m from other plants or structures.

**Fertiliser:** In pots, feed with cow manure pellets or a balanced granular feed in early spring, then a potash-rich granular feed (e.g. NPK 12-10-18) in May and again in July. In the ground, an annual balanced fruit tree feed each February is sufficient. Stop feeding by midsummer to let the wood ripen before winter.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic at the base help deter aphids. In a container, underplant with low, shallow-rooted plants only, to avoid competing with the tree's roots.

**Pruning:** Prune only in summer, during or just after flowering — never in winter, when open wounds on stone fruit are vulnerable to silver leaf disease. Prune lightly each February to maintain shape while flower buds are visible, working around them where possible.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Bred and sold as a compact standard specifically for patio pots and small gardens; in winter, move container-grown plants to a cool, sheltered spot (a shed or a wall-sheltered corner) and wrap the pot in jute sacking or fleece to protect the roots from hard frost.

**West of Ireland:** 'Aprimira' is a self-fertile mirabelle–apricot hybrid plum, so no second pollinator tree is needed — useful in a smaller west of Ireland garden. It flowers in spring like other plums, so a sheltered, sunny spot away from frost pockets protects the blossom from late frost damage. Harvest is August–September; fruits have sweet, orange-yellow flesh with a stone that separates cleanly.

---

### F18. Pomegranate *(Punica granatum)*


***Russian:*** *Гранат обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Pomegranate — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomegranate)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, against a warm, sheltered south- or west-facing wall.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–3m tall (borderline hardy), 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 5.5–7.0. Dislikes waterlogged clay.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width against the wall base. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–3m against wall; borderline hardy, best in a container

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser in spring. Liquid high-potash feed through summer to encourage flowering/fruiting.

**Companions:** Other wall-trained sun-lovers such as passiflora or grapevine on the same warm wall.

**West of Ireland:** Borderline hardy (RHS H4, roughly to −5 to −10°C) — reliable fruiting in the west of Ireland's cooler, shorter summers is uncertain, and pomegranate is best treated primarily as an ornamental flowering wall shrub here, grown against a warm wall or in a large container that can be moved under cover in winter, with any fruit a bonus rather than a certainty.

---

### F19. Raspberry *(Rubus idaeus)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sú craobh · Малина обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Raspberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raspberry)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Better fruiting in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–1.75m tall, 50cm–1m spread.

**Soil prep:** Compost and grit essential on clay. Raised bed recommended. Plant in mounded rows or raised beds between parallel support wires.

**Planting depth:** Crown 5–7cm below soil. Cut canes to 25cm after planting.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between canes; 1.8m between rows

**Fertiliser:** High-potash fertiliser (tomato feed) fortnightly from spring bud break until fruit set. Or balanced granular fertiliser in early spring plus compost mulch. Avoid excessive nitrogen on wet clay.

**Companions:** Garlic, marigolds, tansy deter aphids. Avoid planting near potatoes (shared virus diseases) and wild brambles.

**Your varieties:**

*Summer-fruiting (floricane — fruit on second-year canes; prune fruited canes to the ground in early to mid-autumn):*

**'Glen Ample'** (PBR) — One of the most widely grown summer-fruiting raspberries in the UK; holds the RHS Award of Garden Merit. Bred at the James Hutton Institute (formerly Scottish Crop Research Institute), Scotland. Mid-season; harvest from late June to late July (sometimes into September). Large to very large, deep red, rich-flavoured berries — five large berries can weigh up to 30g. Almost completely thornless (spineless canes and laterals), making harvesting and pruning easy. High yields — over 2kg per plant — and excellent disease resistance. Canes grow to approximately 1.8m; tie to horizontal support wires. Best in well-drained, fertile, moisture-retentive soil; does not do well in waterlogged or very chalky conditions.

*Autumn-fruiting (primocane — fruit on current year's canes; cut ALL canes to the ground in late winter/early spring):*

**'Zefa Herbsternte'** (also sold as 'Zeva Herbsternte') — A primocane (autumn-fruiting) variety bred in Switzerland in 1952; one of the first commercial primocane raspberries and still widely grown. Sometimes spelled *Zefa*, sometimes *Zeva* — both refer to the same cultivar. Late-season: harvest from August to September (or until first frosts). Large, sweet-tasting, dark red berries measuring 2–3cm in length. Vigorous, upright canes to 150cm with thorns. Particularly well suited to cooler northern regions of the UK and Ireland. Good resistance to phytophthora root disease. Primocane cultivar so fruiting is best in its first year (all canes to the ground in late winter). Fruiting is best in cool summers.

**'Autumn Bliss'** — A primocane (autumn-fruiting) variety bred at East Malling Research Station, England. RHS Award of Garden Merit (1993 and 2009). One of the most widely grown autumn raspberries in the UK. Medium-large, firm, dark red berries from late July through to late October. Excellent disease resistance, particularly to aphids. Relatively short, self-supporting canes (to 150cm) that can often stand without support, though tying in is recommended in exposed west of Ireland conditions. Earlier to crop than most autumn varieties.

**'Heritage'** — An American primocane (autumn-fruiting) variety released in 1969 by Cornell University; long considered the standard against which autumn-bearing varieties are measured. Harvest from August into autumn. Medium-sized, firm, conical, deep red berries with a classic sweet-tart raspberry flavour. Excellent cold hardiness. Sturdy, often self-supporting canes. For UK and Irish growing, managed as an autumn raspberry (cut all canes to the ground in late winter). Popular commercially because fruit is firm, travels well, and the variety is reliably productive.

*Patio/dwarf cultivars (suitable for containers; minimum 45–60cm wide, deep container):*

**'Good as Gold'** (Lowberry® Goodasgold®) — A compact, dwarf autumn-fruiting raspberry with a final height under 100cm (usually 80cm), due to early flowering and fruiting which slows further growth. Produces medium-sized, pale yellow/golden fruits from mid-July through to autumn; sweet flavour and aroma. A few thorns present, reducing towards the tips of fruiting shoots. Bred as a patio cultivar, ideal for containers on a sunny patio or balcony. Part of the Lowberry® range of compact raspberry varieties.

**'Lucky Berry'** — A new, self-fertile, dwarf patio raspberry with a vigorous habit despite its compact size. Produces juicy, sweet-tasting red fruits over a remarkably long season of four months, from July to October, fruiting continuously until the first frosts. Part of the Lucky Berry® range of compact, long-season fruit plants selected specifically for pot cultivation. Resistant to diseases and cold. Prune back before winter. Ideal for containers on a terrace, balcony or patio.

**'Hararasp'** (Raspberry Tree) — A genuinely different growth habit: bred by Japanese breeder Toshihiko Hara, this self-supporting variety develops a thick, upright, self-standing "trunk" instead of sprawling canes, so it needs no support wires or staking. White blossom in April–May is followed by especially glossy, shiny berries from June into summer. Disease-resistant with good tolerance of heat and drought. Height varies with training and maturity, from around 75cm up to 1.5–1.75m; spread 50cm–1m. Fruits on one-year-old stems — prune out old fruited stems in early spring, keeping young growth. An excellent choice for the smallest patio, needing no cane support at all.

---

### F20. Redcurrant *(Ribes rubrum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cuirín dearg · Смородина красная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Redcurrant — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redcurrant)

**Light:** 🌥️ Full sun to partial shade. One of the most shade-tolerant fruiting plants — good for north-facing fences trained as a fan.

**Height/Spread:** 1.2–1.5m tall, 1.2–1.5m spread.

**Soil prep:** Compost and grit amendment. Good drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery mark; roots covered ~5cm.

**Spacing:** 1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Sulphate of potash in late winter. Balanced fertiliser after flowering. Annual bark or compost mulch. Do not overfeed with nitrogen.

**Companions:** Gooseberries, whitecurrants. North-facing fan trained redcurrant is a remarkably productive use of otherwise difficult wall space.

---

### F21. Rhubarb *(Rheum × hybridum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Biabhóg · Ревень*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Rhubarb — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhubarb)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Tolerates partial shade better than most vegetables.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall (foliage; taller in flower), 90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moisture-retentive but well-drained. RHS advises raised beds on heavy clay.

**Soil prep:** Remove weeds, dig in two bucketfuls of compost per sq metre. Raised bed (30cm+) on wet clay.

**Planting depth:** Buds 1–2cm below soil level.

**Spacing:** 90cm between crowns

**Fertiliser:** Balanced granular fertiliser in early spring. Generous well-rotted manure or compost mulch each autumn after leaves die back — this is the most beneficial treatment.

**Companions:** Beans and peas nearby (nitrogen-fixers). Keep away from fennel (inhibits many plants). Do not pick in the first year after planting.

---

### F22. Sea Buckthorn *(Hippophae rhamnoides)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Draighean mara · Облепиха крушиновидная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Hippophae rhamnoides — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippophae_rhamnoides)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall, 2–3m spread (suckering).

**Soil needs:** Any well-drained soil, including poor sandy ground; pH 5.5–7.5. Genuinely salt- and wind-tolerant.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — thrives on poor, exposed coastal ground where little else grows.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants; spreads by suckers.

**Fertiliser:** None needed — fixes its own nitrogen via root nodules.

**Companions:** Best given its own space given its suckering habit; useful as a windbreak/coastal hedge.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Very thorny — wear gloves when pruning or harvesting. Dioecious — need at least one male and one female plant for the vitamin-C-rich orange berries.

**West of Ireland:** A tough, spiny, silver-leaved shrub superbly adapted to exposed, salt-laden coastal conditions, bearing masses of tart orange berries in autumn if both sexes are present. An excellent choice for a windswept west of Ireland coastal garden where few other fruiting shrubs would survive.

---

### F23. Serviceberry *(Amelanchier lamarckii)*


***Russian:*** *Ирга Ламарка*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Serviceberry — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelanchier_lamarckii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ part shade. Best autumn colour in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 4–8m tall as large shrub/small tree, 3–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most moist, well-drained soils including clay; tolerates acid to neutral, pH 5.5–7.0. Dislikes waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width and as deep as the rootball. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–4m between plants/structures

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser in early spring. Annual compost or bark mulch; little further feeding needed once established.

**Companions:** Underplant with hellebores, snowdrops, bluebells and ferns; works well as a woodland-edge specimen with Japanese maple and hydrangea.

**West of Ireland:** Multi-season interest — white spring blossom, edible summer berries (loved by birds), fiery autumn colour. Tolerates coastal wind well once established.

---

### F24. Sour Cherry *(Prunus cerasus)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Crann silíní searbha · Вишня обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Prunus cerasus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_cerasus)

**Popular garden cultivars:**

| Variety | Pollination | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Morello' | Self-fertile | Late summer | RHS AGM; by far the most widely grown cooking cherry; very hardy and reliable; dark red, acid-flavoured fruit; excellent on a shady north-facing wall where sweet cherries would not fruit |
| 'Nabella' | Self-fertile | Mid-summer | Similar acid flavour to 'Morello' with slightly larger fruit; good disease resistance |
| 'Kentish Red' | Self-fertile | Traditional/mid-season | An old, traditional English sour cherry, valued for jams and preserves |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade — the one fruit tree in this guide genuinely happy on a shady, north-facing wall.

**Height/Spread:** 2.5–4m tall (on Colt rootstock), 2.5–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile soil; pH 5.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole wider and deeper than the rootball. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% grit. Dig in plenty of well-rotted manure or compost.

**Planting depth:** Graft union at or just above soil level.

**Spacing:** On Colt rootstock (the standard choice for sour cherries), up to 4m; fan-trained against a wall needs 3.5–4.5m of wall space.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser in early spring, with a further feed after harvest.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic nearby to deter aphids.

**Pruning:** Prune in summer only, to reduce the risk of silver leaf disease. Sour cherries fruit on the previous year's wood, so after harvest cut out some of the older fruited wood to encourage new growth for next year — a different regime from sweet cherries.

**West of Ireland:** Genuinely useful for a shadier or north-facing spot where few other fruiting trees would crop well — sour cherries tolerate part shade far better than sweet cherries. Fully hardy in the mild, moist Irish climate. All three cultivars above are self-fertile, so a single tree is enough for a crop.

---

### F25. Strawberry *(Fragaria × ananassa)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sú talún · Клубника садовая (земляника ананасная)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Strawberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for maximum fruiting.

**Height/Spread:** 20–30cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 5.5–6.8, well-drained. Raised bed on clay strongly recommended.

**Soil prep:** Compost and grit. Raised bed 20–25cm high.

**Planting depth:** Crown exactly at soil level — too deep = crown rot; too shallow = roots dry out.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants (or 45cm × 75cm rows if in open ground)

**Fertiliser:** High-potash fertiliser (tomato feed) fortnightly from first flowers until fruiting ceases. Balanced granular fertiliser in early spring. Do not use high-nitrogen fertiliser.

**Companions:** Borage, garlic, chives, marigolds. Avoid planting near brassicas.

**Popular garden varieties:**

| Variety | Type | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Korona' | Summer-fruiting | Early–mid-season (from mid-June) | Large, glossy, dark red, very juicy fruit; excellent resistance to verticillium wilt, botrytis and grey mould — a real advantage in the wet west of Ireland climate |
| 'Ostara' | Perpetual (everbearing) | Early summer, then mid-July until first frosts | Conical, medium-sized, firm, juicy fruit with a flavour recalling wild strawberry; vigorous, spreading habit suits raised beds and hanging baskets |
| 'Summer Breeze' | Perpetual (everbearing) | Throughout the growing season | Compact, ornamental patio strawberry, grown as much for its decorative flowers and habit as for its small, sweet berries; good for containers and borders |

---

### F26. Sweet Cherry *(Prunus avium)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Prunus avium — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_avium)

**Popular garden cultivars (UK/Ireland):**

| Variety | Pollination | Season | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Athos' | Self-fertile | June–July | Compact patio bush grown on its own roots (not grafted); very winter-hardy; stays 100–250cm depending on container/ground |
| 'Stella' | Self-fertile | Mid-July | RHS AGM; the original self-fertile sweet cherry (bred at the John Innes Institute, Norwich); tangy, dark red-black fruit; reliable in cooler areas |
| 'Sunburst' | Self-fertile | Early–mid-July | Large, dark red-black fruit with a full, sweet flavour; considered by many the best all-round self-fertile garden cherry |
| 'Lapins' (syn. 'Cherokee') | Self-fertile | Late July–early August | Large, dark, crack-resistant fruit; extends the season beyond 'Stella'; vigorous, upright |
| 'Sweetheart' | Self-fertile | Very late season | Red to dark red fruit; outstanding flavour and texture; RHS AGM |
| 'Sylvia' | Self-fertile | June–July | Exceptionally compact (125–150cm), ideal for the smallest patios and large containers |

**Cherry rootstocks:**

| Rootstock | Vigour | Approx. height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Colt | Semi-vigorous | 3.5–4m (12–14ft) | Good anchorage, no permanent staking needed once established; tolerates poorer soil than Gisela 5; the traditional choice for a fan or bush in a larger garden |
| Gisela 5 | Semi-dwarfing | 2.4–3m (8–10ft) | The most popular modern choice for a manageable garden cherry; needs permanent staking and fertile, reliably moist soil |
| Krymsk 5 | Semi-vigorous | Similar to Gisela 6, slightly larger than Gisela 5 | Very cold-hardy, well anchored, does not usually need permanent staking |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential — at least 6 hours daily. Avoid frost pockets, since late spring frosts damage blossom.

**Height/Spread:** 1–4m tall depending on rootstock/cultivar (see table), similar spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile soil; pH 5.5–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole wider and deeper than the rootball. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20–30% horticultural grit for drainage. In a pot, use a container at least 30–40cm in diameter with good drainage holes.

**Planting depth:** Graft union at or just above soil level (own-root types like 'Athos' have no graft union — plant at the same depth as the nursery pot).

**Spacing:** Depends on rootstock and form — see rootstock table above; compact patio cultivars like 'Athos' and 'Sylvia' need only 1–1.5m.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fruit tree fertiliser (Growmore or specialist fruit feed) in early spring, with a further feed after harvest. Avoid feeding late in the season.

**Companions:** Chives and garlic nearby help deter aphids. Keep a weed-free, mulched area around the base.

**Pruning:** Prune only in summer, ideally straight after harvest, to reduce the risk of silver leaf disease common to Prunus species.

**Pot/container:** ✅ 'Athos' and 'Sylvia' were bred specifically for pot culture and are among the most compact, patio-friendly sweet cherries available. ⚠️ Protect ripening fruit from birds with netting.

**West of Ireland:** Sweet cherries tolerate the mild, wet west of Ireland winters well provided drainage is adequate; the main local risk is losing blossom to a late spring frost, so a sheltered spot away from frost pockets gives the most reliable cropping. For a small garden or patio, 'Athos' or 'Sylvia' on their own compact roots are the most realistic choice; for a larger garden, any of the self-fertile cultivars above on Gisela 5 (small) or Colt (larger, more tolerant of poorer soil) will crop reliably.

---

### F27. Woodland Strawberry *(Fragaria vesca)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sú talún fiáin · Земляника лесная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Woodland Strawberry — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fragaria_vesca)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade. Tolerates full shade but fruits less freely there.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 25–30cm spread (spreads by runners).

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 5.5–7.0. Tolerates clay and a wide pH range better than most fruit.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost. Mulch with leaf mould to mimic its natural woodland-edge habitat.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level — do not bury the crown.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants; runners will fill gaps and can be left to spread or removed to keep a tidier clump.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch, kept away from the crown.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, primroses — a natural, decorative groundcover under fruit trees and in a woodland-edge bed; also good as informal edging along a path.

**West of Ireland:** Smaller and less showy than garden strawberry, but reliably hardy, shade-tolerant and long-fruiting (often June to autumn), with small, intensely flavoured berries. Spreads gently by runners to form an attractive edible groundcover under trees and shrubs — a good choice for a spot too shaded for garden strawberry.

**A note on "Alpine Strawberry":** This popular garden name refers to cultivated, non-running forms of this same species (*Fragaria vesca*, often listed as var. *semperflorens*), bred as compact, "runnerless" plants that stay in a tidy clump rather than spreading — useful for edging a path or bed where a spreading wild form would be unwelcome. Popular alpine strawberry cultivars include 'Mignonette' and 'Alexandria'. Growing needs are identical to the wild woodland strawberry described above; alpine types simply stay put rather than colonising by runners.

---


---

## GROUP G: GROUNDCOVER & CREEPING PLANTS

### G1. Bellflowers (Rockery) *(Campanula spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Campanula saxifraga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula) · [Adriatic Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_garganica) · [Carpathian Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_carpatica) · [Dalmatian Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_portenschlagiana)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Adriatic Bellflower | *C. garganica* | Up to 10cm | Summer (June–September) | At pot level | Mat-forming, star-shaped lilac-blue flowers with a white centre; RHS AGM; superb for wall crevices and paving cracks |
