**Spacing:** 30–50cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed; over-feeding produces lush but less flavourful growth.

**Companions:** Rosemary, sage, lavender — classic Mediterranean-herb-bed companions with similar sun and drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** French tarragon (*A. dracunculus* 'Sativa') has the superior aniseed-liquorice flavour prized in classic French cooking, but its slender growth struggles with the combination of wet and cold in an Irish winter — best grown in a container that can be moved to a sheltered spot, or given a very free-draining raised bed. Russian tarragon (*A. dracunculus* 'Inodora') is far more vigorous and copes much better with Irish winters in open ground, but its flavour is markedly inferior — more grassy, with a slight bitterness, and lacking the warm aniseed character of the French type. Buy tarragon plants in person where possible, checking the aroma before purchasing, since plants sold under either name can be variable.

---

### H32. Wild Garlic *(Allium ursinum)*

***Irish / Russian:*** *Creamh · Лук медвежий*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Wild Garlic — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allium_ursinum)

**Light:** 🌑 Partial to full shade; more tolerant of shade than most alliums.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, fertile, well-drained soil, slightly acid to neutral; pH 5.5–7.0. Naturally a plant of damp, established woodland.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with leaf mould or multipurpose compost to mimic the humus-rich woodland floor this plant prefers; add horticultural grit only if the site is genuinely waterlogged.

**Planting depth:** Bulbs about 2.5cm deep, planted in autumn.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between bulbs; spreads readily by bulbils and self-seeding to form a dense carpet.

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Native bluebells (a classic damp-woodland combination), ferns, primroses — underplant deciduous trees and shrubs for an early-summer woodland floor display.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Spreads vigorously and can become a nuisance in a small or formal garden — best confined to a naturalistic woodland corner rather than a border, and never introduced into wild areas beyond the garden.

**West of Ireland:** Native and common throughout Ireland, carpeting damp woodland floors and shady hedge banks with garlic-scented white flowers from April to June before dying back completely by midsummer. Both leaves and flowers are edible (with a milder flavour than bulb garlic) and popular for pesto, but take care with identification, since the leaves can be confused with poisonous Lily of the Valley or Lords-and-Ladies before flowering — if in doubt, crush a leaf and check for the unmistakable garlic smell.

---

### H33. Zucchini (Courgette) *(Cucurbita pepo var. cylindrica, var. giraumontia)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cúirséad · Кабачок (Цуккини)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Zucchini / Courgette — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cucurbita_pepo)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun, sheltered from wind.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 90cm–1.2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Rich, moisture-retentive but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Dig in plenty of well-rotted compost/manure into a planting hole or mound — courgettes are hungry, thirsty plants.

**Planting depth:** At pot level, after all risk of frost has passed.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** High-potash liquid feed (tomato feed) weekly once flowering/fruiting begins.

**Companions:** Nasturtiums (trap crop), sweetcorn, beans — classic "three sisters"-adjacent vegetable bed companions.

**West of Ireland:** Very productive in a warm, sheltered, sunny spot; keep well watered in dry spells and harvest fruit young and regularly (every 2–3 days once cropping) to keep plants producing through the season.

---


---

## GROUP P: ORNAMENTAL PERENNIALS

### P1. Alstroemeria *(Alstroemeria spp.)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Alstroemeria — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alstroemeria)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit; tuberous roots dislike sitting in waterlogged ground.

**Planting depth:** Plant tubers 15–20cm deep, well below the frost line, since the fleshy roots resent disturbance and cold.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring; high-potash liquid feed through summer to prolong flowering.

**Companions:** Roses, geraniums, salvias — classic cottage/mixed-border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A long-flowering, exotic-looking herbaceous perennial with clusters of trumpet-shaped, often bicoloured flowers from early summer well into autumn if deadheaded regularly (pull rather than cut the flowering stems, to encourage more). Modern hardy hybrids (e.g. the Princess Lily/Inca series) are reliably perennial in the mild west of Ireland climate given reasonable drainage; a deep planting depth and a dry winter mulch help protect the tubers from the worst of the wet.

---

### P2. Avens *(Geum spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Гравилат*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Geum chiloense — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geum_chiloense)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost; good drainage matters, but the soil should not dry out either.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; deadhead regularly to prolong flowering.

**Companions:** Alliums, irises, roses, hardy geraniums — classic sunny border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A cheerful, clump-forming perennial with semi-evergreen foliage and wiry stems of vivid red, orange or yellow single or double flowers held well above the leaves from late spring through summer. Popular cultivars include 'Mrs J. Bradshaw' (scarlet-red) and 'Lady Stratheden' (golden-yellow). Fully hardy and easy in the mild, moist Irish climate; divide congested clumps every 2–3 years to maintain vigour.

---

### P3. Balloon Flower *(Platycodon grandiflorus)*


***Russian:*** *Ширококолокольчик крупноцветковый*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Platycodon grandiflorus — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platycodon_grandiflorus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 40–60cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes winter waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; resents root disturbance once established.

**Spacing:** 30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, sedum — classic late-summer border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A charming perennial named for its puffed-up, balloon-like buds that pop open into star-shaped blue, white or pink flowers in summer. Slow to emerge in spring (easy to forget it's there); needs sharp drainage to overwinter reliably on Irish clay.

---

### P4. Bellflowers (Border) *(Campanula spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Clustered Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_glomerata) · [Spreading Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_patula) · [Peach-leaved Bellflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_persicifolia)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clustered Bellflower | *C. glomerata* | 30–60cm | Summer | Crown at soil level | Dense clusters of violet-purple, upward-facing bells; spreads by rhizomes into good-sized clumps |
| Spreading Bellflower | *C. patula* | 30–60cm | Summer | Crown at soil level, or surface-sown seed | Open, star-shaped violet-blue bells on branching stems; biennial/short-lived native wildflower |
| Peach-leaved Bellflower | *C. persicifolia* | 60cm–1m | June–July | Crown at soil level | Tall, elegant spires of large cup-shaped lilac-blue or white bells; a classic cottage-garden perennial |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade for all three.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** *C. glomerata*: most well-drained soils including clay, preferring neutral to alkaline, pH 6.5–7.5. *C. patula*: well-drained soil, pH 6.0–7.5. *C. persicifolia*: moist but well-drained, fertile soil.

**Soil prep:** *C. glomerata*: mix clay 50/50 with compost. *C. patula*: mix clay with compost and grit. *C. persicifolia*: mix clay 50/50 with compost, ensuring reasonable moisture retention alongside drainage.

**Planting depth:** *C. glomerata* and *C. persicifolia*: crown at soil level. *C. patula*: crown at soil level, or sow seed on the surface in late spring/summer.

**Spacing:** *C. glomerata*: 30–40cm between plants, divide every 3–4 years. *C. patula*: 20–30cm between plants; biennial/short-lived. *C. persicifolia*: 30–45cm between plants; divide every 2–4 years.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring for all three; *C. glomerata* also benefits from an annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** *C. glomerata*: roses, peonies, ornamental grasses — sunny cottage-border companions. *C. patula*: ox-eye daisy, wild grasses, other cottage/meadow-style companions. *C. persicifolia*: delphiniums, roses, lupins — classic tall cottage-border companions; excellent as a cut flower.

**West of Ireland:** *C. glomerata* spreads by rhizomes to form good-sized clumps — divide every 3–4 years to keep vigorous and control spread in smaller borders. *C. patula* is a biennial or short-lived perennial native British/Irish wildflower, distinct from the more vigorously spreading (and often invasive in gardens) *Campanula rapunculoides* — self-seeds gently; deadhead if self-seeding is not wanted. *C. persicifolia* is a widely grown cottage-garden classic that self-seeds readily in the right conditions and performs well in the cool, moist Irish climate; deadhead to prolong flowering.

---

### P5. Blue Gentian *(Gentiana spp., e.g. G. acaulis, G. sino-ornata)*


***Russian:*** *Горечавка*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Blue Gentian — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentiana)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade, depending on species.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil; most garden gentians prefer slightly acid conditions, pH 5.5–6.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost and grit; a raised bed or rockery suits most species well.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced or ericaceous feed in spring. Annual mulch with compost or leaf mould.

**Companions:** Heather, dwarf conifers, saxifrage — classic rockery/alpine-bed companions.

**West of Ireland:** Intensely blue, trumpet-shaped autumn or spring flowers (depending on species) make this a rockery highlight. The moist, mild west of Ireland climate suits autumn-flowering Asiatic gentians (*G. sino-ornata* type) particularly well, given reasonably acid soil.

---

### P6. Border Sedum (Stonecrop) *(Hylotelephium spectabile)*


***Russian:*** *Очитник видный*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Hylotelephium spectabile — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylotelephium_spectabile)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes rich, wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed — rich feeding produces floppy growth.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, echinacea, achillea — classic late-season border companions; flowers are a magnet for butterflies and bees.

**West of Ireland:** A reliable, easy perennial with fleshy, glaucous foliage and flat heads of pink flowers (ageing to rust) from late summer into autumn, valuable for late-season pollinators and winter seedhead structure. Needs sharp drainage to avoid becoming floppy or rotting on Irish clay.

---

### P7. Chilean Avens *(Geum chiloense)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Geum chiloense — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geum_chiloense)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost; good drainage matters, but the soil should not dry out either.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; deadhead regularly to prolong flowering.

**Companions:** Alliums, irises, roses, hardy geraniums — classic sunny border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A cheerful, clump-forming perennial with semi-evergreen foliage and wiry stems of vivid red, orange or yellow single or double flowers held well above the leaves from late spring through summer. Popular cultivars include 'Mrs J. Bradshaw' (scarlet-red) and 'Lady Stratheden' (golden-yellow). Fully hardy and easy in the mild, moist Irish climate; divide congested clumps every 2–3 years to maintain vigour.

---

### P100. Chinese Lantern *(Physalis alkekengi)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Physalis alkekengi — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physalis_alkekengi)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall (up to 90cm in very good conditions), 60–90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moisture-retentive, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of a wide range of conditions.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost. If planting directly in a border, sink a bottomless container or install a root barrier at least 30cm deep — this is essential, not optional (see Warnings below).

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level, or plant divisions in spring or autumn.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between plants, given its spreading habit — closer spacing simply means it fills in faster.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; otherwise little needed, as it grows vigorously on its own.

**Companions:** Best given its own contained space (large pot or root-barriered bed) rather than mixed closely with less vigorous neighbours.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Spreads aggressively by underground rhizomes, in a similar way to mint, and self-seeds too — genuinely invasive if left uncontained, and difficult to fully eradicate once established in open ground. Always grow in a large sunken pot or behind a deep, solid root barrier rather than directly in a border. All parts are toxic except the fully ripe (bright orange, fully coloured) fruit — unripe berries, leaves and stems contain solanine; wear gloves when handling, and keep away from children and pets.

**West of Ireland:** Grown almost entirely for its papery, bright orange, lantern-shaped calyces in autumn, which enclose a small red berry and persist through winter — spectacular cut and dried for arrangements (cut the stems as the lanterns turn orange, strip the leaves, and hang upside down to dry). Small creamy-white flowers in summer are inconspicuous by comparison. Thoroughly at home in the mild, moist Irish climate — so much so that containment, not cultivation, is the real challenge here.

---

### P8. Columbine *(Aquilegia vulgaris and hybrids)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Colaimbín · Водосбор обыкновенный (аквилегия)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Aquilegia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquilegia_vulgaris)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of clay if not waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants; self-seeds freely

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Foxgloves, hardy geraniums, roses — classic cottage-garden border companions; self-seeds readily among other perennials.

**West of Ireland:** Easy, reliable, and self-seeds freely — often needs thinning rather than encouraging once established. Distinctive spurred flowers in a wide range of colours through late spring/early summer.

---

### P9. Coneflower *(Echinacea purpurea)*


***Russian:*** *Эхинацея пурпурная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Echinacea purpurea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echinacea_purpurea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 20–25% grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 40–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring.

**Companions:** Rudbeckia, ornamental grasses, sedum — classic prairie-style late-summer border.

**West of Ireland:** A popular, easy perennial with large, daisy-like pink-purple flowers with a prominent brown-orange cone, from summer into autumn — excellent for pollinators and later left standing for winter seed-eating birds. Needs decent drainage to thrive reliably on Irish clay.

---

### P10. Cutleaf Coneflower 'Goldball' *(Rudbeckia laciniata 'Goldball')*


***Russian:*** *Рудбекия рассечённая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Rudbeckia 'Goldball' — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudbeckia_laciniata)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun preferred. Will flower in light shade, but the shadier the location the less they will flower.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–2m tall, 60–90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** A good choice for improved clay soils — easy to grow if given full sun or partial shade and a moderately fertile soil that is moist but well-drained. pH 5.5–7.0. Moisture-retentive soil is important — dislikes prolonged dry periods in summer.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost to clay (50/50). If the soil is very heavy or is not free draining, add lots of well-rotted compost to the area and dig it in well.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Plant bare-root crowns with buds 2–3cm below soil.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between; divide every 2–3 yrs

**Fertiliser:** Rudbeckia needs little or no regular applications of fertiliser in most garden soils. Mulching plants in borders with well-rotted manure or garden compost each spring should be sufficient. Certainly do not feed with nitrogen-rich fertilisers — this will only encourage leaf growth at the expense of flowers.

**Special note on R. laciniata:** *R. laciniata* has vigorous rhizomes (horizontal underground stems) that make it better suited to larger gardens. It might need lifting, dividing and reducing every two to three years to keep it in check. *R. laciniata* can reach 3m (10ft). This tall variety is inclined to flop, so pinch out the shoots in early summer to promote strong and bushy growth, or provide support.

**Companions:** Helenium, echinacea, late-flowering salvias, ornamental grasses, asters for a dramatic late-summer/autumn border. Excellent with purple-flowered plants (veronicastrum, verbena bonariensis). The tall flowers of 'Goldball' are stunning in naturalistic or prairie-style planting schemes.

**Pot size:** For containers, use a liquid fertiliser such as Phostrogen or seaweed feed. Minimum 40–50cm diameter; deep container needed given eventual 2–3m height. ⚠️ Better in-ground given ultimate vigour.

**West of Ireland:** Rudbeckia does very well in the moist, cool-summer Irish climate. Does not suffer from drought issues. Excellent for late summer colour when many plants are declining. Divide regularly to prevent uncontrolled spread.

---

### P11. Daylilies *(Hemerocallis spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Лилейник*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Daylilies — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemerocallis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 45–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils including clay, tolerant of a wide pH range 6.0–7.5. One of the easiest, most clay-tolerant border perennials.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost; grit optional, since daylilies are notably tolerant of heavy soil.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level, roots spread out in the hole.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants; divide every 3–4 yrs

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Roses, ornamental grasses, achillea (yarrow) — classic sunny border companions.

**West of Ireland:** Extremely tough, reliable, and genuinely clay-tolerant — one of the best "no-fuss" flowering perennials for west of Ireland gardens. Each flower lasts only a day, but a well-established clump produces a long succession of blooms through summer. Divide every 3–4 years to rejuvenate.

---

### P12. Delphiniums *(Delphinium spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Живокость (дельфиниум)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Delphiniums — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delphinium)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — 6+ hours. Wind shelter equally essential.

**Height/Spread:** 1–2m tall, 60–90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Rich, fertile, well-drained, pH 6.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig 40–50cm deep; generous compost and 20% grit on heavy clay.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; bare-root buds 2–5cm below soil.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between plants; stake essential

**Fertiliser:** Balanced granular fertiliser in early spring. High-potash liquid feed (tomato feed) fortnightly from bud formation through flowering. After first flush: cut stems, apply balanced liquid feed to encourage second flush. Annual well-rotted manure mulch.

**Companions:** Roses, lupins, peonies, alliums, irises in cottage-garden combination. Staking mandatory. Slug protection critical in the wet west.

---

### P13. Edelweiss *(Leontopodium alpinum)*


***Russian:*** *Эдельвейс альпийский*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Edelweiss — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leontopodium_alpinum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile, alkaline soil; pH 6.5–7.5. Excellent drainage essential.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay heavily with 30–40% grit; best in a raised bed, trough or rockery.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Very little required — rich soil produces soft, less characterful growth.

**Companions:** Sempervivum, saxifrage, other alpine rockery plants with the same sharp-drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** A true alpine, and one of the more challenging plants on wet west of Ireland clay — reliable success generally needs a raised, gritty, free-draining bed or trough rather than open ground. Silvery-white star-shaped flower bracts in summer.

---

### P14. Elephant's Ears *(Bergenia spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Бадан*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Elephant's Ears — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bergenia)

**Light:** 🌑 Full shade to ☀️ full sun. Leaves colour best in open, exposed positions.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Humus-rich, moist but well-drained. Excellent on clay.

**Soil prep:** Add compost. Unfussy.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; rhizome just at or slightly below surface.

**Spacing:** 30–60cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Low-maintenance. Balanced slow-release in spring, or just annual bark mulch. Do not overfeed.

**Companions:** Hostas, hellebores, snowdrops, pulmonaria, ferns. Bold evergreen foliage contrasts beautifully with delicate fern texture.

---

### P15. Fern-leaf Peony *(Paeonia tenuifolia)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Piaine · Пион узколистный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Paeonia tenuifolia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paeonia_tenuifolia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile soil, neutral to slightly alkaline; pH 6.5–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging, more so than common garden peonies.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost and 20–30% grit for extra drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown/eyes no more than 2–3cm below soil level — as with all peonies, planting too deep prevents flowering.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring; light high-potash feed as buds form.

**Companions:** Alliums, bearded iris, catmint — classic sunny border companions with similar drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** A smaller, earlier-flowering species peony with finely divided, fern-like foliage and deep red single flowers — distinct from the common garden peonies (*Paeonia* spp.) already in this guide. More particular about drainage than common peonies, so a raised or well-drained spot gives the best results on Irish clay.

---

### P102. Giant Bellflower *(Campanula latifolia var. macrantha)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Campanula latifolia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campanula_latifolia)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun.

**Height/Spread:** Up to 1m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Fertile, moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Ferns and other woodland-edge perennials in partial shade; roses and taller border perennials in sun.

**West of Ireland:** A tall, clump-forming native-range perennial (Europe to western Asia) with large, dark violet-blue, narrowly bell-shaped flowers held in upright racemes from July to September — the darker-flowered form of the species, since the straight species can also occur in white. Genuinely at home in the mild, moist Irish climate; may self-seed and can spread somewhat in good conditions, but is easily controlled by removing unwanted seedlings.

---

### P16. Globe Thistle *(Echinops ritro)*


***Russian:*** *Мордовник обыкновенный*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Echinops ritro — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echinops_ritro)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 90cm–1.2m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes rich, wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% horticultural grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed — thrives on lean soil; rich feeding produces floppy growth.

**Companions:** Sedum, ornamental grasses, achillea — classic dry, sunny border companions; excellent for pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** A striking architectural perennial with spiny, silvery foliage and perfectly spherical, steel-blue thistle flowerheads in summer, popular with bees. Needs genuinely sharp drainage to thrive on the region's clay — a raised bed or gravel garden gives the most reliable results.

---

### P17. Globeflower *(Trollius europaeus / T. x cultorum)*


***Russian:*** *Купальница европейская*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Trollius — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trollius_europaeus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist to wet, humus-rich soil; pH 5.5–7.0. Unusually for a border perennial, thrives in damp ground and tolerates heavy clay very well.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; no need for extra drainage — this plant prefers consistently moist soil.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch, keeping the crown moist.

**Companions:** Astilbe, hosta, candelabra primulas — classic damp border or pond-margin companions.

**West of Ireland:** One of the few showy border perennials genuinely suited to the wetter parts of a west of Ireland garden — round, globe-like yellow or orange flowers on upright stems in late spring. Excellent for a damp border or the margin of a pond garden.

---

### P18. Honesty *(Lunaria annua)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus na gealaí · Лунник однолетний*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Lunaria annua — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunaria_annua)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant and easy.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** Sow direct, 1cm deep, late spring/summer to flower the following year (biennial).

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed.

**Companions:** Foxgloves, forget-me-nots — classic cottage-garden/woodland-edge biennial border.

**West of Ireland:** An easy biennial with purple or white spring flowers followed by the flat, silvery, coin-like seed pods ("moonpennies") prized for dried arrangements. Self-seeds readily in the mild, moist Irish climate, returning reliably once established.

---

### P19. Irises *(Iris spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Bearded Iris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_germanica) · [Siberian Iris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_sibirica)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bearded Iris (Border Iris) | *I. germanica* | 60–90cm | Early summer (May–June) | Rhizome at/just proud of soil surface, top exposed to sun | Classic sunny-border iris; needs sharp drainage, rots in wet clay |
| Siberian Iris | *I. sibirica* | 60–100cm | May–June | Rhizome at soil level or 2–3cm below | Thrives in moist, heavy clay and tolerates boggy ground, unlike Bearded Iris |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for Bearded Iris — at least 6 hours daily for good flowering. Siberian Iris flowers most freely in full sun but tolerates dappled shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–100cm tall depending on species (see table), 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** The two species have almost opposite soil preferences, so choose the right one for your site. Bearded Iris: free-draining, neutral to slightly alkaline soil, pH 6.5–7.5 — rhizomes rot in wet, poorly-drained clay, which is the single biggest cause of failure. Siberian Iris: moist, fertile, neutral to slightly acidic soil, pH 5.8–7.0 — the RHS confirms Siberian irises "do well in heavy clay soil" and prefer consistently moist conditions, tolerating slightly boggy ground near ponds or streams.

**Soil prep:** Bearded Iris: improve clay generously with 30% horticultural grit; raised beds or mounded planting strongly recommended on heavy soil. Siberian Iris: dig in generous compost (50/50 with clay) — no need for heavy grit, since this species actually prefers moisture-retentive conditions; mulch each autumn with bark or compost to retain moisture and protect the rhizome crowns.

**Planting depth:** Bearded Iris: rhizome at or just proud of the soil surface, top exposed to sun — do not bury, unlike most other rhizomes. Siberian Iris: at soil level or just 2–3cm below, planted in spring or early autumn.

**Spacing:** Bearded Iris: 30–45cm between rhizomes, divide every 3–4 years. Siberian Iris: 30–45cm between plants; clumps spread slowly outward over years, divide every 3–5 years.

**Fertiliser:** Bearded Iris: low-nitrogen, high-potash feed (e.g. tomato feed) in spring and again after flowering — avoid manure or high-nitrogen feeds, which encourage rot. Siberian Iris: a single balanced fertiliser (e.g. Growmore) application in early spring is usually sufficient; avoid high-nitrogen feeds, which promote leafy growth at the expense of flowers, and do not feed in late summer or autumn.

**Companions:** Bearded Iris: roses, peonies, alliums — classic sunny border companions with similar sun and drainage needs. Siberian Iris: excellent at pond edges alongside Yellow Flag Iris, bogbean and meadowsweet; in borders, works beautifully with astilbe, hostas, daylilies, primulas and ornamental grasses, and pairs well with native royal fern for a classic Atlantic garden look; alliums flower slightly earlier and make a good succession companion.

**Pot/container:** Siberian Iris: ✅ possible in a 35–40cm diameter / 15–20L container, using multipurpose compost with extra moisture retention (vermiculite rather than grit); keep consistently moist. Bearded Iris is not well suited to pots — needs a wide, shallow container with excellent drainage if attempted.

**West of Ireland:** Between them, these two cover most garden situations. Bearded Iris needs a raised, gritty, south-facing bed to do well on west of Ireland clay, and congested clumps should be divided every 3–4 years, discarding the older woody centre and replanting healthy young rhizomes. Siberian Iris, by contrast, is an outstanding choice for the region — unlike Bearded Iris it actively thrives in the moist, heavy soil typical of the Atlantic coast, is fully hardy to -20°C, and flowers May–June in blue, purple, violet or white depending on cultivar (good choices: 'Caesar's Brother', 'White Swirl', 'Flight of Butterflies').

---

### P20. Japanese Anemone *(Anemone × hybrida)*


***Russian:*** *Ветреница гибридная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Anemone × hybrida — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anemone_%C3%97_hybrida)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun. Excellent in semi-shaded spots.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; plant potted rhizomes in spring. Fully hardy — never needs lifting.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants; can spread by rhizome.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring and compost mulch. Low-maintenance.

**Companions:** Hostas, astilbe, hellebores — all share moisture and partial-shade requirements. Can spread — be aware in small borders.

**West of Ireland:** A tall, late-season herbaceous perennial with saucer-shaped pink or white flowers held on wiry stems from late summer into autumn (August–October), when much else in the shade border has finished. Fully hardy and easy in the mild, moist Irish climate, though it spreads steadily by rhizome and can become difficult to remove once well established — plant with that in mind in a smaller border.

---

### P21. Lady's Bedstraw *(Galium verum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Bolach cnis · Подмаренник настоящий*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lady's Bedstraw — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galium_verum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 20–30cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil, tolerant of poor/dry conditions; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — this native wildflower thrives on lean soil; excess compost/fertility encourages weak, floppy growth.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** None required — over-feeding is counterproductive for this meadow wildflower.

**Companions:** Ox-eye daisy, yarrow, wild grasses — classic dry meadow/wildflower planting.

**West of Ireland:** A native wildflower with masses of tiny, honey-scented yellow flowers in summer, traditionally used to stuff mattresses (hence the name) and once used to curdle milk in cheesemaking. Best suited to a wildflower meadow area or dry, sunny bank rather than a fertile border.

---

### P22. Lanceleaf Tickseed *(Coreopsis lanceolata)*


***Russian:*** *Кореопсис ланцетовидный*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Coreopsis lanceolata — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coreopsis_lanceolata)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes rich, wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed — thrives on lean soil.

**Companions:** Achillea, ornamental grasses, echinacea — classic dry prairie-style border companions.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, long-flowering perennial with bright yellow daisy flowers from early to late summer. Needs sharp drainage to perform reliably on Irish clay and can be short-lived in consistently wet ground; a raised or gritty bed suits it best.

---

### P23. Lilyleaf Ladybells *(Adenophora liliifolia)*


***Russian:*** *Бубенчик лилиелистный (колокольчик лилиелистный)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lilyleaf Ladybells — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenophora_liliifolia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; resents root disturbance once established (like many bellflower relatives, it has a fleshy taproot), so choose the final position with care.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Other cottage-border perennials such as clustered bellflower, delphiniums, phlox.

**West of Ireland:** A relative of the campanulas, with nodding, pale blue-violet bell flowers on upright stems in summer. Less commonly available than campanulas but similarly easy once settled — best left undisturbed once planted.

---

### P25. Lungworts *(Pulmonaria spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Медуница*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lungworts — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulmonaria)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade.

**Height/Spread:** 25–30cm tall, 45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well if not waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants; divide every 3–4 yrs

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Hellebores, ferns, hostas — classic early-spring woodland/shade border planting; one of the earliest nectar sources for bees.

**West of Ireland:** Reliable, easy, semi-evergreen shade perennial with attractively spotted leaves and pink-to-blue spring flowers (often both colours on the same plant). Spreads gently to form good groundcover under trees and shrubs; divide every 3–4 years.

---

### P26. Lupins *(Lupinus polyphyllus and hybrids)*


***Russian:*** *Люпин многолистный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lupins — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lupinus_polyphyllus)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 90cm–1.5m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, slightly acid soil; pH 5.5–6.5. Dislikes shallow chalk.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost; avoid excess lime.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; resents root disturbance, so choose the final position with care.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring — like other legumes, lupins fix some of their own nitrogen, so avoid overfeeding.

**Companions:** Delphiniums, roses, foxgloves — classic tall cottage-border companions.

**West of Ireland:** Tall, showy flower spikes in early-mid summer in a wide colour range. Slug damage to young shoots is the main challenge on damp Irish clay — protect emerging growth in spring. Cut back after flowering to encourage a possible second, smaller flush.

---

### P27. Masterwort *(Astrantia major)*


***Russian:*** *Астранция крупная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Astrantia major — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrantia_major)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well given reasonable moisture.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 40–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Hardy geraniums, ferns, hostas — classic cottage-garden/woodland-edge companions.

**West of Ireland:** An elegant, easy perennial with distinctive pincushion-like flowers surrounded by papery bracts, in shades of white, pink or deep red, from early to late summer. Thrives in the mild, moist Irish climate; self-seeds gently and tolerates both sun and part shade well.

---

### P28. Ornamental Grasses *(various genera)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Festuca glauca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festuca_glauca) · [Pennisetum alopecuroides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennisetum_alopecuroides) · [Calamagrostis × acutiflora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamagrostis_%C3%97_acutiflora) · [Hakonechloa macra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hakonechloa) · [Nassella tenuissima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nassella_tenuissima) · [Carex oshimensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carex_oshimensis) · [Cortaderia selloana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cortaderia_selloana)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Blue Fescue | *Festuca glauca* | 20–30cm | Summer | At pot level | Compact, evergreen, steely-blue needle-like foliage; excellent for rockeries, edging and containers |
| Dwarf Fountain Grass | *Pennisetum alopecuroides* 'Hameln' | 50–60cm | Late summer–autumn | At pot level | Soft, bottlebrush-like flower spikes; deciduous; a compact form of Chinese fountain grass |
| Feather Reed Grass | *Calamagrostis* × *acutiflora* 'Karl Foerster' | 1.2–1.5m | Early–midsummer | At pot level | Strictly upright, non-flopping habit; excellent architectural structure; one of the earliest grasses to flower |
| Japanese Forest Grass | *Hakonechloa macra* | 30–40cm | Late summer | At pot level | Arching, bright green (or gold in 'Aureola') foliage; one of the few grasses that thrives in part shade |
| Mexican Feather Grass | *Nassella tenuissima* (syn. *Stipa tenuissima*) | 40–60cm | Summer | At pot level | Fine, hair-like evergreen foliage in constant motion in the slightest breeze; self-seeds readily |
| Evergold Sedge | *Carex oshimensis* 'Evergold' | 25–35cm | Insignificant | At pot level | Evergreen, cream-and-green striped foliage; tolerates damper soil and part shade better than the others |
| Japanese Blood Grass | *Imperata cylindrica* 'Red Baron' | 40–60cm | Insignificant | At pot level | Upright blades, green at the base flushing brilliant blood-red at the tips, deepening through summer into autumn; slowly spreading rather than clump-forming |
| Pampas Grass | *Cortaderia selloana* | 2–3m | Late summer–autumn | At pot level | Much larger and more architectural than the others in this table — a huge fountain of arching, razor-edged foliage topped by tall, feathery, silvery-white plumes; suits only a large garden with space to spare; ⚠️ sharp, saw-toothed leaf edges, wear gloves |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for Blue Fescue, Dwarf Fountain Grass, Feather Reed Grass, Mexican Feather Grass and Japanese Blood Grass — flowering, upright habit and red colouring all suffer in shade. Pampas Grass also needs full sun. Japanese Forest Grass and Evergold Sedge are the exceptions, both doing well in partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20cm–1.5m tall depending on species (see table), 20cm–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most prefer light, moist but free-draining, moderately fertile soil, pH 6.0–7.5. Evergold Sedge and Japanese Forest Grass are more tolerant of consistently moist, even slightly heavier soil than the others, which need sharper drainage, especially over winter.

**Soil prep:** For Blue Fescue, Feather Reed Grass, Mexican Feather Grass and Japanese Blood Grass on clay: improve with 25–30% horticultural grit, since good winter drainage is essential to prevent rot at the crown. For Japanese Forest Grass and Evergold Sedge: mix clay with compost, without the need for heavy grit. Dwarf Fountain Grass sits between the two, appreciating reasonable drainage but not as fussy as the true drought-tolerant grasses.

**Planting depth:** At pot level for all eight, planted at the same depth as in the nursery pot.

**Spacing:** Blue Fescue and Evergold Sedge: 25–35cm between plants. Japanese Forest Grass, Mexican Feather Grass and Japanese Blood Grass: 30–45cm. Dwarf Fountain Grass: 45–50cm. Feather Reed Grass: 45–60cm, given its height. Pampas Grass: 2–3m — in a different league entirely, this is a genuinely large, architectural grass, not suited to a small border.

**Fertiliser:** Very little needed for most — over-feeding produces lax, floppy growth, especially in Blue Fescue and Mexican Feather Grass. A light spring mulch of garden compost is usually sufficient. Feather Reed Grass and Dwarf Fountain Grass benefit from an occasional balanced feed in spring for the best flowering display.

**Companions:** The seven smaller grasses combine beautifully with late-summer perennials such as sedum, echinacea and rudbeckia, and with spring bulbs planted through the clumps for earlier interest. Evergold Sedge and Japanese Forest Grass pair especially well with hostas, ferns and other shade-loving foliage plants. Japanese Blood Grass looks especially dramatic backlit by low sun, or planted with dark-leaved companions such as Black Mondo Grass. Pampas Grass is best given its own space as a solo specimen rather than crowded by other planting, given its ultimate size.

**Pruning:** Cut deciduous types (Dwarf Fountain Grass, Feather Reed Grass, Japanese Blood Grass) back hard to a few centimetres above the ground in late winter, before new growth starts. Evergreen types (Blue Fescue, Mexican Feather Grass, Japanese Forest Grass, Evergold Sedge) just need old or damaged foliage combed out or lightly trimmed in spring — do not cut hard back. Pampas Grass: cut back hard in late winter/early spring to remove old foliage before new growth starts — always wear thick gloves and long sleeves, since the leaf edges are sharp enough to cut skin.

**West of Ireland:** For a small west of Ireland garden, Blue Fescue, Evergold Sedge and Mexican Feather Grass are the best-behaved and most compact choices — all clump-forming rather than running, so they stay well mannered in a small border or container. Japanese Forest Grass is an excellent choice for a shadier corner where few other grasses would thrive, while Feather Reed Grass gives valuable upright structure and winter interest (its dried flower spikes persist attractively) without becoming floppy in the region's wind and rain. The mild, moist Atlantic climate generally suits ornamental grasses well, though the free-draining species (Blue Fescue, Mexican Feather Grass, Feather Reed Grass) are more prone to winter rot on heavy clay than the moisture-tolerant Evergold Sedge and Japanese Forest Grass. Pampas Grass is reliably hardy and tolerant of coastal wind here too, but its sheer eventual size (2–3m tall and wide) makes it suitable only for a large garden with space to spare — the odd one out in this table, included for comparison rather than as a small-garden recommendation.

---

### P29. Pasqueflower *(Pulsatilla vulgaris)*


***Russian:*** *Прострел обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Pasqueflower — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulsatilla_vulgaris)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — essential. Hates shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Excellent drainage, pH 6.5–8.0. Hates wet clay. Raised bed or pot essential.

**Soil prep:** 40–50% grit/compost mix in raised bed or container.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Do not disturb once established.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Virtually no feeding needed. Adapted to lean limestone soils. Nitrogen causes floppy, non-flowering growth. Grit mulch on the surface is more beneficial than any fertiliser.

**Companions:** Eryngium, Dianthus, Scabiosa, Achillea, ornamental grasses in a gravel garden. Thyme and other rock garden plants nearby.

---

### P30. Penstemon *(Penstemon spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Пенстемон*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Penstemon — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penstemon)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 45–75cm tall depending on variety, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging, especially in winter.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% horticultural grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring.

**Companions:** Salvias, achillea, ornamental grasses — classic sunny border companions with similar drainage needs.

**West of Ireland:** A generally short-lived but free-flowering perennial with tubular, foxglove-like flowers in a wide range of colours from summer into autumn. Winter wet on heavy clay is the main risk here — good drainage and a sheltered spot noticeably improve its hardiness and longevity.

---

### P31. Peonies *(Paeonia spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Пион*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Peonies — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paeonia)

| Cultivar | Colour | Fragrance | Height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sarah Bernhardt | Soft, pastel pink | Strong | 75–90cm | Bred by Lemoine, 1906; huge, fully double blooms; one of the most widely grown and dependable cut-flower peonies ever raised |
| Festiva Maxima | White with crimson flecks at the centre | Strong | 85–95cm | Bred 1851, one of the oldest peonies still widely grown; RHS AGM; early-flowering, good heat tolerance |
| Duchesse de Nemours | Milky white with a creamy-yellow/lemon centre | Strong, often described as the finest of the white peonies | 75–90cm | Bred by Calot, 1856; RHS AGM 1993; fully double, globe-shaped blooms; a classic against which other white peonies are measured |
| Kansas | Vibrant carmine-red to watermelon-red, does not fade in sun | Light to moderate | 75–90cm | Introduced 1940; American Peony Society Gold Medal 1957; very strong, rigid stems needing little staking; excellent, long-lasting cut flower |
| Bowl of Beauty | Rose-pink guard petals surrounding a dense centre of creamy-yellow staminodes | Light | 80–90cm | Bred by Kelway, 1949; RHS AGM; a Japanese-type peony, visually quite different from the fully double cultivars above — single row of large petals rather than a dense pompom |

**Light:** ☀️ to 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. At least 4–6 hours for good flowering.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 60–90cm spread.

**Spacing:** 90cm between plants

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, fertile, pH 6.5–7.0.

**Planting depth (herbaceous):** ⚠️ Eyes MAX 2.5–5cm below soil. Too deep = no flowers. The most commonly made planting mistake.

**Planting depth (tree peony):** Graft union 10–15cm below surface.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in early spring as red shoots emerge. High-potash feed (tomato feed) as buds develop. Annual well-rotted compost mulch, keeping mulch away from crown.

**Companions:** Alliums (flower as peonies fade — elegant combination). Salvia, irises, delphiniums in mixed border. Roses, foxgloves, geraniums. Avoid planting near taller shade-casting plants.

---

### P32. Perennial Flax *(Linum perenne)*


***Russian:*** *Лён многолетний*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Linum / Perennial Flax — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linum_perenne)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil, tolerant of poor/dry conditions; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes winter wet.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; avoid rich soil, which shortens the plant's lifespan.

**Companions:** Achillea (yarrow), lavender, other sun-loving, drought-tolerant border perennials.

**West of Ireland:** Delicate, sky-blue flowers open fresh each morning through summer on airy, wiry stems. Can be short-lived, especially where drainage is poor — a raised or gritty spot extends its life considerably on Irish clay.

---

### P33. Pheasant's Eye *(Adonis vernalis / A. amurensis)*


***Russian:*** *Адонис весенний*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Adonis — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adonis_(plant))

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade (some afternoon shade in summer, when the plant is dormant, is fine).

**Height/Spread:** 30–40cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, humus-rich soil; neutral to slightly alkaline, pH 6.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit for good drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; ⚠️ toxic

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, hellebores and other very early spring flowers; goes dormant by summer, so pair with later perennials to fill the gap.

**West of Ireland:** One of the earliest perennials to flower, with bright yellow, buttercup-like blooms often appearing in late winter. Slow to establish and resents disturbance, so choose its position carefully and leave undisturbed once planted.

⚠️ All parts are toxic if ingested.

---

### P34. Pincushion Flower *(Scabiosa columbaria)*


***Russian:*** *Скабиоза голубиная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Scabiosa columbaria — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scabiosa_columbaria)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–8.0. Tolerates chalky/alkaline ground well.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed.

**Companions:** Achillea, ornamental grasses — classic dry, sunny border companions; excellent for pollinators and as a cut flower.

**West of Ireland:** A long-flowering, easy perennial with delicate lavender-blue pincushion-like flowers from early summer into autumn if deadheaded. Needs sharp drainage to thrive reliably on Irish clay; genuinely excellent for bees and butterflies.

---

### P35. Pinks & Sweet Williams *(Dianthus spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Sweet William](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianthus_barbatus) · [Carnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianthus_caryophyllus) · [Chinese Pink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianthus_chinensis) · [Maiden Pink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianthus_deltoides) · [Garden Pink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dianthus_plumarius)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sweet William | *D. barbatus* | 30–60cm | Early summer | At pot level; sow June–July (biennial) | Classic biennial cottage-garden pink with dense flat flowerheads; self-sows readily |
| Carnation (Border Carnation) | *D. caryophyllus* | 45–60cm | Summer (June–August) | At pot level | Clove-scented double flowers on a woody-based perennial; tolerates maritime exposure and salt-laden air |
| Chinese Pink | *D. chinensis* | 15–30cm | Summer | At pot level, or sow 3mm deep in spring | Usually grown as an annual or short-lived biennial bedding plant rather than a long-term perennial |
| Maiden Pink | *D. deltoides* | 15–20cm | Late spring–summer | At pot level | Low, mat-forming groundcover species; RHS AGM; good for rockeries and path edges |
| Garden Pink | *D. plumarius* | 25–35cm | Early–midsummer | At pot level | The classic perennial cottage-garden pink; many named cultivars (e.g. 'Doris', 'Mrs Sinkins') |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for all — at least 6 hours daily. Sweet William tolerates partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–75cm tall depending on species (see table), 25–35cm spread.

**Soil needs:** All prefer neutral to slightly alkaline, well-drained soil, pH roughly 5.8–7.5 depending on species, and dislike waterlogged clay. Good air circulation helps avoid root rot and fungal disease. Carnation is unusually tolerant of salt-laden coastal air.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and 20–30% horticultural grit for all species — good drainage matters more than exact pH.

**Planting depth:** All at pot level, planted at the same depth as in the nursery pot. Sweet William: sow June–July as a biennial to flower the following year. Chinese Pink: can also be surface-sown (3mm deep) direct in spring. See table above for spacing guidance per species.

**Spacing:** Sweet William and Carnation: 30–35cm and 45–60cm between plants respectively. Chinese Pink: 15–20cm. Maiden Pink: low groundcover, plant 20–30cm apart. Garden Pink: 25–35cm.

**Fertiliser:** Routine feeding is not required for any of them. A well-balanced feed in early spring promotes new growth and flowering; perennial types (Carnation, Garden Pink, Maiden Pink) can also be fed once in late spring with an organic fertiliser such as comfrey or chicken pellets. Container-grown plants of any species benefit from a fortnightly liquid feed while in flower.

**Companions:** Roses, stocks, alliums, lavender, thyme and campanula in a well-drained sunny border; foxgloves, forget-me-nots, wallflowers and pansies alongside Sweet William in particular. Other annual bedding such as cornflower and love-in-a-mist suit Chinese Pink in a cottage-style summer border. Maiden Pink and Garden Pink both combine well with Phlox subulata, Iberis sempervirens and sempervivum at the front of a rockery.

**West of Ireland:** The perennial and biennial types (Carnation, Garden Pink, Sweet William) find the region's wet winters the main challenge — a raised bed with gritty soil or a sheltered, south-facing spot gives the best results, and Sweet William in particular benefits from the mild winters, though it is still best treated as a biennial with fresh seed sown each year. Chinese Pink is best treated as an annual, replanted fresh each year for reliable colour. Maiden Pink is the easiest of the five, forming a trouble-free low mat once given sharp drainage.

---

### P101. Purpletop Vervain *(Verbena bonariensis)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Verbena bonariensis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verbena_bonariensis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–2m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moderately fertile, well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Adaptable to loam or sandy soils; good drought tolerance once established.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 20–25% grit for reliable drainage, especially over winter.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants; self-seeds freely once established.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed; balanced feed in spring is sufficient.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, echinacea, rudbeckia — classic naturalistic/prairie-style border companions; excellent for pollinators.

**West of Ireland:** A graceful, airy, architectural perennial with tall, wiry, branching stems topped by tight clusters of small violet-purple flowers from mid-summer through autumn — its "see-through" habit makes it a favourite for planting towards the front of a border without blocking the view behind. Self-seeds readily in the mild Irish climate; pinch back young stems in early summer for a bushier habit, and deadhead to manage self-seeding if it becomes too enthusiastic.

---

### P36. Sea Holly *(Eryngium spp.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cuilleann trá · Синеголовник*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Eryngium — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eryngium)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Very free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes rich, wet ground — genuinely thrives on poor, sandy or coastal soil.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay heavily with 30–40% grit; raised bed or gravel garden strongly recommended.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; deep taproot resents disturbance/division once established.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** None needed — thrives on lean soil.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, achillea — classic dry gravel-garden companions; excellent for pollinators and dried arrangements.

**West of Ireland:** An architectural, spiny-leaved perennial with metallic blue thistle-like flowerheads in summer, genuinely happy in coastal, salt-exposed, sharply-drained conditions — an excellent choice for an exposed sunny spot on the Atlantic coast, provided the ground is not heavy wet clay.

---

### P24. Sea Lavender (Statice) *(Limonium spp.)*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Limonium sinuatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limonium_sinuatum) · [Limonium vulgare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limonium_vulgare) · [Limonium gmelinii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limonium_gmelini)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Statice | *L. sinuatum* | 30–45cm | Summer–early autumn | Short-lived perennial/biennial usually grown as an annual; wide colour range (violet, lavender, pink, rose, orange, yellow, white); the classic dried/cut-flower statice |
| Common Sea Lavender | *L. vulgare* | 30–40cm | August–September | Native to Atlantic coasts of Europe including Britain; hardy perennial; lavender-lilac to pink-mauve flowers; a genuine salt-marsh specialist |
| Gmelin's Sea Lavender | *L. gmelinii* | 50–90cm | Midsummer–early autumn | Hardy clump-forming perennial; smoky-blue to lavender-purple flowers; semi-evergreen basal rosette; drought-tolerant once established |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for all three.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining soil; pH 6.0–8.0. All three are naturally coastal/salt-marsh plants and are genuinely salt-tolerant. *L. sinuatum* and *L. gmelinii* tolerate poor, sandy or stony ground well; *L. vulgare* is more of a true salt-marsh specialist and appreciates a touch more moisture than the other two.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% horticultural grit; a raised bed or gravel garden suits all three well.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level for all three; all resent root disturbance once established, so choose the final position carefully.

**Spacing:** *L. sinuatum*: 30–45cm between plants. *L. vulgare*: 30–40cm between plants. *L. gmelinii*: 45–60cm between plants, given its larger ultimate size.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed for any of the three — they thrive on lean, poor soil; over-feeding produces lax growth.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, achillea, sedum — classic gravel-garden and coastal-planting companions; all three have airy sprays excellent for cutting and drying.

**West of Ireland:** Three tough, drought- and salt-tolerant plants with sprays of small, papery flowers from summer into autumn, all genuinely useful for an exposed coastal west of Ireland garden. *L. sinuatum* (Statice) is the classic florist's cut-and-dried flower, usually treated as an annual here; *L. vulgare* is the true native-range coastal species, most at home in a damp, salty spot; *L. gmelinii* is the largest and most robust of the three, a reliable hardy perennial border plant. Not to be confused with Siberian Statice *(Goniolimon tataricum)*, already in this guide, which is a related but distinct genus with a similar airy, dried-flower habit.

---

### P37. Siberian Statice *(Goniolimon tataricum)*


***Russian:*** *Кермек татарский*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Siberian Statice — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goniolimon_tataricum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 45–60cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil, tolerant of poor/dry ground; pH 6.0–7.5. Dislikes winter wet.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with grit for good drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; deep taproot, so choose the final position with care.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; avoid rich soil.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, achillea, sedum — classic gravel-garden or dried-flower border companions.

**West of Ireland:** Airy sprays of small white/papery flowers held well above a basal rosette, popular for drying. Best given a raised, gritty, well-drained spot given the west of Ireland's high rainfall, as it is more prone to winter losses on flat wet clay than many border perennials.

---

### P38. Slender Vervain *(Verbena rigida)*


***Russian:*** *Вербена жёсткая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Vervain — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verbena_officinalis)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 1–1.5m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of poor, dry conditions once established.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Little required; avoid rich soil, which produces floppy growth.

**Companions:** Lavender, catmint, ornamental grasses — classic gravel-garden and pollinator-border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A native wildflower with wiry, airy stems of tiny lilac flowers through summer, valuable for pollinators, and long used in traditional herbal medicine. Self-seeds gently; best in a sunny, well-drained spot rather than heavy wet clay.

---

### P39. Sneezeweed *(Helenium autumnale)*


***Russian:*** *Гелениум осенний*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Helenium autumnale — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helenium_autumnale)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 90cm–1.2m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well provided it isn't permanently waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring.

**Companions:** Rudbeckia, ornamental grasses, sedum — classic late-summer border companions.

**West of Ireland:** A reliable, easy perennial with daisy-like flowers in warm reds, oranges and yellows from late summer into autumn, valuable for extending colour late in the season. Tolerates the region's moist soil well; divide every few years to keep it vigorous.

---

### P40. Sneezewort *(Achillea ptarmica 'The Pearl')*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus corráin · Тысячелистник птармика*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Yarrow — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achillea_millefolium)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — at least 6–8 hours. Tolerates partial shade but flowers less freely and becomes leggy.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Loamy soil is ideal, but yarrow tolerates clay soil if it drains well. Loosen soil 12–15 inches deep and mix in 2–4 inches of compost before planting. Avoid overly rich soil; too much fertility can cause floppy stems. pH 5.5–7.0. Very adaptable — will grow in poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Loosen soil to 30–40cm depth and mix in compost before planting. Do not add heavy nitrogen; this causes floppy stems. On clay, add 15–20% grit to improve drainage.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; bare-root crowns with buds 2–3cm below soil surface. Space 30–60cm apart.

**Spacing:** 30–60cm between plants; divide every 2–3 yrs

**Fertiliser:** Apply a thin layer of compost each spring. Additional fertiliser is rarely necessary. Over-feeding produces tall, floppy plants that fall over. Yarrow needs little or no regular applications of fertiliser in most garden soils. Mulching plants in borders with well-rotted manure or garden compost each spring should be sufficient.

**Companions:** Yarrow helps deter unwanted pests, attracts native insects and predators like those that manage aphids and caterpillars, and works quietly to improve soil quality by replenishing nutrients. Yarrow pairs beautifully with weigela and hydrangea. Also excellent with roses, delphiniums, salvias, ornamental grasses, and echinacea. One of the best pollinator plants — flat-topped flowers are perfect landing platforms for hoverflies, butterflies and bees. ⚠️ Can spread aggressively in good garden soil — divide every 2–3 years.

**Pot size:** 30cm diameter, 25cm deep container; multipurpose compost with 20% grit.

**West of Ireland:** Native *A. millefolium* grows wild throughout Ireland and is extremely well adapted to Irish conditions. Tolerates the wet, windy west coast. Excellent for naturalistic planting, wildflower meadow schemes, and cottage gardens.

---


---

### P41. Society Garlic *(Tulbaghia violacea)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Tulbaghia violacea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulbaghia_violacea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 25–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 25–30% horticultural grit; a raised bed or large container gives the most reliable results on Irish clay.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 25–30cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season.

**Companions:** Lavender, rosemary and other sun-loving, well-drained border companions; said to help deter aphids nearby, like other alliums.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Grows very well in a container, which also makes winter protection straightforward.

**West of Ireland:** An evergreen, clump-forming, garlic-scented perennial from South Africa with grey-green strappy leaves and clusters of star-shaped lilac flowers held on tall stems through summer into autumn. Borderline hardy (RHS H3, roughly to -5°C) — reliable outdoors only in the mildest, most sheltered coastal west of Ireland gardens; elsewhere, best grown in a container that can be moved to a frost-free spot for winter, or given a thick dry mulch if left in the ground.

---

### P42. Stocks *(Matthiola incana / M. longipetala)*


***Russian:*** *Левкой седой*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Stocks — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthiola_incana)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Best fragrance in the evening in a sheltered spot.

**Height/Spread:** 30–90cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, pH 6.5–7.5. Slightly alkaline preferred.

**Soil prep:** Compost and 20% grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; seeds surface-sown.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser at planting. Liquid tomato feed fortnightly once buds appear. Moderate feeder — do not overfeed with nitrogen.

**Companions:** Wallflowers, sweet William, pansies. Place near seating areas or windows for exceptional evening fragrance.

---

### P43. Toadflax *(Linaria vulgaris)*


***Russian:*** *Льнянка обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Toadflax — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linaria_vulgaris)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–90cm tall, 30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained soil, tolerant of poor/dry ground; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — this native wildflower is unfussy; avoid over-rich soil, which encourages excess soft growth and spread.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level, or sow seed on the surface.

**Spacing:** 20–30cm between plants; spreads readily

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Ox-eye daisy, yarrow, other native wildflower meadow companions.

**West of Ireland:** A native wildflower with snapdragon-like yellow and orange flowers on upright spikes through summer, valuable for bees. Spreads by rhizomes and self-seeds readily, so best suited to a wildflower area or informal bank rather than a tidy border, where it can be to be kept in check.

---

### P44. Yarrow *(Achillea millefolium)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Athair thalún · Тысячелистник обыкновенный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Yarrow — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achillea_millefolium)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — at least 6–8 hours. Tolerates partial shade but flowers less freely and becomes leggy.

**Height/Spread:** 30–60cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Loamy soil is ideal, but yarrow tolerates clay soil if it drains well. Loosen soil 12–15 inches deep and mix in 2–4 inches of compost before planting. Avoid overly rich soil; too much fertility can cause floppy stems. pH 5.5–7.0. Very adaptable — will grow in poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Loosen soil to 30–40cm depth and mix in compost before planting. Do not add heavy nitrogen; this causes floppy stems. On clay, add 15–20% grit to improve drainage.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; bare-root crowns with buds 2–3cm below soil surface. Space 30–60cm apart.

**Spacing:** 30–60cm between plants; divide every 2–3 yrs

**Fertiliser:** Apply a thin layer of compost each spring. Additional fertiliser is rarely necessary. Over-feeding produces tall, floppy plants that fall over. Yarrow needs little or no regular applications of fertiliser in most garden soils. Mulching plants in borders with well-rotted manure or garden compost each spring should be sufficient.

**Companions:** Yarrow helps deter unwanted pests, attracts native insects and predators like those that manage aphids and caterpillars, and works quietly to improve soil quality by replenishing nutrients. Yarrow pairs beautifully with weigela and hydrangea. Also excellent with roses, delphiniums, salvias, ornamental grasses, and echinacea. One of the best pollinator plants — flat-topped flowers are perfect landing platforms for hoverflies, butterflies and bees. ⚠️ Can spread aggressively in good garden soil — divide every 2–3 years.

**Pot size:** 30cm diameter, 25cm deep container; multipurpose compost with 20% grit.

**West of Ireland:** Native *A. millefolium* grows wild throughout Ireland and is extremely well adapted to Irish conditions. Tolerates the wet, windy west coast. Excellent for naturalistic planting, wildflower meadow schemes, and cottage gardens.

---


---

## GROUP S: SHADE & WOODLAND PLANTS

### S1. Asian Bleeding Heart *(Lamprocapnos spectabilis, syn. Dicentra spectabilis)*


***Russian:*** *Дицентра великолепная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Lamprocapnos spectabilis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamprocapnos_spectabilis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well given reasonable drainage.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost/leaf mould.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, hellebores — classic woodland-edge companions that fill in as it dies back.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Goes dormant and disappears completely by midsummer — plant where later-emerging neighbours will fill the gap.

**West of Ireland:** A classic, much-loved woodland perennial with arching stems of pink-and-white heart-shaped flowers in spring. Thrives in the mild, moist Irish climate given part shade; its complete summer dormancy is worth planning around when siting it.

---

### S2. Auricula *(Primula auricula)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus na mbanríon · Первоцвет ушковый*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Auricula — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_auricula)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade; protect from strong midday sun.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 20–25cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, humus-rich soil; neutral to slightly alkaline, pH 6.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay generously with grit and compost; best in a raised bed, trough or pot given its need for sharp drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; do not bury.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season, stopping in winter.

**Companions:** Other alpine/trough plants such as saxifrage and sempervivum; traditionally displayed in individual pots on a shelf ("auricula theatre").

**West of Ireland:** Alpine primula relative, best grown in pots or a raised trough rather than open clay, since winter wet is the main threat. Divide congested clumps every 2–3 years after flowering.

---

### S3. Common Dog Violet *(Viola riviniana)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Fanaigse · Фиалка Ривинуса*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Common Dog Violet — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viola_riviniana)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade; tolerates full sun to full shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Most moist, well-drained soils including clay; pH 5.5–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — very adaptable native woodland-floor plant.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Primroses, wood sorrel, ferns — classic native woodland-floor groundcover; the larval food plant for several fritillary butterflies.

**West of Ireland:** Easy, unfussy native wildflower with violet-blue flowers in spring, valuable groundcover under trees and shrubs, and important for wildlife. Self-seeds gently to form drifts.

---

### S4. Coral Bells *(Heuchera spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Гейхера*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Coral Bells — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuchera)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred; purple/dark-leaved varieties tolerate more sun, green/lime varieties prefer more shade.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil, rich in organic matter; pH 6.0–7.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit for drainage — Heuchera dislikes waterlogging, especially in winter.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level — do not bury the crown, which can cause rot.

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season. Light annual mulch with compost, keeping it away from the crown.

**Companions:** Hostas, astilbe, ferns, hellebores — classic shade/part-shade border and edging companions; excellent foliage contrast plant.

**West of Ireland:** Reliable, low-maintenance evergreen/semi-evergreen foliage perennial. Divide congested clumps every 3 years in spring or autumn to keep vigorous.

---

### S5. Cowslip *(Primula veris)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Bainne bó bleachtáin · Первоцвет весенний*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Auricula — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_auricula)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade; protect from strong midday sun.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 20–25cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, humus-rich soil; neutral to slightly alkaline, pH 6.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay generously with grit and compost; best in a raised bed, trough or pot given its need for sharp drainage.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; do not bury.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season, stopping in winter.

**Companions:** Other alpine/trough plants such as saxifrage and sempervivum; traditionally displayed in individual pots on a shelf ("auricula theatre").

**West of Ireland:** Alpine primula relative, best grown in pots or a raised trough rather than open clay, since winter wet is the main threat. Divide congested clumps every 2–3 years after flowering.

---

### S6. Dusky Cranesbill *(Geranium phaeum)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Crobh odhar · Герань тёмно-бурая*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Geranium phaeum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geranium_phaeum)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 60–80cm tall, 45–60cm spread.
