**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of clay and dry shade once established.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 40–45cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed once established.

**Companions:** Hellebores, ferns, foxgloves — classic woodland-edge hardy geranium companions.

**West of Ireland:** An easy, tough hardy geranium with distinctive deep maroon-purple, reflexed flowers in late spring, tolerating both sun and quite deep, dry shade once settled in. Self-seeds gently; genuinely one of the most trouble-free perennials for a mixed shade border here.

---

### S7. Epimedium (Barrenwort) *(Epimedium spp.)*


📷 **Reference photos:** [Epimedium × versicolor 'Sulphureum'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epimedium) · [Epimedium × perralchicum 'Frohnleiten'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epimedium) · [Epimedium × rubrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epimedium)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *E.* × *versicolor* 'Sulphureum' | *E.* × *versicolor* 'Sulphureum' | 30cm | Spring (April–May) | Crown at soil level | Pale sulphur-yellow flowers; semi-evergreen heart-shaped leaves flushed bronze when young; RHS AGM |
| *E.* × *perralchicum* 'Rose Queen' | *E.* × *perralchicum* 'Rose Queen' | 30–40cm | Spring (April–May) | Crown at soil level | Rose-pink, spurred flowers; vigorous, semi-evergreen groundcover, one of the toughest and most reliable Epimediums |
| *E.* × *rubrum* | *E.* × *rubrum* | 20–30cm | Spring (April) | Crown at soil level | Crimson-red, white-spurred flowers; young foliage flushed bright red, ageing green with red-tinted margins in autumn |

**Light:** 🌥️ to 🌑 Partial to full (dry) shade — one of the very best perennials for difficult dry shade under trees and shrubs.

**Height/Spread:** 20–40cm tall depending on species (see table), 30–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably drained soil, including clay; pH 5.5–7.5. Remarkably tolerant of dry shade once established, though it establishes best with reasonable moisture in its first year or two.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; no special drainage measures needed.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level for all three.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed once established — a light balanced feed in spring for the first couple of seasons helps establishment.

**Companions:** Ferns, hellebores, hostas, snowdrops and other dry-shade or woodland-edge perennials.

**Pruning:** Cut old, tatty foliage back hard in late winter, just before the new flower spikes and leaves emerge, to show off the flowers clearly and refresh the display for the coming season.

**West of Ireland:** All three are tough, spreading (but not invasive) semi-evergreen to evergreen groundcover perennials with delicate, spurred spring flowers held above (or just before) attractive heart-shaped foliage. Genuinely one of the best plant groups for the difficult dry shade under mature trees, where the region's rainfall rarely fully reaches the soil — once established, all three cope with remarkably little summer moisture. A superb, low-maintenance choice for a woodland-edge or shaded border in a west of Ireland garden.

---

### S8. Forget-me-nots *(Myosotis sylvatica)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus míonla · Незабудка лесная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Myosotis sylvatica — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myosotis_sylvatica)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant and easy.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; not fussy.

**Planting depth:** At pot level, or sow seed on the surface in late spring/summer to flower the following year (usually grown as a biennial).

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; self-seeds freely.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed.

**Companions:** Tulips, wallflowers, foxgloves — a classic spring bedding partner, especially with spring bulbs.

**West of Ireland:** A cheerful, easy biennial with sprays of small sky-blue flowers in spring, self-seeding readily to return year after year once established. Fully hardy and thrives in the mild, moist Irish climate.

---

### S9. Foxgloves *(Digitalis purpurea and perennial species)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Lus mór · Наперстянка пурпурная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Foxgloves — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digitalis_purpurea)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun if soil stays moist.

**Height/Spread:** 1–2m tall, 30–45cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Nutrient-rich, moist, pH 5.5–7.5. Native to Ireland.

**Soil prep:** Add compost. Very adaptable. Self-seeding.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; seeds sown on surface (need light to germinate).

**Spacing:** 30–45cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Low-maintenance — rarely needs feeding. Light balanced fertiliser in spring for vigorous second-year plants. Bark mulch to retain moisture.

**Companions:** Ferns, astilbe, hostas in shaded beds. With roses, alliums, and delphiniums in sunnier borders. Superb companion as they attract bumblebees very effectively.

---

### S10. Hellebore (Lenten Rose) *(Helleborus spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Морозник*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hellebore — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helleborus)

**Light:** 🌥️ to 🌑 Partial to full shade. Ideal under deciduous trees or shrubs. Avoid full sun which scorches leaves.

**Height/Spread:** 30–45cm tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Humus-rich, fertile, moisture-retentive, pH 6.0–8.0. Excellent on clay.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost. Annual compost or leaf mould mulch.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; crown just at or slightly below soil surface.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release in early spring after clearing old foliage. Annual compost mulch after flowering — most effective treatment. Takes 2–3 years to settle after planting; do not disturb once established.

**Companions:** Snowdrops (January–March together — one of the great winter garden combinations). Cyclamen coum, pulmonaria, early daffodils. Hostas and ferns alongside in summer.

---

### S11. Hosta / Plantain Lily *(Hosta spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Хоста*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Hosta sieboldiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosta_sieboldiana) · [Hosta 'Undulata Albomarginata'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosta) · [Hosta 'Frances Williams'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosta)

| Variety | Height | Leaf colour | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| *H. sieboldiana* 'Elegans' | 60–90cm | Large, rounded, deeply veined blue-grey | RHS AGM; one of the largest and most dramatic hosta leaves; slug-resistant thick substance |
| *H.* × *tardiana* 'Halcyon' | 40cm | Frosty, glaucous blue-grey, heart-shaped when mature | RHS AGM; one of the best and most popular blue-leaved hostas; pale lavender bell-shaped flowers in summer |
| *H.* 'Undulata Albomarginata' | 30–45cm | Wavy, twisted mid-green leaves with a broad creamy-white margin | Vigorous and reliable; one of the most widely grown variegated hostas |
| *H.* 'Shadowland Diamond Lake' | 40–50cm | Rounded, deep blue leaves | Compact, slug-resistant, holds its blue colour well through summer |
| *H.* 'Frances Williams' | 60–75cm | Blue-green, heavily puckered, with an irregular gold-yellow margin | RHS AGM; a classic, extremely popular large variegated hosta |
| *H.* 'Patriot' | 40–50cm | Dark green with a crisp, wide white margin | Fast-growing, reliable, strong colour contrast |

**Light:** 🌥️ to 🌑 Partial to full shade. Golden and yellow-leaved types tolerate more sun; blue-leaved varieties need more shade — their glaucous "bloom" fades in strong sun.

**Height/Spread:** 10–90cm tall depending on variety (see table), 30–90cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, fertile soil; pH 5.5–7.5. Grows well on clay.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; crown just at soil surface.

**Spacing:** 30–90cm depending on variety — check the ultimate spread, since some (like 'Sieboldiana Elegans') become very large.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release granular in spring. Balanced liquid feed monthly from April through midsummer. Do not feed after August. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Astilbe, ferns, hellebores, bergenia. Snowdrops and winter aconites bloom in the gaps before hostas emerge in spring.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Slugs and snails are the main challenge in the moist west of Ireland climate — wool pellets, copper tape (on pots), or nematode treatments are essential companions. Thicker-leaved varieties (e.g. 'Sieboldiana Elegans') show noticeably more slug resistance than thin-leaved types.

**West of Ireland:** The classic genus for a shaded Irish border, valued above all for handsome, often large and boldly variegated foliage rather than its relatively modest lily-like summer flowers. The mild, consistently moist Atlantic climate suits hostas very well — the main obstacle to a good display is slug and snail damage rather than the weather, so factor in some form of control from the moment new shoots emerge in spring.

---

### S12. Lily of the Valley *(Convallaria majalis)*


***Russian:*** *Ландыш майский*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Convallaria — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convallaria_majalis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20cm tall, 30cm+ spread (spreads by rhizome).

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well if not waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost/leaf mould.

**Planting depth:** Rhizome ("pip") 2–3cm deep, growing point up, in autumn.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants; ⚠️ toxic, spreads

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual leaf mould mulch.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, hellebores — classic shaded woodland-floor groundcover.

**West of Ireland:** Fragrant white bell flowers in late spring, spreading by rhizomes to form good groundcover under trees and shrubs over time — can be vigorous once established, so give it room or a contained bed if a tidy border is wanted.

⚠️ All parts are toxic if ingested.

---

### S13. Monkshood *(Aconitum napellus)*


***Russian:*** *Борец клобучковый (аконит)*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Aconitum / Monkshood — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aconitum_napellus)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full sun; prefers cooler root conditions.

**Height/Spread:** 90cm–1.5m tall, 45–60cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates clay well if not waterlogged.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level.

**Spacing:** 45–60cm between plants; ⚠️ toxic, wear gloves

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Delphiniums, hostas, astilbe — classic cottage/damp border companions.

**West of Ireland:** Reliable, long-lived tall spikes of deep blue-purple hooded flowers in late summer.

⚠️ **All parts are highly toxic if ingested, and sap can irritate skin on contact — wear gloves when handling, and site away from children's play areas and vegetable beds.**

---

### S14. Native Irish Ferns

📷 **Reference photos:** [Male Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryopteris_filix-mas) · [Lady Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athyrium_filix-femina) · [Broad Buckler Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryopteris_dilatata) · [Hard Shield Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polystichum_aculeatum) · [Polypody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polypodium_vulgare) · [Royal Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osmunda_regalis) · [Deer Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blechnum_spicant) · [Hart's Tongue Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asplenium_scolopendrium)

| Fern | Latin Name | Light | Height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Male Fern | *Dryopteris filix-mas* | 🌥️–🌑 | 60–100cm | Most robust native; tolerates exposed positions |
| Lady Fern | *Athyrium filix-femina* | 🌥️ | 60–120cm | Prefers sheltered, moist shade |
| Broad Buckler Fern | *Dryopteris dilatata* | 🌥️–🌑 | 60–100cm | Glossy dark fronds; excellent in deep shade |
| Hard Shield Fern | *Polystichum aculeatum* | 🌥️ | 45–60cm | Evergreen; tolerates dry shade & coastal exposure |
| Polypody | *Polypodium vulgare* | 🌥️ | 15–40cm | Grows on walls and banks; evergreen |
| Royal Fern | *Osmunda regalis* | ☀️–🌥️ | 1–2m | Loves wet clay and boggy spots; spectacular |
| Deer Fern | *Blechnum spicant* | 🌥️–🌑 | 30–60cm | Evergreen; needs acidic soil (pH 4.5–6.0) |
| Hart's Tongue Fern | *Asplenium scolopendrium* | 🌥️–🌑 | 30–60cm | Evergreen; undivided strap-like fronds; tolerates lime/chalk unlike most ferns |

**Height/Spread:** See Height column above for each species; spread is generally 30–90cm depending on species, forming slowly spreading clumps.

**Soil needs:** Most: moist, humus-rich, acidic to neutral (pH 5.0–7.0). Deer fern: acidic conditions. Hart's Tongue Fern: unusually tolerant of lime/chalk for a fern, so also suits neutral to alkaline soil.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost. Extra moisture retention for Royal Fern and Lady Fern.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Crowns of Dryopteris and Osmunda should sit 7–12cm above soil.

**Spacing:** 30cm–2m between plants depending on species — see table above.

**Fertiliser:** Very low feeding requirements. Light balanced fertiliser in spring or annual compost top-dressing is sufficient. Do not overfeed. Bark mulch around base to retain moisture.

**Companions:** Hostas, astilbe, hellebores, snowdrops, bluebells. Native ferns under deciduous trees is the ideal naturalistic Irish planting. Japanese maple and bamboo make beautiful structural companions. Foxgloves self-seed among ferns magnificently.

**West of Ireland:** You are in prime fern territory. Royal Fern *(Osmunda regalis)* is spectacular in boggy west of Ireland conditions, and Hart's Tongue Fern's unusual undivided fronds and lime tolerance make it a useful, easy native for a shaded wall or bank.

---

### S15. Native Irish Mosses


📷 **Reference photo:** [Irish Native Mosses — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mosses_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade; most native mosses scorch in strong, direct sun.

**Height/Spread:** 1–5cm tall (mat-forming), spreading indefinitely.

**Spacing:** Establish as low patches; allow to spread and knit together naturally over 1–2 growing seasons.

**Soil/surface needs:** Consistently moist, humid conditions on soil, rock, tree bases, walls or fallen wood; most prefer slightly acid, humus-rich conditions, though some (like common tamarisk-moss) tolerate neutral ground.

**Establishing mosses:** Unlike flowering plants, mosses are not "planted" in the usual sense — they establish from spores or small transplanted clumps ("moss transplant") pressed onto a clean, consistently damp surface (soil, rock, log, or the shaded base of a wall) and kept moist while they knit in, generally over one growing season.

**Care:** No feeding — added fertiliser harms moss. Keep the area free of fallen leaf litter, which can smother it, and maintain humidity/shade; mosses generally do not need protection from cold.

**Companions:** Ferns, wood sorrel, primroses and other shaded, damp woodland-floor plants; mosses are a natural companion groundcover beneath the native ferns already in this guide.

**West of Ireland:** Ireland's mild, exceptionally damp Atlantic climate is superb for mosses generally, and the west of Ireland (especially Kerry's oceanic woodlands) is internationally important for its bryophyte (moss and liverwort) diversity. A shaded, damp corner, old wall, or fallen log left undisturbed will often colonise naturally with little help. A selection of common native species suited to garden conditions:

| Common name | Species | Typical habitat |
|---|---|---|
| Fox-tail Feather-moss | *Thamnobryum alopecurum* | Shaded rocks, streamsides, tree bases in humid woodland |
| Common Tamarisk-moss | *Thuidium tamariscinum* | Damp ground and rotting logs in woodland; tolerates neutral soils |
| Bank Haircap Moss | *Polytrichastrum formosum* | Shaded banks, ditches, woodland and tree stumps on acidic soils |
| Common Haircap | *Polytrichum commune* | Damp, acidic ground, bog margins and woodland |
| Swan's-neck Thyme-moss | *Mnium hornum* | Very common on tree bases, rotting wood and acidic woodland floor |
| Springy Turf-moss | *Rhytidiadelphus squarrosus* | Damp lawns, banks and open woodland; tolerant of some sun |

---

### S16. Non-Native Ornamental Ferns

📷 **Reference photos:** [Japanese Wood Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryopteris_erythrosora) · [Western Sword Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polystichum_munitum) · [Ostrich Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matteuccia_struthiopteris) · [Sunset Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryopteris) · [Fortune's Holly Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrtomium_fortunei) · [Koidzumi's Wood Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryopteris) · [Maidenhair Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiantum_venustum) · [Japanese Painted Fern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athyrium_niponicum)

| Fern | Latin Name | Light | Height | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Japanese Wood Fern | *Dryopteris erythrosora* | 🌥️–🌑 | 45–60cm | New fronds coppery-red maturing to green; semi-evergreen; acid soil (pH <6.0) |
| Western Sword Fern | *Polystichum munitum* | 🌥️–🌑 | 60–120cm | Robust evergreen; tolerates clay if moist; protect crown from winter wet |
| Ostrich Fern | *Matteuccia struthiopteris* | 🌥️–🌑, tolerates sun if kept moist | 1–1.8m | Deciduous, shuttlecock form; spreads by runners; excellent in a bog garden |
| Sunset Fern | *Dryopteris lepidopoda* | 🌥️–🌑 | 60–75cm | New fronds fiery orange-red maturing to olive-green; semi-evergreen |
| Fortune's Holly Fern (var. clivicola) | *Cyrtomium fortunei* var. *clivicola* | 🌥️–🌑 | 30–60cm | Evergreen; very cold-hardy; bold, glossy fronds; tolerates dry shade |
| Koidzumi's Wood Fern | *Dryopteris koidzumiana* | 🌥️–🌑 | 60–90cm | Deciduous/semi-evergreen; warm copper new growth; shade and clay tolerant |
| Maidenhair Fern (Himalayan) | *Adiantum venustum* | 🌥️–🌑 | 15–25cm | Not native, but genuinely hardy (RHS AGM); delicate black-stemmed fan-shaped fronds; spreads slowly into a mat |
| Japanese Painted Fern | *Athyrium niponicum* 'Metallicum' | 🌥️–🌑 | 30–45cm | Deciduous; metallic silver-grey fronds flushed purple-red along the midribs; RHS AGM; one of the most colourful shade ferns available |

**Height/Spread:** See Height column above for each species; spread is generally 30–90cm depending on species, forming slowly spreading clumps.

**Soil needs:** Most: moist, humus-rich, acidic to neutral (pH 5.0–7.0). *Dryopteris erythrosora*: acid soil (pH <6.0). *C. fortunei* var. *clivicola*: neutral to slightly alkaline tolerated (pH 6.0–8.0). *D. koidzumiana*: pH 5.5–7.0. *Adiantum venustum*: moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil, pH 5.5–7.0. Japanese Painted Fern: moist, humus-rich, slightly acidic soil, pH 5.5–6.5 — dislikes drying out, which dulls its metallic colouring.

**Soil prep:** Add generous compost. Extra moisture retention for Ostrich Fern.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Crowns should sit at or just below the soil surface. *Adiantum venustum* has a thin, creeping rhizome planted at pot level.

**Spacing:** 30cm–1.8m between plants depending on species — see table above.

**Fertiliser:** Very low feeding requirements. Light balanced fertiliser in spring or annual compost top-dressing is sufficient. Do not overfeed. Bark mulch around base to retain moisture.

**Companions:** Hostas, astilbe, hellebores, snowdrops, bluebells. Japanese maple and bamboo make beautiful structural companions.

**West of Ireland:** You are in prime fern territory here — the mild, wet Atlantic climate suits almost all of these species, and Ostrich Fern in particular is spectacular in a boggy corner.

**Notes on selected species:**

**Fortune's Holly Fern var. clivicola** (*Cyrtomium fortunei* var. *clivicola*): A particularly cold-hardy variety of Fortune's holly fern (hardy to -15°C or below), originating in Japan and Korea. Produces bold, glossy, deep green fronds with holly-leaf-shaped pinnae. Fully evergreen. One of the few ferns that genuinely tolerates dry shade — invaluable under trees where moisture competition is fierce, a challenge common in west of Ireland woodland gardens. Grows well in neutral to slightly alkaline soils as well as acidic conditions, giving it wider site flexibility than many ferns. Plant at pot level; the crown should sit at or just below the soil surface. Slug-resistant. Height 30–60cm; spread to 60cm. Pot size: ✅ 30–40cm / 15L+.

**Koidzumi's Wood Fern** *(Dryopteris koidzumiana)*: A Japanese species related to the common Male Fern, increasingly grown as a garden fern in the UK and Ireland. Produces upright, broadly lance-shaped fronds with attractive coppery-bronze new growth in spring, maturing to dark green. Semi-evergreen to deciduous depending on exposure; in the mild west of Ireland, usually semi-evergreen. Tolerates clay, moist soils, and shade well. Plant at pot level; crown at soil surface or just below. pH 5.5–7.0. Height 60–90cm; spread 60cm. Pot size: ✅ 40–50cm / 25–30L.

---

### S17. Primulas *(Primula spp.)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sabhaircín · Первоцвет*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Primroses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_vulgaris) · [Drumstick Primula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_denticulata) · [Candelabra Primula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primula_japonica)

| Species | Latin name | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Candelabra Primula | *P. japonica* | 30–60cm | Late spring–early summer | Crown at soil level | Tiered whorls of flowers in pink, red, purple or white; RHS AGM cultivars available; wants genuinely wet, pond-margin-type conditions; self-seeds into colonies |
| Drumstick Primula | *P. denticulata* | 20–45cm | Mid-spring–early summer (April–June) | Crown at soil level | Spherical "drumstick" flowerheads in purple, lilac, pink, blue or white; RHS AGM; native to Himalayan/Chinese alpine meadows |
| Primroses | *P. vulgaris* | 10–15cm | Late winter–spring | Crown at soil level | Native Irish wildflower; pale yellow flowers; self-seeds gently to form drifts |

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade for all three; Primroses tolerate more sun in the cooler, moister west of Ireland climate than in drier regions, while Candelabra Primula actually prefers a degree of shade — full sun can drain its flower colour.

**Height/Spread:** 10–60cm tall depending on species (see table), 20–40cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil for all three; pH 5.5–7.0. Primroses and Drumstick Primula tolerate clay well provided it is not permanently waterlogged. Candelabra Primula is the most water-demanding of the three, genuinely wanting the reliably wet, pond-margin-type conditions of a bog garden — this is closer to a marginal water plant than a normal border perennial.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost/leaf mould for Primroses and Drumstick Primula. For Candelabra Primula, dig in generous compost or leaf mould at a genuinely damp or pond-edge site rather than trying to improve a dry border to suit it.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level for all three.

**Spacing:** Candelabra Primula: 30–40cm between plants. Drumstick Primula: 20–30cm between plants. Primroses: 20–25cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring for all three. Annual leaf mould/compost mulch.

**Companions:** Bluebells, wood anemones, violets, ferns — classic native woodland-floor and hedge-bank planting for Primroses. Drumstick Primula combines well with other spring bulbs and moisture-loving woodland-edge perennials. Candelabra Primula belongs at the pond edge alongside astilbe, rodgersia, and other bog-garden perennials.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Drumstick Primula's leaves and stems can cause mild skin irritation in sensitive individuals — wear gloves if handling a lot of foliage.

**West of Ireland:** Primroses are one of Ireland's most familiar native spring wildflowers, found throughout hedge banks and woodland, easy and long-lived. Drumstick Primula is not native but thrives in the same cool, consistently moist Atlantic climate that suits its Himalayan origins, self-sowing readily once established to form good-sized colonies — best given reliably moist soil rather than a border that dries out in summer. Candelabra Primula is best reserved for a genuine pond margin or bog bed here; given that moisture, it self-seeds generously into striking tiered colonies.

---

### S18. Rough Tree Fern *(Cyathea australis)*


***Russian:*** *Циатея южная*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Cyathea australis — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyathea_australis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade, sheltered from wind and cold.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall (trunk + fronds) in cultivation, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, well-drained soil; pH 5.5–6.5. Consistent moisture is essential, especially at the crown.

**Soil prep:** Dig in generous compost/leaf mould; a warm, sheltered microclimate is essential.

**Planting depth:** At the same depth as in the nursery pot.

**Spacing:** 2–3m from structures and other large plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season; water into the crown.

**Companions:** Hostas, ferns, hellebores — classic shaded woodland-garden companions.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Less cold-hardy than Dicksonia antarctica — needs a very sheltered spot and crown protection (wrap with fleece/straw) in hard frost.

**West of Ireland:** An Australian tree fern with a rough, fibrous trunk and large arching fronds, similar in effect to the more commonly grown Tasmanian Tree Fern but generally less hardy. Best suited to the mildest, most sheltered west of Ireland gardens, with winter crown protection in colder spells.

---

### S19. Round-Lobed Hepatica *(Hepatica nobilis)*


***Russian:*** *Печёночница благородная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Round-Lobed Hepatica — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatica_nobilis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade; ideal under deciduous trees.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15cm tall, 15–20cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil, tolerant of neutral to slightly alkaline; pH 6.0–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with leaf mould/compost and a little grit.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; resents disturbance once established.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring. Annual leaf mould mulch.

**Companions:** Snowdrops, hellebores, wood anemones — classic very-early-spring woodland-floor planting.

**West of Ireland:** One of the earliest woodland perennials to flower, with delicate blue, pink or white blooms often appearing before the leaves in late winter/early spring, followed by attractive lobed evergreen foliage. Slow-growing but long-lived once settled — best left undisturbed.

---

### S20. Silver Fern *(Cyathea dealbata)*


***Russian:*** *Циатея серебристая*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Cyathea dealbata — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyathea_dealbata)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade, sheltered from wind and cold.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall (trunk + fronds) in cultivation, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, well-drained soil; pH 5.5–6.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig in generous compost/leaf mould; a warm, sheltered microclimate is essential.

**Planting depth:** At the same depth as in the nursery pot.

**Spacing:** 2–3m from structures and other large plants.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed monthly through the growing season; water into the crown.

**Companions:** Hostas, ferns, hellebores — classic shaded woodland-garden companions.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Less cold-hardy than Dicksonia antarctica — needs a very sheltered spot and crown protection in hard frost.

**West of Ireland:** New Zealand's iconic silver fern, named for the distinctive white-silvery underside of its fronds (the emblem on the All Blacks jersey). Borderline hardy at best in Ireland — reliable only in the mildest, most sheltered coastal gardens with winter crown protection; a real specialist's choice compared with the tougher Tasmanian Tree Fern.

---

### S21. Solomon's Seal *(Polygonatum multiflorum / P. × hybridum)*


***Russian:*** *Купена многоцветковая*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Polygonatum multiflorum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygonatum_multiflorum)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade.

**Height/Spread:** 60–90cm tall, 30–45cm spread (spreads by rhizome).

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich soil; pH 5.5–7.5. Tolerates clay well given reasonable moisture.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost/leaf mould.

**Planting depth:** Rhizome 5–8cm deep, autumn or spring.

**Spacing:** 30–40cm between plants.

**Fertiliser:** Light balanced feed in spring; annual leaf mould mulch.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, hellebores — classic woodland-floor companions; arching stems look best rising through or behind lower groundcover.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Berries and rhizomes are toxic if eaten.

**West of Ireland:** A graceful woodland perennial with arching stems hung with small white bell flowers in late spring, followed by black berries. Spreads gently by rhizome to form good-sized colonies in the mild, moist shade the west of Ireland climate provides — genuinely easy once established.

---

### S22. St Patrick's Cabbage *(Saxifraga spathularis)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cabáiste mhadra rua · Камнеломка лопатчатая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [St Patrick's Cabbage — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxifraga_spathularis)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade to full shade; also tolerates exposed, unshaded mountain sites in its native range.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20cm tall, 20–30cm spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, acid soil among rocks; pH 5.0–6.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with 30–40% ericaceous compost/leaf mould and some grit — best replicating a humid, rocky woodland or streamside setting.

**Planting depth:** Crown at soil level; shallow, stoloniferous rooting.

**Spacing:** 15–20cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** Very little required. Light ericaceous feed in spring if growth is poor.

**Companions:** Ferns, mosses, other Irish native rock/woodland plants for an authentic west of Ireland native rockery or shaded bank.

**West of Ireland:** A true west of Ireland native and local treasure — part of the "Lusitanian flora" found naturally only in Ireland and parts of Portugal/Spain, most abundant in Kerry and west Cork on shaded, humid rocks and hedge banks. An excellent, genuinely native choice for a shaded rockery or damp stone wall in this guide's own region. Evergreen rosettes of fleshy leaves with delicate white, pink-speckled flowers in early summer.

---

### S23. Tasmanian Tree Fern *(Dicksonia antarctica)*


***Russian:*** *Диксония антарктическая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Dicksonia antarctica (Tasmanian Tree Fern) — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dicksonia_antarctica)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial to full shade; shelter from direct hot sun and, importantly, from cold drying winds.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall (trunk + fronds), 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, free-draining, slightly acid soil; pH 5.5–6.5. Needs consistent moisture at the crown and root zone but good drainage — dislikes both drought and waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with generous compost/leaf mould (40%+) and grit. In a container, use a peat-free ericaceous or woodland-type compost mix.

**Planting depth:** Trunk (caudex) at same depth as previously grown; the fibrous "trunk" is actually a mass of roots and needs to stay moist.

**Spacing:** 1.5–3m between plants; wrap crown in hard frost

**Fertiliser:** Balanced liquid feed watered into the crown monthly through the growing season. Regular watering — including watering directly into the crown in dry spells — is more important than feeding.

**Companions:** Hostas, ferns, hellebores, snowdrops — classic damp, shaded woodland-garden planting.

**West of Ireland:** RHS hardiness H4–H5 (borderline, roughly to −5°C, less with protection); the mild, humid west of Ireland climate suits it well in a sheltered spot, but in harder winters wrap the crown with fleece or straw. Water the crown regularly in the growing season — this is the single most common cause of failure when it is allowed to dry out.

---

### S24. Wood Sorrel *(Oxalis acetosella)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Seamsóg · Кислица обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Wood Sorrel — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxalis_acetosella)

**Light:** 🌑 Full shade to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 5–10cm tall, 20–30cm spread (spreads by rhizome).

**Soil needs:** Moist, humus-rich, acid to neutral soil; pH 5.0–6.5.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with leaf mould/compost.

**Planting depth:** Crown/rhizome at soil level.

**Spacing:** 10–15cm between plants

**Fertiliser:** None required.

**Companions:** Ferns, mosses, primroses — classic deep-woodland-floor groundcover, often found growing directly among tree roots and on old stumps.

**West of Ireland:** A dainty native woodland groundcover with clover-like leaves that fold down at night and delicate white, purple-veined flowers in spring. Spreads gently by rhizomes to form soft carpets in deep shade where little else will grow — ideal under mature trees or in a native woodland-style corner.

---


---

## GROUP T: ORNAMENTAL TREES & SHRUBS

### T1. Bald Cypress *(Taxodium distichum)*


***Russian:*** *Таксодиум двурядный*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Taxodium distichum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxodium_distichum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–25m tall (much smaller and slower-growing while young in cultivation), 6–9m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any soil including clay; pH 5.5–7.5. Genuinely tolerates wet, waterlogged ground better than almost any other tree in this guide.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — one of the very few trees that actively tolerates poorly-drained, wet clay.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 4–6m minimum from structures for a young tree; considerably more given its eventual size.

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established.

**Companions:** Astilbe, ferns, yellow flag iris and other moisture-loving plants at its base.

**West of Ireland:** A deciduous conifer, native to swampy ground in the south-eastern USA, with soft feathery foliage turning russet-orange in autumn before falling. Genuinely useful for a consistently damp or poorly-drained corner of a west of Ireland garden where few other trees would thrive; large, long-lived, and slow-growing.

---

### T2. Balsam Fir *(Abies balsamea)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Abies balsamea — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abies_balsamea)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–20m tall (full-sized; dwarf cvs. much smaller), 5–8m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, well-drained, acidic to neutral soil; pH 5.0–6.5. Dislikes hot, dry conditions.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with ericaceous or multipurpose compost and grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 4–6m between plants for a full-sized tree; dwarf cultivars much closer.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release feed in spring while young.

**Companions:** Underplant with ferns, hostas and other woodland-edge shade plants.

**Fragrance 🌲:** Famously fragrant — the classic "Christmas tree" scent, released strongly when the needles are crushed or the tree is cut; often used in balsam-filled pillows and potpourri.

**West of Ireland:** A neat, conical North American conifer, the archetypal Christmas tree, with soft, flat, dark green needles and upright purplish cones. Prefers cool, consistently moist conditions and dislikes hot, dry summers, which suits the mild west of Ireland climate reasonably well, though it performs best with some shelter from strong coastal wind. Full-sized specimens are large — a dwarf cultivar is a better fit for most gardens.

---

### T3. Bamboo *(clump-forming: Fargesia spp., Chusquea spp.)*


***Russian:*** *Бамбук*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Bamboo — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fargesia)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Fargesia prefers some afternoon shade.

**Height/Spread:** 2–5m tall (clump bamboo), 1–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils including clay; prefers moist, fertile, free-draining. Choose clump-forming species — running bamboo *(Phyllostachys)* requires a 60cm deep root barrier.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width. Mix clay 50/50 with compost. Mulch well.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 1.5–3m from structures; root barrier for running types

**Fertiliser:** High-nitrogen fertiliser (lawn feed or balanced granular) each spring. Top-dress with compost each autumn. Container plants: balanced liquid fertiliser monthly during the growing season.

**Companions:** Ferns, hostas, astilbe, hellebores. Bamboo provides shelter for smaller plants. Root barrier required near structures.

---

### T4. Black Locust *(Robinia pseudoacacia 'Frisia')*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Robinia pseudoacacia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinia_pseudoacacia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential for the best golden leaf colour.

**Height/Spread:** 10–15m tall, 6–8m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any well-drained soil, including poor or dry ground; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of poor soil (it fixes its own nitrogen) but dislikes waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Improve heavy clay with grit for the first few years; established trees are very tolerant.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark. Stake well while young — brittle wood is prone to wind damage.

**Spacing:** 6–8m between plants; can eventually reach 12–15m, though slower and more compact than the straight species.

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established — a nitrogen-fixing legume.

**Companions:** Underplant with shade-tolerant bulbs and perennials once the canopy provides light dappled shade.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Brittle branches can break in strong wind — avoid very exposed sites, and site away from paths/parking. Thorny on some growth.

**West of Ireland:** A fast-growing deciduous tree grown for its soft, golden-yellow, fern-like foliage that lights up a garden from spring to autumn, with fragrant white pea-flowers in early summer. Tolerant of poor soil and pollution, but its brittle wood is vulnerable to breakage in the region's strong Atlantic winds — a sheltered spot is best.

---

### T5. Border Forsythia *(Forsythia × intermedia)*


***Russian:*** *Форзиция средняя*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Border Forsythia — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forsythia_%G3%97_intermedia)

**Light:** 🌤️ Full sun to partial shade. Flowers better in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 2–3m tall, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 6.0–8.0. Tolerates clay very well.

**Soil prep:** Standard clay improvement with compost and grit.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 1.5m between; 2m from walls

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser (Growmore) in early spring. Annual compost mulch after pruning. Avoid high-nitrogen feeds. ⚠️ Prune immediately after flowering — never in autumn/winter (flower buds form on old wood).

**Companions:** Spring tulips underplanted, Vinca or Pulmonaria as underplanting, holly or yew as evergreen backdrop.

---

### T6. Cider Gum (Eucalyptus) *(Eucalyptus gunnii)*


***Russian:*** *Эвкалипт Ганна*
📷 **Reference photo:** [Eucalyptus gunnii — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucalyptus_gunnii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20m tall if left unpruned (or 2–4m if coppiced/stooled annually), 4–8m spread (much less if coppiced).

**Soil needs:** Any well-drained soil; pH 5.5–7.5. Dislikes waterlogging, especially while young.

**Soil prep:** Improve heavy clay with 20–30% grit while the tree establishes.

**Planting depth:** At pot level.

**Spacing:** 2–4m between plants if coppiced annually; considerably more if left to grow as a full tree.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring for the first few years.

**Companions:** Ornamental grasses, hardy geraniums — good foliage-contrast companions if kept coppiced.

**West of Ireland:** A fast-growing evergreen tree with rounded, silvery-blue juvenile foliage much prized for cut foliage and flower arranging. Fully hardy in the mild Irish climate; most gardeners "stool" or coppice it hard every year or two to keep the prized round juvenile foliage and a manageable size, rather than letting it grow into a full-sized tree.

---

### T7. Common Hawthorn *(Crataegus monogyna)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Sceach Gheal · Боярышник однопестичный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Common Hawthorn — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crataegus_monogyna)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 5–8m tall, 5–8m spread.

**Soil needs:** Very adaptable — most soils including heavy clay, chalk, and poor ground; pH 5.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — one of the most tolerant trees of clay and exposure. Improve drainage with grit only on the wettest sites.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** As hedge: 30–45cm; as tree: 4–6m from structures

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established. Light mulch of compost in early years.

**Companions:** Classic native hedgerow tree — combines with blackthorn, holly and hazel for a mixed native hedge; underplant with primroses, bluebells and violets.

**West of Ireland:** Ireland's most familiar native hedgerow and windbreak tree ("whitethorn"), exceptionally tolerant of wind, salt spray and exposure — an excellent, low-cost choice for shelterbelts on Atlantic-facing boundaries. Masses of white May blossom followed by red autumn haws valuable for birds.

---

### T8. Douglas Fir *(Pseudotsuga menziesii)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Pseudotsuga menziesii — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudotsuga_menziesii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20m+ tall (full-sized; dwarf cvs. much smaller), 4–8m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, well-drained soil, acidic to neutral; pH 5.0–6.5. Dislikes hot, dry sites.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** This is a genuinely enormous forest tree (naturally reaching well over 60m) — full-sized specimens need a very large garden or estate; dwarf and compact cultivars are a far more realistic choice for most gardens, spaced 2–4m apart.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release feed in spring while young.

**Companions:** Best given its own space if grown as a full-sized specimen; dwarf cultivars combine well with other conifers and heathers.

**Fragrance 🌲:** Needles are noticeably fragrant when crushed or bruised, often described as citrusy or resinous.

**West of Ireland:** A majestic evergreen conifer, introduced to the UK and Ireland from western North America in the 1820s and now naturalised in some large estate woodlands. Tolerates the mild, moist Atlantic climate well and is not a true fir despite the name (it belongs to its own genus, *Pseudotsuga*). Given its ultimate size, this is a tree for a large garden, parkland or woodland planting rather than an average suburban plot — choose one of the many dwarf or compact cultivars available for a smaller space.

---

### T10. Dwarf Mountain Pine *(Pinus mugo var. mugo)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Pinus mugo — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_mugo)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — needed to keep the habit compact and dense; tolerates part shade but grows looser.

**Height/Spread:** 1–1.5m tall (dwarf/prostrate forms), 1.5–2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably drained soil, pH 5.0–7.5; naturally found on poor, rocky mountain soils, so tolerates poor and thin ground well. Dislikes permanently waterlogged clay.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole twice the width of the rootball. Mix the excavated clay 50/50 with multipurpose compost, adding 20–30% horticultural grit to guarantee sharp drainage — this matters more than fertility for a plant adapted to mountain scree. On the wettest clay, plant on a slightly raised mound.

**Planting depth:** At the same level as in the pot; do not bury the base of the stems.

**Spacing:** 1.5–2m between plants; ultimate size around 1.5–2m tall and wide, reached slowly.

**Fertiliser:** None needed at planting — wait 6–8 weeks before feeding to avoid burning new roots. From the following spring, a light dressing of Kieserite (magnesium sulphate) or an organic conifer feed each February–March keeps the needles a good green. Otherwise very low-maintenance.

**Companions:** Heather, dwarf junipers, sempervivum and other rockery or gravel-garden plants that share its need for sharp drainage.

**West of Ireland:** A genuinely tough, slow-growing dwarf conifer from the mountains of central and southern Europe — very cold-hardy and, once established, tolerant of salt-laden wind, which makes it a good choice for an exposed coastal west of Ireland garden. Needs almost no pruning beyond removing dead wood.

---

### T11. Elder 'Black Lace' *(Sambucus nigra 'Black Lace')*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Sambucus nigra — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambucus_nigra)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for the deepest near-black leaf colour; tolerates partial shade but foliage turns more green.

**Height/Spread:** 2–3m tall, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils including clay, moist but well-drained; pH 5.5–7.5.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with compost; very adaptable and easy.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants; can be kept smaller with annual pruning.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced feed in spring; annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Contrasts beautifully with lime-green or golden foliage (e.g. golden Philadelphus, Choisya 'Sundance') and with pink or white-flowered shrubs and perennials.

**Pruning:** For the best, largest foliage, cut hard back to a framework in late winter each year (sacrificing some flowers); for flowers and berries as well as foliage, prune more lightly.

**West of Ireland:** A striking large shrub with finely cut, near-black purple foliage and pink-flushed, lacy flowerheads in early summer, followed by black elderberries (edible when cooked, like the wild native elder). Fully hardy and easy in the mild, moist Irish climate — a dramatic dark foil for pale or pastel-flowered companions.

---

### T12. English Oak *(Quercus robur)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Quercus robur — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quercus_robur)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20–35m tall (very large, long-lived), 20m+ spread.

**Soil needs:** Any soil including clay and chalk; pH 5.0–8.0. Extremely tolerant and lime-tolerant.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — one of the most adaptable trees to heavy clay in this guide, needing no special amendment.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 8–10m minimum from structures for a young tree, and ideally considerably more given its eventual size (up to 35m) — this is a long-lived, very large forest tree.

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established.

**Companions:** Bluebells, wood anemones and other native woodland-floor plants once the canopy provides dappled shade; supports an exceptional range of native Irish insect and bird life.

**West of Ireland:** A magnificent, iconic native-range tree (present in Ireland since prehistory, though most Irish "native" oak woodland is now largely sessile oak, *Quercus petraea*) valued as much for wildlife as for timber — few other trees support as much insect, lichen and bird life. Extremely long-lived, tolerant of the region's heavy clay and high rainfall, and a genuine multi-generational specimen tree — plant only where there is truly room for a very large, long-lived tree, well away from buildings and drains.

---

### T13. False Spiraea *(Sorbaria sorbifolia)*


***Russian:*** *Рябинник рябинолистный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Sorbaria sorbifolia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorbaria_sorbifolia)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 1.5–2.5m tall, 1.5–2.5m spread (suckering).

**Soil needs:** Most moist soils including clay; pH 5.5–7.5. Very adaptable and tolerant of poor drainage better than many shrubs.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — dig hole twice rootball width, mix clay 50/50 with compost.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 1–1.5m between plants; spreads by suckers

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser in spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Good at the back of a mixed border with hydrangea and deutzia; also useful for stabilising damp banks.

**West of Ireland:** Vigorous, suckering shrub with creamy plume-like flowers in summer — spreads readily by suckers, so best given room to naturalise (e.g. a bank or informal border) rather than a tight border, where it can be to be kept in check by removing suckers.

---

### T14. Fraser Fir *(Abies fraseri)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Abies fraseri — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraser_fir)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ light shade.

**Height/Spread:** 9–17m tall in cultivation, 4–6m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist, well-drained, acidic soil; pH 5.0–6.0. Dislikes heat and drought.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with ericaceous or multipurpose compost and grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 4–6m between plants for a full-sized tree.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release feed in spring while young.

**Companions:** Underplant with ferns, hostas and other acid-loving woodland-edge plants.

**Fragrance 🌲:** Strongly and pleasantly fragrant, often described as resembling turpentine or classic pine-forest scent; the needles are notably long-lasting once cut, which is why this species is the most popular Christmas tree species commercially grown in North America.

**West of Ireland:** A neat, conical North American fir native to the high Appalachian Mountains, with dark green needles showing silvery-white undersides. Prefers cool, consistently moist conditions similar to its native mountain habitat, which the mild, damp west of Ireland climate can suit well given reasonable shelter; dislikes hot, dry summers, which are rarely an issue here. A good choice where a genuinely fragrant conifer specimen is wanted.

---

### T15. Fuzzy Pride-of-Rochester *(Deutzia scabra)*


***Russian:*** *Дейция шершавая*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Fuzzy Pride-of-Rochester — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutzia_scabra)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade. Flowers best and stays more compact in full sun; needs a sheltered spot out of the coldest wind.

**Height/Spread:** 2–3m tall, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most moist, well-drained soils including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Not fussy once drainage is adequate.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 1.5–2m between plants; grows 1.8–3m tall

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser in early spring. Annual compost mulch.

**Companions:** Lewis mock orange, lilac, weigela — classic spring/early-summer flowering shrub border. Underplant with spring bulbs.

**West of Ireland:** A larger, more vigorous Deutzia than the dwarf *D. gracilis* type, with distinctive peeling cinnamon-coloured bark and fuzzy, sandpaper-textured leaves (hence "scabra," Latin for rough) that give this cultivar its common name. Double white flowers, sometimes tinged pink, in early summer on arching branches. Prune immediately after flowering, removing a proportion of the oldest stems at the base each year to keep it renewing well — it flowers on the previous year's wood, so late pruning sacrifices next year's display. Note: in parts of North America this species can self-seed and naturalise; in an Irish garden setting this is not generally a concern, but deadheading after flowering avoids any risk of unwanted seedlings.

---

### T16. Ginkgo *(Ginkgo biloba)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Ginkgo biloba — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginkgo_biloba)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 15–25m tall (slow-growing), 5–10m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any well-drained soil, including clay; pH 5.0–8.0. Extremely tolerant of a wide range of conditions once established.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit for the first few years while roots establish; mature trees tolerate clay well.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 6–8m between plants for a full-sized tree; slow-growing, so this size takes many decades to reach.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in spring for the first few years only; established trees need none.

**Companions:** Underplant with spring bulbs and shade-tolerant perennials once the canopy matures; in youth, while still small, combines well with ornamental grasses.

**West of Ireland:** An ancient, exceptionally tough and disease-resistant deciduous conifer relative with distinctive fan-shaped leaves that turn bright butter-yellow in autumn. Male trees are usually chosen for gardens, since female trees produce foul-smelling fruit. Extremely long-lived and virtually pest- and disease-free; tolerates urban pollution, wind and a wide range of soils, making it a genuinely low-maintenance choice, though slow-growing, so buy as large a specimen as you can afford if a quicker impact is wanted.

---

### T17. Golden Mimosa *(Acacia baileyana)*


***Russian:*** *Акация Бейли*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Acacia baileyana — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acacia_baileyana)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun essential, in a sheltered spot away from cold winds.

**Height/Spread:** 3–5m tall (borderline hardy, often smaller here), 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Free-draining, poor to moderately fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerates poor, dry soils but hates waterlogging.

**Soil prep:** Improve clay with 30% horticultural grit; a raised bed or south-facing wall base gives the best chance of success.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 3–4m between plants; fast-growing.

**Fertiliser:** Little needed once established; a balanced feed in spring for young plants only.

**Companions:** Rosemary, lavender and other Mediterranean-style plants sharing its need for sun and sharp drainage.

**West of Ireland:** A fast-growing evergreen with feathery, purple-flushed young foliage (ageing to blue-grey) and masses of fragrant yellow, mimosa-type flowers in late winter. Borderline hardy (RHS H3, roughly to -5°C) — reliably survives only in the mildest, most sheltered coastal west of Ireland gardens; elsewhere, best grown in a large container that can be moved under cover for winter.

---

### T18. Holly *(Ilex aquifolium)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Cuileann · Падуб остролистный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Holly — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilex_aquifolium)

**Light:** 🌑 Full shade to ☀️ full sun. Extraordinary adaptability. More berries in sunnier positions. ⚠️ Plant at least one male + one female for berries.

**Height/Spread:** 3–8m tall (as hedge/specimen; can reach 15m unpruned), 2–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** pH 4.5–8.0. Tolerates heavy clay, poor soil, coastal exposure.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice rootball size, backfill with clay/compost mix. Grit helpful but not essential.

**Planting depth:** At pot level; fibrous shallow root mat, do not bury deeply.

**Spacing:** 1.5–3m between; slow-growing hedge

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release fertiliser in early spring only if plant looks weak or pale. Annual bark mulch more useful than heavy feeding. Container: slow-release granular fertiliser each spring, liquid feed monthly in summer.

**Companions:** Yew (classic Irish churchyard combination), forsythia (glows against dark foliage), winter-flowering hellebores, snowdrops beneath. Critical: plant at least one male (e.g. 'Silver Queen' — confusingly a male) and one or more berry-bearing females nearby (within 50–100m).

⚠️ **All parts except berry flesh toxic to humans and livestock.**

---

### T19. Horse Chestnut *(Aesculus hippocastanum)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Aesculus hippocastanum — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesculus_hippocastanum)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 20–25m tall, 15–20m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most soils including clay; pH 5.5–7.5. Adaptable and tolerant.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — a tolerant tree once established.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** Needs a genuinely large garden or parkland setting — 10m+ from structures, and considerably more space to reach its full, wide-spreading canopy.

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established.

**Companions:** Best given its own space, since it casts dense shade and has a wide root spread once mature.

**West of Ireland:** A large, familiar deciduous tree (not native to Ireland or Britain, but long naturalised, originally from the Balkans), grown for its impressive upright "candles" of white or pink flowers in May and its glossy conkers in autumn — a childhood favourite. Fully hardy in the mild, moist Irish climate, but its eventual size and dense canopy make it suitable only for a large garden, parkland or avenue planting, not an average suburban plot. Increasingly affected by leaf miner and bleeding canker in parts of the UK/Ireland, which can shorten leaf display but rarely kills an established tree outright.

---

### T20. Irish Yew *(Taxus baccata 'Fastigiata')*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Iúr · Тис ягодный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Irish Yew — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxus_baccata)

**Light:** 🌑 Full shade to ☀️ full sun. Narrow column; produces more compact form in full sun. Discovered as natural mutation in Co. Fermanagh in the 18th century — all Irish yews worldwide descend from that one tree.

**Height/Spread:** 3–5m tall, 1–1.5m spread (narrow, fastigiate).

**Soil needs:** Most well-drained soils, pH 5.0–8.0. Tolerates heavy clay if drainage adequate. Deeply dislikes waterlogged roots.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit. On very wet clay, plant on a raised mound.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Extensive but not deep root system.

**Spacing:** Column 1m wide eventually; 1m from structures

**Fertiliser:** Very little required. Light balanced slow-release fertiliser in spring if plant looks pale. Annual bark mulch more beneficial than chemical feeding. Avoid high-nitrogen feeds.

**Companions:** Holly, peonies, roses (as a dark backdrop), hellebores, snowdrops. Evergreen column provides structure year-round.

⚠️ **All parts except red berry flesh are highly toxic to humans, horses and livestock. Do not plant near paddocks.**

---

### T21. Japanese Barberry *(Berberis thunbergii 'Atropurpurea')*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Berberis thunbergii — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berberis_thunbergii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for the best deep purple leaf colour; tolerates partial shade but foliage turns greener.

**Height/Spread:** 1–1.2m tall, 1–1.2m spread.

**Soil needs:** Any reasonably drained soil, including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Very adaptable and tolerant of poor soil.

**Soil prep:** Minimal — one of the easiest and most tolerant shrubs of clay in this guide. Add compost to very heavy ground.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between plants for a hedge; 1–1.2m as a specimen.

**Fertiliser:** None needed once established.

**Companions:** Golden or lime-green shrubs (e.g. Choisya, golden Philadelphus) for foliage contrast; spring bulbs beneath.

**Warnings ⚠️:** Sharply spined stems — wear thick gloves when pruning; site away from paths used by children.

**West of Ireland:** A tough, spiny deciduous shrub with rich reddish-purple foliage all season, small pale yellow spring flowers, red autumn berries and fiery autumn leaf colour. Extremely hardy and low-maintenance, tolerant of wind, pollution and poor soil — an excellent low hedge or barrier planting for an exposed site.

---

### T22. Japanese Camellia *(Camellia japonica)*


***Russian:*** *Камелия японская*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Japanese Camellia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camellia_japonica)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred, sheltered from early morning sun (which can damage frosted buds) and cold wind.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall, 1.5–2.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich, acid soil; pH 5.0–6.5. Will yellow (chlorosis) on alkaline soil.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay generously with ericaceous compost (40%+) and grit. In a container, use peat-free ericaceous compost.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark; shallow-rooted, do not bury the stem base.

**Spacing:** 1–1.5m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Ericaceous slow-release feed after flowering in spring. Avoid feeding after midsummer, to let wood ripen before winter.

**Companions:** Japanese skimmia, hydrangea macrophylla, Japanese maple — classic acid-soil shrub border.

**West of Ireland:** The mild, humid west of Ireland climate suits camellias well, though a sheltered spot out of early morning sun and harsh wind protects the flowers. Excellent in a large container of ericaceous compost if clay soil is not acid enough.

---

### T23. Japanese Cedar *(Cryptomeria japonica)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Japanese Cedar — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptomeria)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ partial shade.

**Height/Spread:** 10–20m tall (dwarf cvs. 1–1.5m), 3–5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, fertile, acid to neutral soil; pH 5.5–6.5. Tolerates a wide range of soils including clay, but dislikes drying out and dislikes strong maritime exposure (unlike many other conifers in this guide).

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with multipurpose or ericaceous compost and 20% grit for drainage. Choose a site sheltered from strong winds — this is essential, not optional, for this species.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 4–6m between plants for a full-sized tree; dwarf cultivars (e.g. 'Globosa Nana', 'Vilmoriniana') can go as close as 1–1.5m.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release feed in early spring. Keep soil evenly moist, especially while young; established trees tolerate short dry spells but perform best without prolonged drought.

**Companions:** Japanese maple, camellia, rhododendron and other acid-loving woodland-edge shrubs; underplant with ferns and hostas.

**Pot/container:** ✅ Dwarf cultivars such as 'Globosa Nana' are well suited to a large container of ericaceous or multipurpose compost; full-sized forms need open ground.

**West of Ireland:** A large, elegant evergreen conifer with soft, awl-shaped needles that take on a bronze tint in winter and attractive, peeling red-brown bark. Full-sized forms need a big, sheltered garden — this is not a tree for an exposed coastal site, since RHS and other growers note it dislikes strong winds and salt-laden maritime exposure, unlike many conifers already in this guide. Dwarf cultivars are a much better fit for a smaller or more exposed west of Ireland garden.

---

### T24. Japanese Flowering Cherry *(Prunus serrulata)*


***Russian:*** *Вишня мелкопильчатая*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Prunus serrulata 'Kanzan'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_%27Kanzan%27) · [Prunus 'Shirotae'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_serrulata) · [Prunus 'Kiku-shidare'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prunus_serrulata)

| Variety | Height | Flowering | Planting depth | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 'Kanzan' | Up to 8m | Mid-spring (April) | Graft union at/above soil | Vase-shaped becoming spreading; deep-pink double flowers; the most widely planted flowering cherry |
| 'Shirotae' (syn. 'Mount Fuji') | 4–6m | Mid–late spring | Graft union at/above soil | Spreading, flat-topped crown with arching branches; fragrant, semi-double pure white flowers; RHS AGM |
| 'Kiku-shidare' (syn. 'Kiku-shidare-zakura', Cheal's Weeping) | 2.5–4m | Mid–late spring | Graft union at/above soil | Weeping habit with gracefully arching branches; fully double rich pink "chrysanthemum" flowers; RHS AGM |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun for all three. Avoid frost pockets — late frosts damage the blossom.

**Height/Spread:** 2.5–8m tall depending on variety (see table), 2.5–6m spread.

**Soil needs:** Well-drained, fertile soil; pH 6.0–7.5. None tolerates permanently wet ground.

**Soil prep:** Dig a hole at least 60cm wide and 45cm deep. Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit. Stake well, especially the weeping 'Kiku-shidare'.

**Planting depth:** Graft union at or just above soil level for all three.

**Spacing:** 'Kanzan': 4–5m from structures. 'Shirotae': 4–6m, given its wide spreading crown. 'Kiku-shidare': 2.5–4m — the most compact of the three.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced fertiliser in early spring. Compost mulch around the base each spring. Do not feed in late summer.

**Companions:** Daffodils and tulips beneath (spectacularly timed with the blossom). Forget-me-nots as a blue carpet. Hostas and hardy geraniums as underplanting.

**Pruning:** Prune only in summer, if needed, to reduce the risk of silver leaf disease common to Prunus species.

**West of Ireland:** All three are hardy, reliable flowering cherries for the mild, moist Irish climate. 'Kanzan' is the classic bold pink double-flowered form widely planted in Irish streets and gardens; 'Shirotae' offers a softer, more elegant white display with a wide, flat-topped habit well suited to a lawn specimen; 'Kiku-shidare' brings a graceful weeping form to a smaller garden where the upright vigour of 'Kanzan' or 'Shirotae' would be too much. All three benefit from a sheltered spot away from strong coastal winds, which can damage the blossom and shorten the display.

---

### T25. Japanese Maple *(Acer palmatum)*


***Russian:*** *Клён дланевидный*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Japanese Maple — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acer_palmatum)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred. Morning sun with afternoon shade is ideal. Variegated and dissectum types especially wind-sensitive. Avoid open exposed positions.

**Height/Spread:** 2–4m tall (in a container/small garden), 2–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, slightly acidic, pH 5.5–6.5.

**Soil prep:** Dig 2–3× diameter of rootball. Mix clay with compost, 25% ericaceous compost and 25% grit in backfill. Shallow-rooted — do not dig deeply.

**Planting depth:** At pot level. Shallow-rooted; do not bury.

**Spacing:** 2–4m from structures; wind shelter essential

**Fertiliser:** Slow-release balanced fertiliser (e.g. Osmocote) in spring. Container: liquid feed monthly April–July at balanced rate — do not feed after July. Annual composted bark mulch.

**Companions:** Hostas (classic combination), ferns, hellebores, snowdrops beneath. Excellent specimen plant. Avoid planting with other thirsty trees nearby.

**West of Ireland:** Wind shelter is essential. Sheltered courtyard or walled garden position is ideal.

---

### T26. Japanese Skimmia *(Skimmia japonica)*


***Russian:*** *Скиммия японская*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Japanese Skimmia — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skimmia_japonica)

**Light:** 🌑 Shade to 🌥️ partial shade preferred; tolerates full sun in cooler, moister climates like the west of Ireland.

**Height/Spread:** 1–2m tall, 1–1.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil; prefers slightly acid to neutral, pH 5.5–6.5. Dislikes dry or chalky soils.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with 30–40% ericaceous compost and grit for drainage. Add well-rotted organic matter.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark; shallow-rooted, do not bury the stem base.

**Spacing:** 60–90cm between plants; ♂+♀ within a few metres for berries

**Fertiliser:** Ericaceous slow-release feed in spring. Mulch annually with ericaceous compost or bark.

**Companions:** Hellebores, ferns, hostas and other shade/woodland plants; Japanese maple and hydrangea for a shaded border.

**West of Ireland:** Dioecious — plant at least one male (e.g. 'Rubella') near females (e.g. 'Nymans') for the females' red berries. Compact, reliable evergreen for shaded, sheltered spots; good in containers on the north side of a house.

⚠️ Berries are mildly toxic if ingested in quantity.

---

### T27. Korean Fir *(Abies koreana)*


📷 **Reference photo:** [Abies koreana — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abies_koreana)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌥️ partial shade, with some shelter from cold wind.

**Height/Spread:** 2–5m tall in cultivation (dwarf cvs. much smaller), 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Deep, moist, slightly acidic to neutral, well-drained soil; pH 5.0–6.5. Grows poorly in heavy clay unless well amended.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay generously with ericaceous or multipurpose compost and grit for drainage — this species is less clay-tolerant than many other conifers in this guide.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 3–4m between plants for a full-sized specimen; slow-growing, so this takes many years; dwarf cultivars (e.g. 'Silberlocke') can go much closer.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced slow-release feed in spring while young.

**Companions:** Heather, dwarf conifers and other acid-loving woodland-edge plants.

**Fragrance 🌲:** The needles are noticeably fragrant, particularly when crushed.

**West of Ireland:** A compact, slow-growing, RHS AGM conifer from the mountains of South Korea, prized for its short, dense, glossy needles (silvery beneath) and striking upright violet-blue cones, produced even on young trees. Prefers cooler conditions and good shelter from wind — well suited to the mild but often breezy west of Ireland climate given a reasonably sheltered spot, though it dislikes being planted in unamended heavy clay.

---

### T28. Lewis Mock Orange *(Philadelphus lewisii)*


***Russian:*** *Чубушник Льюиса*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lewis Mock Orange — view source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphus_lewisii)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ part shade. Best flowering and fragrance in full sun.

**Height/Spread:** 2–3m tall, 2–3m spread.

**Soil needs:** Most well-drained soils including clay; pH 6.0–7.5. Tolerant of a wide range once established.

**Soil prep:** Dig hole twice the rootball width. Mix excavated clay 50/50 with compost and 20% grit for drainage.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 1.5–2m between plants

**Fertiliser:** Balanced general fertiliser in early spring. Annual compost mulch. Avoid high-nitrogen feeds, which favour leaves over flowers.

**Companions:** Deutzia and Lilac (classic mixed shrub border companions), roses, spring bulbs beneath.

**West of Ireland:** State flower of Idaho, very hardy and wind-tolerant once established — a good choice for exposed Atlantic gardens. Prune immediately after flowering (flowers on previous year's wood).

---

### T29. Lilac *(Syringa vulgaris)*


***Irish / Russian:*** *Craobh liathchorcra · Сирень обыкновенная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Lilac — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syringa_vulgaris)

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun — needs at least 6 hours to flower well.

**Height/Spread:** 4–7m tall, 3–4m spread.

**Soil needs:** Neutral to slightly alkaline, pH 6.5–8.0.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay 50/50 with compost and 20–25% grit. Add garden lime if soil is acidic. Slight mound if waterlogging is a risk.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark.

**Spacing:** 1.5m between; 2m from walls

**Fertiliser:** Superphosphate or balanced fertiliser in early spring. Avoid high-nitrogen feeds (produces leaves, not flowers). Annual compost mulch around base.

**Companions:** Roses (May–June combination), alliums, irises, peonies, delphiniums, aquilegia, geraniums. Foxgloves self-seed naturally nearby.

---

### T101. Magnolias *(Magnolia spp. and hybrids)*


***Russian:*** *Магнолия*

📷 **Reference photos:** [Magnolia liliiflora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_liliiflora) · [Magnolia stellata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_stellata) · [Magnolia × soulangeana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_%C3%97_soulangeana) · [Magnolia × loebneri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_%C3%97_loebneri) · [Magnolia denudata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia_denudata)

| Species/Hybrid | Latin name | Height/Spread | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lily Magnolia | *M. liliiflora* | 2.5–3m / 2.5–3m | Compact, later-flowering than star magnolia, so often escapes late frosts |
| Star Magnolia | *M. stellata* | 2–3m / 2–3m | Very early, multi-tepalled star-shaped flowers; tolerates alkaline soil better than most magnolias |
| Saucer Magnolia | *M. × soulangeana* | 4.5–6m / 4.5–6m | The classic large-flowered spring magnolia; considerably bigger at maturity than the others here |
| Loebner Magnolia | *M. × loebneri* | 4–6m / 4–6m | Hybrid of *M. kobus* × *M. stellata*; vigorous, free-flowering, good frost resistance for an early magnolia |
| Yulan Magnolia | *M. denudata* | 6–9m (compact cultivars smaller) / 5–8m | One of the oldest magnolias in cultivation, grown in China for over 1000 years; pure white in the species, though cultivars extend the colour range |
| "Little Girl" hybrids | *M. liliiflora* 'Nigra' × *M. stellata* 'Rosea' | 2.5–4m / 2.5–4m | US National Arboretum series bred for later flowering and better frost avoidance |
| Gresham hybrids | *M. liliiflora* 'Nigra' × *M. × veitchii* | 5–8m / 4–6m | Taller American hybrids; sometimes mislabelled as *soulangeana* by nurseries |

**Light:** ☀️ Full sun to 🌤️ part shade, sheltered from cold wind. Morning sun and a sheltered position help protect early flowers from late frost damage.

**Height/Spread:** Varies considerably by species/hybrid — see table above; 2–3m for the more compact Star and Lily Magnolias, up to 6–9m for Saucer and Yulan Magnolias at full maturity.

**Soil needs:** Moist but well-drained, humus-rich soil; slightly acid to neutral, pH 5.0–6.5. Dislikes shallow chalk and waterlogging. Star Magnolia tolerates alkaline soil somewhat better than the others.

**Soil prep:** Mix clay with 30% compost/ericaceous compost and grit for drainage. Use a wide, shallow planting hole. Avoid deep planting.

**Planting depth:** At nursery soil mark; shallow, fleshy roots on all magnolias — handle gently and do not disturb once planted.

**Spacing:** 2–3m between plants/structures for the more compact species (Star, Lily); 4–6m for Saucer, Loebner and Yulan magnolias, given their larger eventual size.

**Fertiliser:** Balanced or ericaceous slow-release feed in spring. Annual mulch with compost or bark, kept away from the trunk, is more beneficial than heavy feeding.

**Companions:** Underplant with hellebores, ferns, snowdrops, species crocus and *Iris reticulata*, all of which flower before or as the magnolia leafs up; hardy geraniums make good ground cover beneath. Pairs well with Japanese maple. White- or pale-flowered types show up beautifully against an evergreen backdrop.

**Cultivars:**

| Cultivar | Species/Hybrid | Colour | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 'Nigra' | Lily Magnolia | Deep reddish-purple outside, paler pink inside | RHS AGM; the most widely grown *liliiflora* cultivar; flowers mid-to-late spring and sporadically into autumn |
| 'O'Neill' | Lily Magnolia | Deep purple-red | Noted for being particularly cold-tolerant among named forms of the species |
| 'Darkest Purple' | Lily Magnolia | Very dark, rich purple | Among the darkest-flowered cultivars available in the species |
| 'Royal Star' | Star Magnolia | White | More tepals than the species (25–30); blooms ~2 weeks later, reducing frost risk; robust and easy to propagate |
| 'Waterlily' | Star Magnolia | White | Blooms ~2 weeks later than the species, also reducing frost risk |
| 'Rosea' | Star Magnolia | Pink-flushed buds opening paler pink/white | A popular pink-toned selection of the species |
| 'Alexandrina' | Saucer Magnolia | Deep rose-purple outside, white inside | Upright, multi-stemmed habit; one of the most widely planted saucer magnolia cultivars |
| 'Lennei' | Saucer Magnolia | Deep magenta-purple outside, white inside | Broad, pyramidal habit; flowers slightly later than the species |
| 'Lennei Alba' | Saucer Magnolia | Pure white | Broad, pyramidal habit; later-flowering; good for smaller gardens |
| 'Rustica Rubra' | Saucer Magnolia | Rose-red | More compact in height than other soulangeana cultivars |
| 'Alba Superba' | Saucer Magnolia | Pure white outside with a purple-pink flush at the base of each petal, white inside | Old Belgian cultivar (mid-19th century); among the first saucer magnolia cultivars to open each spring |
| 'Satisfaction' | Saucer Magnolia | Rich pink-purple | A more modern, free-flowering saucer magnolia selection |
| 'Leonard Messel' | Loebner Magnolia | Purple-pink outside, white inside | RHS AGM; bred at Nymans Garden, 1955; very free-flowering, good for smaller gardens |
| 'Yellow River' | Yulan Magnolia | Citrus/lemon-yellow | Fragrant, lemon-scented; flowers in early May; smaller and more compact than the species |
| 'Betty' | "Little Girl" hybrid | Reddish-purple outside, white inside | Compact, late-flowering shrub that avoids most late frosts |
| 'Susan' | "Little Girl" hybrid | Purple-red, twisted tepals | RHS AGM; fragrant; despite occasional loose association with saucer magnolias in conversation, has no *soulangeana* parentage |
| 'Heaven Scent' | Gresham hybrid | Lavender-pink outside, white inside | RHS AGM; fragrant, goblet-shaped flowers; sometimes sold as "*soulangeana* Heaven Scent" by nurseries, but this is a commercial mislabelling, not true *soulangeana* |

**West of Ireland:** Magnolias are one of the great spring spectacles of an Irish garden, from the earliest star- and lily-flowered types through to the large, showy saucer and yulan magnolias. All are reliably hardy in sheltered west of Ireland gardens, but as a group their early flowers are vulnerable to being blackened by a late frost — avoid frost pockets, exposed coastal wind, and east-facing positions where early-morning sun after a frosty night can damage flowers. Choose species and cultivars to suit the space available: Star and Lily Magnolias for a small garden, Saucer, Loebner and Yulan magnolias where there's room for a genuinely large specimen.

---

### T30. Mophead / Lacecap Hydrangea *(Hydrangea macrophylla)*


***Russian:*** *Гортензия крупнолистная*

📷 **Reference photo:** [Hydrangea macrophylla — view on Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrangea_macrophylla)

**Light:** 🌥️ Partial shade preferred. Morning sun with afternoon shade is ideal. Bigleaf hydrangea performs best in areas with dappled sunlight, partial shade, or deep shade. Protect it from afternoon sun. The macrophylla cultivars famously tolerate coastal exposure.

**Height/Spread:** 1–2m tall, 1.5–2.5m spread.

**Soil needs:** They love a fertile, humus-rich, clay soil. pH 5.0–6.0 for blue flowers; 6.0–7.0 for pink flowers. Moist but well-drained. Good salt tolerance.
